PREFACE 

The following Lessons on Real Estate Law have two purposes: 

The real estate broker and everyone who has dealings in real estate are certain to be faced with some of the legal questions which are discussed in the following pages. One purpose of the lessons is to give to such persons some idea of the way in which these questions are dealt with by the courts. This will frequently enable these people to protect themselves from making serious errors and will often indicate to them the instances when they should consult a competent legal practitioner. Furthermore, if legal proceeding should become necessary, this knowledge will make intelligent clients of those students who have absorbed the contents. 

The other purpose of the lessons is to make better and more appreciative citizens. Events of the past few years have tended to emphasize to most thoughtful persons the very valuable heritage which has been handed down to citizens in all English speaking countries. The legal rights which we enjoy today have been made possible by the intelligent labor, struggles, and sufferings of countless persons over a period of several centuries both in this country and in England. Among the most important of these rights may be mentioned the right to jury trial, the right to free speech, the benefit of free press, the right to be free from oppressive arrest and oppressive prosecution for crime and the preservation and protection of legitimate interests in property. If we are to continue making progress toward a still more enlightened and efficient democracy a widely diffused knowledge of our law and our legal institutions will be of great value. 862273 LO

REAL ESTATE LAW REAL ESTATE GLOSSARY 

in an amortized mortgage that the entire balance becomes due or may be declared by the mortgage holder to be due if one payment is in default. action—A proceeding in a court to enforce a right which the party thinks he has. administrator-One appointed by a probate or surrogate court to settle the estate of an intestate deceased, or of a testate deceased in absence of a competent named executor. adverse possession-Possession under claim of title, which possession is hostile, exclusive, notorious and continuous; one having possession with the consent of the owner is not in adverse possession. agent-One who acts for another. 

Usually there is a contract under which the agent is entitled to compensation, but no such contract is necessary. aliquot-A simple fractional part such as one-third, one-seventh or one-tenth. appellant-One who appeals to a higher court. appellee-One against whom an appeal is taken. assessment-An evaluation of lands for tax purposes. assignee in bankruptcy-One appointed to take title to a bankrupt's goods and real property, and to settle up the bankrupt's estate under supervision by the court. assise-Trial, usually trial by jury. attestation-The act of witnessing a written document. It is usually required that wills be attested, but not contracts or ordinary conveyances of land. 

averments-Allegations or statements in a complaint or other pleading.

bankrupt-One who has been declared such by a bankruptcy court, either at his own petition or at that of a creditor or creditors. battery-Unlawful contact with another's person, not consented to. beneficial interest—The right to the benefit of property to which one does not have the legal title; for example, the interest of the beneficiary of a trust. bona fide-In or with good faith; without fraud or deceit. breach of trust-A trustee's failure to act in the best interests of the beneficiaries of the trust; for example, if he fails to collect money due to the trust estate and thereby the claim is never realized upon, he is guilty of breach of trust. cestui que trust-One for whose benefit the legal title to property is held in trust by a trustee. cestui que use-One for whose benefit property is passively held. chancery-Equity. chattel-Movable property. chose in action-A right of action, founded on contract or in tort, to collect for a breach of contract or for an injury to person or property.

civilly dead-In some states by special statute one sentenced to life imprisonment is civilly or legally dead. codes of civil procedure-In some states procedure is regulated by a series of statutes called codes of civil procedure. codicil—An amendment or an addition to a will; it must be attested in the same way as was the original will. consensus ad idem—Agreement. consideration-That which is given or suffered in exchange for a promise. contract-An agreement between two or more persons, based upon consideration, to do or not to do a certain thing. conveyance-Transfer of legal title or other property right in land or chattels. course-A direction of a line in a legal description of land in a deed of conveyance. covenant-A promise contained in a contract or a conveyance. In early law the term was applied only to promises under seal, which did not require consideration. curtesy-The right of a widower in his deceased wife's real estate. damages-Compensation which may be recovered by a person who has suffered loss or injury at the hands of another, either through a breach of contract or through a tort such as a trespass to property. decedent-A deceased person. declaration of trust-The instrument, or the informal writing, whereby the trustee declares that he holds property in trust for the benefit of another. deed in escrow-Where A executes a deed of conveyance of property to X but delivers it to B to be delivered to X upon the happening of a certain event, such as the payment of the purchase price of the property, B is said to hold the deed in escrow. default-Failure to fulfill a promise or other obligation. defeasance clause in a mortgage—A clause providing that if the mortgage debt be paid by a certain date the conveyance is to be void. defendant-One against whom an action is brought. demise-Convey. demurrer-An objection that a complaint or other pleading does not state a good cause of action or a good defense. demurrer to evidence-An objection that the evidence has not proved a cause of action. devise-A gift of real property by will. disclaimer-Refusal of a devisee or legatee to accept a devise or legacy. dower-The right of the widow in her deceased husband's real estate; this right becomes inchoate at the time of marriage and must therefore be released by her if the husband conveys the property; it becomes vested at the time of his death.

easement-The right of one person to use in some manner a portion of the land of another; for example, the right to cross over the land of another is called an easement or right of way. eminent domain-Right of the state or semi-public corporations to condemn land for use toward the general good; for example, for a highway or for a public school. equitable servitude-Restriction on the use of land imposed by the grantor of the land. equity—A supplementary system of law which grew up in order to overcome the failure of the common law to meet new conditions. 

It has been called a system of jurisprudence wherein what is fair and just between claimants may be discovered and decreed without the limiting handicaps of the com- 10

mon law. error-A plaintiff in error is one who takes a case to a higher court by contending that the court below committed an error in its proceedings; a defendant in error is the one against whom such a proceeding (writ of error) is taken. escheat-Where land was conveyed to A and his heirs and A died without a will and without heirs the land was said to escheat or go to the state. estate-In real estate law, the degree, nature, quantity and extent of one's interest in real property; for example, an estate in fee simple, for life, or for years. estoppel-Operates to bar an action or a defense because of a misrepresentation of fact acted upon by the other party. exception-Objection. executor-One named in a will to carry out the provisions of the will. fee-synonomous with fee simple. fee simple title-Absolute title to land which a man holds for himself and his heirs forever, unconditionally. feoffment-A deed which accompanied delivery of possession where conveyance was made by livery of seisin. feofee to uses-One to whom land was conveyed for the use of or for the benefit of either a third party or the transferor. fiduciary-One in whom trust and confidence is reposed either with regard to the care of property or the making of contracts with third parties. Some fiduciaries, such as trustees, executors and administrators are entrusted with legal title; other, such as bailees, guardians and receivers, have only possession; still others, such as ordinary agents have neither title nor possession. foreclosure-Remedy of mortgagee where the mortgagor is in default. fraud-The law has never undertaken to define fraud lest some new kind of fraud might be developed outside the definition, thus embarrassing the courts. There are several kinds of fraud, all involving dishonesty of some sort. A false representation of fact made with the intention of its being acted upon becomes actionable if the other party does rely upon it to his damage. Conveyances in fraud of creditors are transfers made by an insolvent person in order to prevent his creditors from reaching the property transferred. freehold-A fee or life estate in land. gratuitious-Free; without compensation. guardian-Custodian of the property or of the person of a minor, or of an insane or an otherwise mentally incompetent person.

habendum-A clause in a deed of conveyance which states the interest which the transferee is to have in the land. holographic will-One completely in the testator's handwriting and signed by him; such a will does not need to be attested by witnesses. impanel-The action of the clerk of the court in making up the list of persons called to court to form a jury, as well as in listing the jurors who have been selected for a trial. incorporeal-An incorporeal right is one which is intangible, such as a right of action to collect money, an easement of way over property, or the right of a landlord to property which he has leased to a tenant, usually called a reversionary right. indebitatus assumpsit-An action to recover the selling price of goods sold on credit. 11

insurable interest-An interest in property which gives the holder the right to become an insured; any one who has such an interest in property that he would lose if the property should be damaged by fire, has an insurable interest in the property. intestate-One who dies leaving no will. inure-To take effect.

judgment-The final decision of a competent court.

landlord-Landowner who has leased or rented his land to another, called a tenant. lien-A legal claim or charge upon real or personal property for the payment of a debt. livery of seisin-Delivery of possession usually accompanied by a deed of feoffment; the term is applied to a very early method of conveying land, now very nearly obsolete.

master of the rolls-In English law, the judge of a lower equity court. memorandum-An informal or brief note describing some transaction, or outlining some intended contract or deed. monument-An object or natural feature of the land used in a survey or conveyance to describe the land. mortgagee-One who receives a mortgage. mortgagor-One who gives a mortgage.

non-suit-A dismissal of an action caused by plaintiff's failure to prove his case at the trial. note-A promissory note is a promise to pay money. nuncupative will—A will of personal property made orally in the presence of witnesses and declared to be the testator's will; it must be made in the last illness and reduced to writing soon after the death. ordinance-A local law passed by a municipality; that is, by a city or town.

parol—Originally it meant not under seal; when the statute of frauds is involved it means oral; when the parol evidence rule is involved it means any evidence outside of or extrinsic to the written instrument. pecuniary-Pertaining to money. per curiam-By the court. plaintiff-A person who brings an action. precedent-A case or decision of a court which furnishes an authority for future decisions in similar cases. premises of a deed-The earlier part. principal-One for whom an agent acts. 

probate-The action or process of proving a will. profits a prendre-The right to take soil or product of the soil from land of another; for example, the right to cut and take trees for firewood. pro tanto-Proportionally. quit claim deed-A deed which, without warranty, merely undertakes to convey the grantor's interest in the property thus conveyed. reformation-The correction of a contract or conveyance which by mistake does not express the real intent of the parties thereto. remainder-The remaining part of an estate after a conveyance has been made of a life estate or term for years to another. If a conveyance is made so that title goes to B for life and then to C, C is said to have an estate in remainder. 12

remainderman-One who is entitled to the remainder of an estate. replevin—An action brought to recover possession of goods unlawfully taken or detained. rescission-Cancellation of a contract or conveyance. reversion—The estate left to a transferor after he has conveyed an estate for life or for a term of years to another.

seal-A sign made on a document to attest its execution. seisin (seizin)-Possession of land with intent to claim freehold interest; possession under legal title or some legal right. special assumpsit-An action by a seller to collect damages or payment of contemplated benefit, for non-performance on the part of a buyer under contract. spendthrift trust-A trust under which the beneficiary is protected from his own improvident acts and from creditors. spouse-Party to a marriage. statute-An act of the legislature as distinguished from the common law which has grown up by repeated judicial decision. statute of limitations—A statute declaring that no suit shall be maintained on a cause of action unless brought within a specified period after the right accrued. statute of uses-A statute which placed legal title in the person who had the use or beneficial interest in the property.

tenant-One who holds or possesses land or tenements by any kind of right or title; in common parlance, one in temporary possession of land of another, called the landlord. tender-Offer. testate-One who has made a will; one who dies leaving a will. testator-One who has made a will; one who dies leaving a will. tort-A wrong such as trespass to land, assault, battery, nuisance, libel or slander, which gives rise to a cause of action. trespass-An entry on land in the possession of another without the latter's consent. transferee-One to whom a transfer of property is made. transferor-One who makes a transfer of property. trover-An action to recover the value of goods wrongfully retained by another for his own use. trust-Property held by one person for the benefit of another. trustee-One who holds the legal title to property for the benefit of another. vendor-Seller. verdict-Decision of a jury.

warranty deed-Deed wherein the grantor obligates himself and his heirs to guarantee and defend the grantee and his heirs against loss through claims of others to the land conveyed. writ-An order in writing from a court of justice directing or authorizing a person to perform a certain act. 13

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 1 WHO MAKES THE LAW? 

A. Who Makes the Law? 

1. Introduction 

Who makes the law under which we live? It is made by Congress, by state legislatures, by city councils, and, chiefly, by judicial precedent. The United States Constitution was made by representatives of the people, in a convention called for that purpose, in 1789. 

State constitutions were made in state constitutional conventions. 

Congress and the various state legislatures pass statutes; city councils, by authority of the legislatures, pass ordinances. But the great bulk of our law has been inherited from the judge-made law of England. Some of it is more than seven hundred years old. 

But how do judges make law? Is it not their province merely to declare what the law is? Suppose that A says to his son B, "I will give you that eighty-acre piece of land which is back from the road." 

B, in reliance upon this oral promise goes into possession of the land and makes improvements on it, but A does not give him a deed. A is killed in an automobile accident and the other children of A claim that the eighty-acre farm is a part of A's estate. B insists upon having it for himself. They threaten to eject him and he sues them asking for a deed to the land. The lower court refuses to award it to him, and he appeals to the court of last resort in the state; if there has been no decision covering the point in that state, the appeal court considers the decision in other states, in order to decide the case. If they decide that B is, or is not, entitled to the land, that decision becomes a precedent in that state. It is these precedents, decided in our forty-eight states, in our federal courts, in England, Canada, Australia, South Africa, and other English speaking countries, which constitute the major part of our Anglo-American law. When we say that courts do not make law 19

but only declare what the law is, we are emphasizing the fact that, in making their decisions, courts are expected, as a matter of professional duty, to follow previous decisions. Sometimes, a court does not follow the prior decision on a given point in its own state, because it is convinced that the earlier decision was wrong; then we say that the previous decision on that point is overruled. 

B. Meaning of Common Law; of Equity 

The phrase "common law" as now used may mean any one of three things, depending upon the context. It may mean, first, the common law system as distinguished from the civil law system; second, common law as distinguished from statutory law; or, third, common law as distinguished from equity. It may mean the common-law system of English speaking, Anglo-American jurisdictions as distinguished from the civil law system which prevails in France, Italy, Spain, other countries of the Western European continent, South America, Central America, Mexico and Japan. Between the common law system and the civil law system, there are many important differences. The mass of our own law is found in judicial decisions-judge-made law; the mass of the law in civil law countries is found in statutes, with very little attention paid to precedents. The history of the common law covers only about seven or eight centuries; that of the civil law covers about twentyfive centuries, having been derived from the non-criminal part of the Roman law. One of the most outstanding characteristics of our law is trial by jury. In civil law countries trials are before a judge or judges, except that France has imported from England trial by jury in serious criminal cases, according the privilege of jury trial to a defendant who has already been found guilty by the trial judges.

It may mean common law as disinguished from statutory law. 

For example, at the early common law, a landlord who was entitled to the possession of land at the end of a tenant's tenancy was entitled to use force to put the tenant out; but by statute such forcible entries have been made illegal. 

It may mean common law as distinguished from equity. Some four or five centuries ago, when conditions in England had so changed that the common law decisions no longer worked justly in all cases, the English king allowed persons who could not get justice in the common-law courts to appeal to his secretary or chancellor for justice. In the course of time, this grew up to be a separate court called the Court of Chancery or Equity. It was called equity because the chancellor's decisions were more just and fair and 20

equitable than were the outgrown common law rules. In America today, however, common law and equity are in most states administered by the same court; but the auxiliary system of equity is still considered as distinct from the common law. 

It is still necessary, of course, to keep changing our law in order to meet new social and economic conditions, but this is accomplished today chiefly by statutes passed by the legislature, not by equity, which is now almost as well settled as is the original common law.

To illustrate: If A makes a written contract to convey his farm. to B, and later wrongfully refuses to convey, we say that B may either sue A at common law for the breach of contract and get a judgment for money damages by way of compensation for the loss suffered, or he may sue in equity and get a decree that A convey the farm to B upon B's paying the purchase money. Here is another example: If A should keep his pigs on his own land close to

B's kitchen, B could, at common law, get a judgment for money damages for A's maintaining such a nuisance; whereas in equity, he can get an injunction forbidding A to continue keeping his pigs there.

What is the significance of the word "common" in the phrase, common law? Very early, in England, the rules and customs which governed the various parts of England were not uniform. When they did become fairly uniform, they were then referred to as the common law-that is, the law common to all parts of England. 

2. Meaning of Real Property or Real Estate 

If A has such a right in land that upon his death it will descend to his heirs unless he leaves it by will to someone else, we say that he has a "fee simple estate;" but, if his estate ends at his death, then we say that he has a "life estate." In either case, we say that he has a "freehold interest" in the land. If his right is only for a fixed number of years, we say he has an estate less than freehold, even though the number of years is quite large. Six or seven hundred years ago, if A had a freehold interest in a piece of land, that is, either an estate in fee or for life, and B ousted him and took possession, A had what was called a real action to get back his possession; it was called real from the Latin word "res" meaning thing; that is, he could get back the thing itself. But if he had only an estate for years in the land and B ousted him, A would merely bring against B an action for damages to compensate him for the loss of his right in the land. Since all he could get was a personal 21

judgment against A for money damages, the action was called a personal action. Later, the name of the action was affixed to the interest itself, so that freehold interests in land came to be called real property, while estates for years came to be called personal property. The distinction is still important in most states; because, if A dies having left by will his real property to X, and his estate for years in other land to Y, and there is not enough of other personal property (that is, money and chattels and claims for money against third persons) left to pay all of A's debts, A's executor must first sell the estate for years to pay A's debts, before resorting to the real property devised to X. In common parlance, however, all interests in land are frequently called real estate or real property, and a realtor is likely to handle rentals as well as to sell freehold interests on commission. So "land" and "real property" are frequently used as synonyms. Land is the soil of the earth and, in theory, extends indefinitely downward, includes all that is within it (as ores, oil, etc.) and everything firmly affixed to it, unless the contrary is expressed by contract: it includes all buildings, retaining walls, driveways, etc., all fencing, standing trees, seed in the ground, growing crops, etc. Crops, trees, minerals and other products of the land become chattels-that is, movable property-when severed from the land. 

3. Forms of Action at Common Law, in Equity, and Under the Codes 

Before the Norman conquest of England in 1066, justice was administered by the people themselves in people's courts, and the law which was enforced in those courts was not written but was merely handed down by oral tradition. After the conquest, the 

King-William I-gradually established a system of royal courts which eventually all but crowded out the old Anglo-Saxon courts, because they were administered by trained, professional judges assisted by trained attorneys at law and justice was more prompt and effective than in the people's courts. These king's courts were open to suitors only upon payment of a fee for an original writ issued by the king's secretary, the writ authorizing the court to hear and decide the case. These writs were of varying forms according to the facts of the particular case. Thus a writ authorizing the court to hear a case in which the plaintiff complained that the defendant had committed a battery upon him, would be quite different from a writ in which the plaintiff complained that the defendant had borrowed his horse and refused to deliver it back to him. 22

A. At Common Law 

Thus originated the common-law forms of actions. One of the earliest forms that have come down to us is trespass, which lies for a direct violation of one's personal security by a battery, by an attempt to commit a battery (called an assault) or by imprisonment either by a physical enclosure or by arrest. Trespass also lies for a direct violation of the possession of land or of chattels. 

For indirect violations of personal security or the possession of property, or for violations of intangible rights in property, such as the right of a landlord in leased land, or the right to be free from a nuisance committed by one's neighbor, the plaintiff is compelled to bring an action which was first called, "trespass upon the special case," but later shortened to "trespass on the case," "action on the case" and, finally, to "case." For example, if B wrongfully walks across A's land or commits a battery on him or takes his truck away from him, A's remedy is an action of trespass. But if B keeps his pigs close to A's kitchen window or negligently runs over A in the highway or causes serious damage to land while it is in the possession of A's tenant, X, A's remedy is in case. 

If A sells goods to B on 90 days credit and at the end of that time B fails to pay for them, A's remedy is said to be in "indebitatus assumpsit," for the price; if A did not sell the goods to B but merely contracted to sell them, and B later refused to accept them, then A's remedy is said to be "special assumpsit," for the loss occasioned to him by B's refusal to perform the contract. 

If B wrongfully takes A's chattel from him, or borrows it and refuses to deliver it up to A upon request, A may bring the action of "trover," for the value of the chattel, or "replevin," to get back the chattel itself.

If B is in possession of A's land and wrongfully refuses to give it up, A may maintain an action of "ejectment;" or, if B does not claim any right to remain in possession, A may usually get possession within a few days by a statutory proceeding, the sheriff or constable putting B out of possession.

B. Under the Codes 

In many states today there are codes of civil procedure, that is, systems of rules, which purport to abolish common law forms of action, so that under these codes the plaintiff need not give his action any particular name; but the distinctions created by the forms of action still substantially exist in the law. 

C. In Equity

In equity there are no forms of action. The plaintiff merely files 23

his bill, asking for "equitable relief," such as the specific performance of a contract; or an injunction against a "tort," such as waste committed by the tenant upon his landlord's property or a nuisance committed by the defendant, such as conducting a slaughter house close to the plaintiff's residence; or for the correction of a mistake in a deed of conveyance or in a contract. 

4. How Legal Rights Are Enforced 

Suppose that on January 1, A contracts in writing to sell to B his house and lot on Cherry Street for $10,000, settlement to be made on September 1. On September 1, B refuses to accept the conveyance and A insists that the refusal is wrongful; in the mean time, property values have declined. How may A enforce his right?

A. At Common Law 

He may bring an action at common law-special assumpsit-for damages to cover the difference between the actual value on September 1, and the contract price of $10,000. To do this a declaration or complaint must be filed in the trial court, and B must be served with a summons to appear and answer the declaration or complaint. He may, perhaps, protest that A does not have good title or that A made false representations to him to induce him to make the contract. Either A or B may insist upon a trial by jury. After the jury is selected and impaneled, evidence is introduced by A, then by B in defense, and then by A in rebuttal. The court instructs the jury as to the law applying to the pleadings and the evidence, and the jury retires to the jury room and agrees upon its verdict; and upon this verdict the court renders judgment which is final, unless the losing party takes the case to a higher court by a writ of error, pointing out what he regards as mistakes made by the trial court; or by an appeal, which takes the entire case before the higher court. A judgment for money damages is satisfied by the sheriff selling enough of the judgment debtor's property to pay the judgment. If neither side desires a jury, the case is tried by the court without a jury. If A wins, either the jury or the court assesses the damages to be paid by B to A for breach of the contract. 

B. In Equity

But perhaps A is not satisfied with a judgment for money damages; it is likely to be difficult for him to find another buyer, and he is anxious to get the purchase price; what may he do about it?

He may file a bill in equity, asking that the court by decree compel B to accept the conveyance and pay the purchase price. In such a 24

case, neither A nor B may insist upon a jury trial, so the trial is before the court without a jury. If the court finds that there was no mistake or fraud in the transaction and that B's refusal to go through with the transaction was wrongful, a decree for specific performance will be issued; if B refuses to comply with that decree, without any excuse therefor, he may be fined and sent to jail for contempt of court, until he is ready to comply with it. 25

4. What three different things may be meant by the term, common law? What is the significance of the word "common?" 

5. In what two important respects does our common law system differ from the civil law system?

7. What are the two different meanings of "real estate," or "real property"?

9. If there is default by either party in a contract to sell land, what is the common law remedy of the other party? 

10. What is his remedy in equity? 26

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 2 TRESPASSING 1. Trespassing on the Surface-Trespassing Animals 

If B is on land which is in the possession of A, and A wishes to get him off, what may A do about it?

If B came there with A's permission, then A must first request him to leave; if B refuses, A may push or pull him off and, if he is not strong enough to do it himself, he may get others to help him. 

He may not strike B, unless B actively resists. If B does actively resist, striking A, A may stand his ground and oppose force to force, unless B's attack so threatens his life that he can defend himself only by killing B; in such a case A must retreat if he can safely do so; if he cannot retreat with safety he is justified in killing 

B. If he is in his dwelling house he need not retreat. An Englishman's house is his castle and so is an American's. 

If B walks across A's land insisting that he has a right to do so, merely putting him off is obviously ineffectual. A should bring an action at law-trespass-against him and, if he gets a judgment against B, that will usually settle the matter; if B persists, then it will be necessary to get a decree of an equity court that he desist; if he still persists, he may be punished for contempt of court by a fine or imprisonment in the county jail. In order to get a judgment against B, it is not necessary to show that B caused any damage by walking across A's land, and it is no defense to B that he thought that he was entitled to walk across the land. A mistake of title is no excuse to one who commits a trespass. 

If B's animals escape from his land and come onto A's land and damage his crops, A may, of course, drive them off; and he may use a nonferocious dog to help him. He may also get a judgment against B, to compensate him for the damage done to his land and crops; and it is no defense that the animals escaped because the 27

fences were flattened by a storm, or that a third party opened a gate and allowed them to escape. On the other hand, if B drives his truck onto A's land because the public way was impassable or because he accidentally lost control of the truck due to an unexpected heart attack, B is not liable.

It is also a trespass on the surface, if B erects or maintains a fence across A's land or if he throws a stone upon it or spreads manure on it, even though such last mentioned act may benefit the land.

If B peaceably acquires possession of A's land, but such acquisition was wrongful, A's remedy is to bring not an action of trespass, but an action of ejectment to get back into possession. But if 

B should, while in such wrongful possession, cut down a large amount of timber or do other seriously destructive acts, A may get an equity decree against the commission of such acts in the future while he is bringing his action of ejectment. Such a remedy is especially necessary where B is insolvent. 

Where B obtained permission from A to deposit a small amount of rock upon his vacant lot in New York City, and deposited huge quantities of rock thereon, A was given an equity decree that B himself remove the rock, the expense of removal being very great. 

Where B erected a barrier on A's land although with A's permission, but erected it so as to interfere seriously with A's business, A was given an equity decree that B remove the barrier. 

2. Trespassing Under the Surface 

If B is mining coal beneath the surface of his land and, by mistake, mines coal across the boundary line under A's land, A may hold B liable for the damage done to his land by removing the coal, even though B's mistake was made reasonably, in good faith; or, A may bring replevin for the coal thus mined by B, or may compel B to pay him for its value. 

A trespass beneath the surface may also result, if B piles large quantities of earth on his own land close to A's land, thus causing subterranean squeezing of land, pushing some of the under earth into A's land. 

3. Trespassing Above the Surface 

Courts formerly said that one's possession of land extended from the center of the earth up to the zenith, so that if B should cross A's land in a balloon, at any height, it would be a trespass. Many states now have statutes practically making an air-highway five 28

hundred feet above the surface of the earth, so that airplanes have a right to fly at that altitude or higher. If an airplane, from any height, drops something onto A's land, causing damage, A is entitled to a judgment for the amount of the damage. And if 

B's plane, flying lower than 500 feet, should frighten A's cattle, B would be liable for the ensuing damage.

It is also a trespass above the surface if B puts his hand over the boundary line, or if the eaves of his building extend across it, or if he strings telegraph or telephone or electric light wires across it. 

4. Encroachments on A's Land 

If B wrongfully leaves his car or other chattel on A's land, A may either place it on B's land or in the public highway in a safe place, or turn it over to the keeper of the public pound. 

If B erects a building, partly on A's land, A may disencumber his land by pushing it over onto B's land. If the building is too heavy he may usually get a decree that A remove the building across the boundary line. If the building is so large and heavy that this cannot be done, it means, as a practical matter, that B must buy a small strip of A's land. A must, in any case, get a settlement or a judgment at law for damages, or a decree in equity that B remove the building, within the period of the statute of limitations-usually 20 or 15 years-or B will acquire the right to keep the building where it is. If the encroachment is merely an overhanging cornice it may perhaps be removed either by B or by A, and thus settle the matter. If not removed before the end of the statutory period-20 or 15 years-B will acquire the right to keep it there indefinitely.

If B has a tree growing on his land but the branches overhang A's land, what may A do about it? Suppose there is fruit growing on the overhanging branches, is A entitled to the fruit? No; since the trunk of the tree is on B's land, the fruit belongs to him, and he may enter A's land and get the fruit, compensating A for any damage done; if A objects to B's gathering the fruit, he may gather it himself and place it on B's land. And he may cut off the overhanging branches up to the boundary line and put them on B's land. He may also cut off the roots up to the boundary line. If, because of the shade, A is unable to grow anything on his land he may get a judgment against B for the amount of the damage thus suffered. These rights of A last as long as the tree lasts; the overhanging branches are regarded, not as a trespass as in the case of a cornice, but as a permissive nuisance; and the statute of limita- 29

tions does not apply, as it does in the case of the overhanging cornice. 

5. Nuisance 

One who is in the occupation of land is entitled to have the air free from unreasonable pollution by disagreeable vapors, such as ammonia fumes, or disagreeable odors, such as odor from cattle pens, and also free from unreasonable noise. He is also entitled to have water in a natural watercourse flow through or past his land without diminution, deterioration or alteration by acts on the part of others.

Where is his remedy in these cases? Occasionally he may abate the nuisance himself; for example, if a dog howls all night and keeps him and his family awake he may kill the dog. He may bring an action on the case and get judgment for the amount of damage caused by the nuisance, but his usual remedy is to get a decree of an equity court enjoining the continuance of the nuisance. 

If the persons occuping land above him on a natural water-course flowing through or past his land, dump sewage into the stream and render the water unfit for use, it is no answer that the amount of sewage dumped in by any one of them would not have caused a nuisance; but if they act independently of each other, each one is liable only for his share of the damage. If they act in concert, each one knowing that the others are also dumping sewage, then each one is liable for the entire amount of damage. 

If an adjoining proprietor decides to build up to the boundary line between himself and his neighbor and makes an excavation therefor, he must use due care to shore up his neighbor's land to keep it from falling into the excavation. But if there is a building on the adjacent land the burden of shoring up to keep the building from falling into the excavation, is on the owner of the building. 

If A and B occupy adjoining pieces of land and they quarrel, and if B, in order to spite A, erects a tight board fence twelve feet high near the boundary line and paints it yellow or some other objectionable color, what may A do about it? In most states A is entitled to a court decree that the fence be removed. But this applies only if the thing erected is of no use; if B had erected a chicken house for his chickens, instead of the fence, A would not be entitled to an injunction. 

6. Disparagement of Property 

If B, without excuse, misrepresents to third persons the quality of A's land, for example, that the soil is too poor to raise good crops, 30

or that A does not have good title to the land, A may get judgment for damages for the loss suffered by him, in an action on the case; and, by the better view, A may get an equity decree that B cease making such misrepresentations. If he gets the equity decree he gets also compensation for harm already suffered by him, down to the date of the decree. 31

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 1) 

ADAMS BROS. v. CLARK 

Kentucky Supreme Court, 1920. 189 Ky. 279, 224 S.W. 1041.

OPINION OF THE COURT BY JUDGE SAMPSON-Reversing. *** By this appeal we are asked to decide whether the owner of trespassing chickens which have crossed over a lawful fence, as defined by our statutes, and eaten and destroyed grain and garden vegetables of great value of another, is liable for the loss and damage thus occasioned by his fowls. It is a case of first impression in this jurisdiction. Indeed there appear to be few cases upon the subject found in the reports of either England or America, and the text writers have carefully avoided the question, although we do find many texts by learned writers as well as decisions by this and other courts dealing with the liability of the owner of domestic animals other than fowls, for trespass.

That we may have a clear understanding of the case before us for decision, we will say that appellants, Adams Brothers, have a large feed barn and garden surrounded by a lawful fence four and one-half feet high in the city of Smithland. Before the commencement of this action they had kept a large amount of live stock in and on their premises aforesaid and had kept large quantities of grain for stock feed in their barn, and in a part of their lot they had planted a garden of vegetables. Just across the street Mrs. 

Byrnes Clark has a poultry yard wherein it is alleged she kept from 250 to 400 chickens. These chickens crossed the fence into the lots of Adams 

Brothers and there ate and destroyed the garden vegetables belonging to said Adams Brothers, to their damage, as it is alleged, of $600.00, and this suit was instituted by them against Mrs. Clark to recover that sum for the loss and damage caused by the chickens. She filed a general demurrer¹ to the petition, which was sustained, with leave to Adams Brothers to amend, which was done, and she again filed a general demurrer to the petition as amended which, after due hearing, the court sustained, and appellants declining to further plead their petition was dismissed and they appeal, thus raising in this court the question stated above, the facts being admitted. 

By the common law of England the owner of domestic animals, including fowls, was required to keep them on his own premises, and was liable for their trespass upon the lands of another. The courts held that an entry of a person's cattle on another's land constituted actionable trespass, regardless of whether the lands were enclosed or not. The same rule was applied to fowls as to cattle, and the owner was made to respond in damages for their depredation. However, in many of the states in this union the common law of England was found inapplicable to the prevailing conditions and our courts and legislative bodies have modified the rule in some of the states so that the owner of live stock is not compelled to keep them enclosed but may allow them to range at will on uninclosed land of others without 1. The general demurrer raises the question as to whether the plaintiff's petition stated a cause of action against the defendant-that is, whether the defendant was liable for the damage done by her hens. 32

incurring liability therefor. This modification, so far as it affects Kentucky, applies only to cattle and not to fowls. 

Our legislature many years ago passed an act defining what constituted a lawful fence and allowing recovery for the destruction of property by domestic animals only in cases where the one complaining had his property enclosed by a fence of a given height, strength and closeness. Manifestly, this statute, which reads: "Every strong and sound fence of rails, or plank, or wire and plank, or iron or of hedge, four and one-half feet high, and being so close that cattle can not creep through, or made of stone or brick four and one-half feet high, or a ditch three feet deep and three feet broad, with a hedge two feet high, or a rail, plank, stone, smoothed or barbed wire, or a brick fence two and one-half feet high on the margin thereof, the hedge or fence being so close that cattle can not creep through, shall be deemed a lawful fence." was not intended to apply to chickens or other fowls but only to such stock as are included in the generic term cattle. 

The next section, No. 1781, which reads: "If any cattle shall enter into any grounds over or through a lawful fence the owner or manager of the cattle shall, for the first breach, be liable to the owner or occupant of such ground for such damage to his trees, grass, grain, crops, cattle or land as he may have sustained thereby; and for every subsequent breach by the cattle of the same owner, double damages. And after giving the owner or manager of such cattle at least five days' notice, in writing, of the fact of two previous breaches into the same inclosure by the cattle of the same owner, the owner or occupant of such inclosure shall have a lien on the cattle to indemnify him on account of any damages sustained by the third or any subsequent trespass of such cattle and may enforce his lien by action as in case of a mortgage lien," makes it certain that only such domestice animals as are included in the term "cattle," as defined by law, are embraced within the act. The statutes, section 1781, follow the common law and award the injured party practically the same remedy anciently provided by the common law. Thus it will be seen that while our legislature has modified the common law rule with respect to the running at large of cattle, and the courts have concurred in this departure, there has been no change whatever in the common law with respect to fowls, such as chickens.

We have no national common law in the United States, but we do have a common law in the several states, each for itself, but this common law is always subject to change by statute. 5 R.C.L. 809. 

In the absence of a national common law, in this country, each state is left to determine the common law for itself according to its peculiar needs and policies. By our Constitution it is provided: "All laws which on the first day of June, one thousand seven hundred and ninety-two, were in force in the state of Virginia, and 2. This rule was adopted in the prairie states where a large part of the land was used for pasturing cattle and there was little material out of which to construct fencing. As the prairie states were settled they gradually went back to the old rule that one must at his peril keep his cattle from straying from his field to his neighbor's land. 33

which are of a general nature and not local to that state, and not repugnant to this Constitution, nor to the laws which have been enacted by the general assembly of this Commonwealth, shall be in force within the state until they shall be altered by the general assembly."

The common law of England, subject to such legislative enactment as the assembly of Virginia had passed, was in force in Virginia at the time of the adoption of our present Constitution and, therefore, became a part of the law of our state by the section above quoted, and indeed has been the law of our state from the adoption of our first Constitution. The Virginia convention of 1776 declared "that the common law of England, all statutes or acts of parliament made in aid of the common law prior to the fourth year of the reign of King James I and which are of a general nature, and not local to that kingdom *** shall be the rule of decision, and shall be considered in full force until the same shall be altered by the legislative power of this colony." ...

We are unable to find any enactment of our lawmaking body or judicial warrant for changing or modifying the common law rule in Kentucky with respect to domestic fowls. It is argued by counsel for appellee that by long usage and custom the people of Kentucky have established a common law which allows the running of fowls at large without the owner incurring liability for their foraging. It is possible for a Commonwealth like Kentucky to so establish law, but it is hardly possible where such rule transcends the emphatic and unmistakable enactment of the lawmaking body or a constitutional provision of the state. It must be granted that the common law of a state may be changed at any time by a legislative act and certainly by constitutional provisions. Since we by our Constitution and by act of our legislature have adopted the common law with respect to the running at large of domestic animals, including fowls, in so far as we have not specifically changed the same by act of our general assembly, it follows that the rule of the common law with respect to fowls is in force in the same way and to the same extent in Kentucky that it was at the time of the adoption of our Constitution, and at the time of the act of our legislature adopting the common law, and is as much a part of our law as any statute which we have adopted. This must be so even though we sometimes find it difficult in wading through texts and court decisions to determine exactly what the common law rule was upon a given subject. 

There can however be no doubt about the common law rule with respect to cattle, for it is laid down by Blackstone and other ancient common law commentators in substance as follows: The entry of a person's cattle on another's land constitutes an actionable trespass regardless of whether the land is inclosed or uninclosed. The right to recover damages is, however, affected by the fact that a duty to fence his lands is sometimes impressed on the latter by agreement, by prescription or by statutes. The rule applicable to cattle governs the right to recover damages against the owner of fowls who permits them to trespass upon another's land, and this is the view taken in reported cases wherein it is held that one who permits fowls under his control to make incursions on another's premises and damages the latter's goods is liable as for a trespass. The remedy was held to be (a) impounding the fowls or (b) by action for damages on the part of the owner of the lands invaded, but he had no right to kill or injure the fowls. . . . 34

The common law in such American states as have declared upon the subject seems to correspond to the English common law. In one case it was held that a flock of turkeys which entered upon the lands of another and destroyed apples and corn incurred a liability against their owner even though the premises were unfenced because the owner through his fowls became a trespasser, the court saying: "He (the landowner) is not bound to fence against any such animal, and may lawfully omit to do so." Bulpet v. 

Matthews, 145 Ill. 345; McPherson v. James, 69 Ill. App. 337. It has also been ⚫held that domestic fowls are animals and subject to all the rules and regulations governing animals under the common law. State v. Brumar, 111 

Ind. 98.

In the early history of this state when settlements were scattered and rural districts but sparsely settled, there was little or no excuse for enforcing the common law rule against the running at large of fowls, and it has seldom if ever come up in our courts, but under present conditions, such as prevail in Smithland and other towns and communities in this Commonwealth, it is highly important that the owner of fowls should have regard for their roving and wasteful natures and exercise care to see that they do not do damage to the crops or property of another. It is argued in brief of counsel for appellee that "the right to freely range their chickens is of the utmost importance to that great number of people endeavoring to meet the high cost of living and make their small salaries and small business enterprises carry them and their families through the year," but the equally important and sacred right of the same class of citizens, equally great in number, to plant and raise a garden or truck patch on their own premises, without annoyance or trouble to neighbors, to help feed themselves and their families and thus tide over the high cost of living period is wholly overlooked and forgotten. The right of free range to the chicken ends where the equal right of the gardener begins. The poultryman has rights in his property just as the gardener has in his, but no greater, and neither is required to yield to the other except that the one must yield where his rights cease and the other's begin. One must always use his property in a way not to do a legal wrong to another. If a poultryman allows his chickens to run at large and to trespass upon the garden of another, the wronged person has no right to injure or kill them but he must confine himself to his civil rights, and this is true even though the gardener should find the fowls on his possessions trespassing at the time. 

The common law with respect to such fowls has never been changed from its adoption at an early period in this state. It therefore follows that it is the duty of the owner of chickens and other such fowls to confine them on his own premises, if it is likely that they will stray on to the premises of another and there do damage. It has never been lawful in this state, though the general practice, for the owner of fowls to allow them to range at will on the land and property of another against his consent; for a trespass, such as is complained of in this case, the law affords at least one remedy and that remedy was invoked by Adams Brothers in the bringing of this action against Mrs. Clark. We, therefore, hold that one who sustains a loss through the trespass of domestic fowls of another, may recoup such loss in damages by a proper action seasonably commenced, though his premises are wholly unfenced, because it would be well nigh impossible to fence against such animals as have the power to mount on wings. This we con- 35

ceive to be the common law of Kentucky as adopted from the English com mon law.*** 

Every owner of fowls must know their nature and their proneness to run at large and wander far from home; to make incursions upon the property of others and there commit depredations. This is their inherent nature. It is the natural propensity of domestic fowls to rove and in doing so to wholly disregard property lines and even fences. This propensity is well known to all owners, and when an owner with this knowledge allows his fowls to run at large to the injury of his neighbors he is a wrongdoer and is liable for their trespasses even in the absence of a statute or ordinance to that effect. 

It is also contended by appellee that the maintenance of a barn by appellants amounted to an attractive nuisance in so far as chickens and fowls are concerned. We can not agree with this contention. The attractive nuisance doctrine has no application to the facts of this case. It is lawful to have and keep upon your place a barn with grain in it and it has never been held that such is an attractive nuisance even to fowls. This doctrine has only been applied to cases where one erects upon his premises and leaves unguarded a contrivance or device which in its nature is calculated to entice children or other thoughtless persons onto the premises to their personal injury, but has never so far as we are able to ascertain been applied to a situation where animals have crossed a lawful fence and committed depredations to the injury of the person on whose premises they entered. 

The trial court committed prejudicial error in sustaining a demurrer to the petition as amended of appellants, Adams Brothers. The cause is therefor reversed for further proceedings in harmony with this opinion. 

Judgment reversed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 50, par. 4; p. 4, par. 5. 

(Par. 1) PFEIFFER v. GROSSMAN 

Illinois Supreme Court, 1853. 15 Ill. 53.

TREAT, C. J. This was an action of trespass quare clausum fregit¹ brought in 1853, by Pfeiffer against Grossman. The plea was not guilty. It appeared in evidence that the plaintiff had title to a certain tract of land; that according to a survey made in 1851 a fence claimed by the defendant was on this tract. 

The fence inclosed about half an acre of the tract, part of which was in timber and the rest in cultivation.

The fence was built by McGuire, who was in possession previous to the defendant. Prior to the survey there was some difficulty between the plaintiff and defendant as to the boundary line, the latter claiming to the fence. The defendant was dissatisfied with the survey, and continued in possession of the ground up to the fence, although notified by the plaintiff to remove the fence. After the suit was brought the defendant caused another survey to be made, which agreed with that made in 1851. It was stated by the plaintiff's counsel that the suit was brought for the purpose of establishing the boundary line between the parties. The court refused to give these instructions: "That the putting a fence or letting it stay on the land of another per- 

1. That is, trespass by which he broke the close or enclosure. It has long been settled, however, that there need be no enclosure marking the boundary line of the plaintiff's land. Crossing the boundary line is enough to make a trespass. 36

son is a trespass in the eye of the law, for which the aggrieved person is entitled to at least nominal damages; that the ploughing up of another's land and cultivating it although the land may thereby be improved, is still a trespass in law, for which the person aggrieved is entitled to at least nominal damages." The jury found the issue for the defendant and the court rendered judgment on the verdict.

The instructions not only asserted correct legal principles, but they were strictly applicable to the case. If a party puts a fence on another's land or ploughs up the soil, he is liable as a trespasser. Such acts are a violation of the owner's right of possession, to redress which the law gives him an action. 

And the action is maintainable, although the owner is not substantially injured. He is entitled to nominal damages for the intrusion upon his possession. The defendant can not defeat the action by showing that the plaintiff is not materially prejudiced, or even that he is actually benefited. A right is invaded and a wrong committed, and that is a sufficient basis for an action. Every unauthorized entry on the land of another is a trespass, for which an action will lie. The law implies damage to the owner, and in the absence of proof as to the extent of the injury, he is entitled to recover nominal damages. Especially in this case where the suit is brought for the purpose of settling a question of right.2

The judgment is reversed and the cause remanded (that is, sent back to the trial court). 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 4, par. 5. (Par. 4)

HOFFMAN v. ARMSTRONG 

New York Commission of Appeals, 1872. 48 N. Y. 201.

The action is for assault and battery. (Reported below, 46 Barb 337.) 

The facts are these: Dr. Hoffman and the defendant were the owners of adjoining lands, separated by a line fence. There was a cherry tree standing upon the land of Dr. Hoffman with limbs overhanging the land of the defendant. The plaintiff, who was a sister of Dr. Hoffman, and lived with him, went upon the line fence and undertook to pick cherries from a limb of the tree which overhung the defendant's land. He forbid her, and on her still persisting, the defendant attempted to prevent her by force and did her a personal injury.

The court held, and so charged the jury, that "every person upon whose lands a tree stands owns the whole of that tree, notwithstanding portions of it may overhang the lands of another; and in this case, as it is conceded that the body or trunk of the tree was wholly upon the land of Dr. Hoffman, he was entitled to all the fruit growing thereon, and hence, if the defendant attempted to prevent the plaintiff from picking such fruit by violence he was a wrongdoer, and this action lies against him, and your verdict should be for the plaintiff."

The defendant excepted to the several legal propositions contained in the charge, and requested the judge substantially to charge that the limbs of the tree overhanging the land of the defendant belonged to him, that he was 2. It is not necessary for the plaintiff to show any damage because trespass is used here for the purpose of vindicating plaintiff's claim to the disputed tract of land. 37

entitled to the fruit thereon, and that he had the right to prevent the plaintiff from picking it by the application of all necessary force, if she refused to desist after being requested to do so. This was refused and exceptions were 

LOTT, Ch. C. The only material question presented in this case is, whether the owner of land overhung by the branches of a fruit tree standing wholly on the land of an adjoining owner is entitled to the fruit growing thereon. 

The defendant claims the ownership of land includes everything above the surface, and bases his claims on the maxim of the law "Cujus est solum ejus est usque ad coelum,” (that is, the ownership of land extends upwards even to the zenith,) and that consequently he was the owner of the overhanging branches and the fruit thereon. The general rule unquestionably, is, that land hath in its legal signification an indefinite extent upward, including everything terrestrial, not only the ground or soil, but everything which is attached to the earth, whether by the course of nature as trees, herbage and water, or by the hands of man as houses and other buildings. . . This rule, while it entitles the owner of the land to the right to it, and to the exclusive use and enjoyment of all the space above it, and to erect any superstructure thereon that he may see fit-and no one can lawfully obstruct it to his prejudice—yet if an adjoining owner should build his house so as to overhang it, such an encroachment would not give the owner of the land the legal title to the part so overhanging. It would be a violation of his right, for which the law would afford an adequate remedy, but would not give him an ownership or right to the possession thereof. (See Aiken v. Benedict, 39 Barb., 400.)

Although different opinions have been held as to the rights of owners of adjoining land in trees planted, the bodies of which are wholly upon that of one, while the roots extend and grow into that of the other and derive nourishment therefrom, it was considered by ALLEN J., in giving the opinion of the court in Dubois v. Weaver, (25 N.Y. Rep. 123, etc.), that the tree is wholly the property of him upon whose land the trunk stands ... 

The ground or reason assigned in those cases for holding that the owner of land on which no part of a tree stands, but into which the roots extend has any interest, is that the tree derives its nourishment from both estates, and not the ground or maxim on which the defendant's claim is based. 

We have not been referred to any case showing that where no part of a tree stood on the land of a party, and it did not receive any nourishment therefrom, that he had any right therein, and it is laid down in Bouvier's Institutes, (section 1573), that if the branches of a tree only overshadow the adjoining land, and the roots do not enter into it, the tree wholly belongs to the estate where the roots grow. 

The rule or maxim giving the right of ownership to everything above the surface to the owner of the soil has full effect without extending it to anything entirely disconnected with or detached from the soil itself. 

It follows, from the views above expressed, that the ruling of the judge at the circuit was right, and the judgment appealed from must be affirmed, 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 31, par. 5. 38

(Par. 5) 

BURKE v. SMITH 

Michigan Supreme Court, 1888 69 Mich. 380 37 N. W. 838.

MORSE, J. The parties to this suit own adjoining lots in the city of Kalamazoo.

The complainant built two dwelling-houses on his lot for the purposes of rental. One house fronts on Park street, the other upon Osborn street. These houses came up within about two feet of the line between him and the defendant.

When these houses were built, Smith had a house on his lot, fronting on Park street, with room for a drive way between his house and complainant's premises. About the time complainant erected his houses, Smith built a house on his lot fronting on Osborn street. Complainant's houses were about 14 feet front, with a single tier of rooms running back from the street. 

These parties got into a quarrel, and, as the result of petty annoyances on both sides, the defendant finally put a screen or fence in front of the lower side windows of the complainant, as it is claimed covering, obscuring, and darkening the same, and shutting out the light and air therefrom. The evidence shows these screens to be two in number, and above eleven feet high, coming up to the top of the lower window of complainant's houses. 

They were built by setting posts in the ground, and nailing boards against them. They were open at the bottom below the windows. 

I think it is established by the evidence that these screens were not put up for a fence, or any other necessary or useful or ornamental purpose, but simply to shut out the view of defendant's premises from complainant's windows.

Smith claims that he did not wish the occupants of complainant's houses to gaze into his windows, or to witness the getting out of and into carriages of his family at the horse block beside the drive-way, and for that reason put up these barriers. There is plenty of evidence that when he was erecting these screens he said he was doing it to shut the light out of Burke's windows. I think there was nothing but malice in his motives. 

The complainant files his bill of complaint alleging the ownership, value, and use of the property belonging to him; the desirability of these houses for rental to families being averred as constituting their chief value. He alleges that these screens were unnecessarily erected from malicious motives, and for the express and avowed purpose of darkening the windows of his two houses, and cutting off the light from entering the windows of said houses, obstructing the view from them, and thereby injuring the value of the houses. He avers that they are an intolerable nuisance; that, by their existence, light and air are prevented from freely entering his houses, the view from the windows is wholly obstructed and cut off, the looks and appearance of the houses greatly injured, their desirability as homes greatly lessened, their rental value depreciated, and their actual market value reduced more than $500, and prays that said screens may be abated as a nuisance and a perpetual injunction allowed against a continuation or renewal of the same.

The court below granted the prayer of the complainant's bill. 39

These screens are erected entirely upon the lot of the defendant and he appeals to this court, claiming that he has a perfect right to erect and maintain them, and that the question of his motives has nothing to do with legal aspects of the case, though he disclaims any malice against complainant. 

It must be taken for granted, in disposing of this case, that these screens were not erected for the purpose of fences, or for any other necessary, useful, or ornamental purpose. The pretense that they were built to keep prying eyes from observing what was going on in the houses or yard of the defendant is not supported by the proofs. The evidence is clear to my mind that malice alone entered into the reason and motive of their erection. 

The proofs are conclusive upon this subject. 

It is admitted by the counsel for the complainant that he would have no redress had the defendant erected houses or useful buildings or structures as near to complainant's line as these screens are, even though the consequent damage of such erection would have been as great or greater than it has been or now is from the effect of these screens upon the dwellings of complainant in every respect here complained of. 

But his contention is that these screens being a damage to the houses of complainant, and being erected for no good or useful purpose, but with the malicious motive of doing injury, they become and are such a nuisance to the property of complainant that equity will cause their removal, and enjoin their future erection or continuance. 

He invokes the legal maxim that— "Every man in the use of his own property must avoid injury to his neighbor's property as much as possible;"and argues that, while it is true that when one pursuies a strictly legal right his motives are immaterial, yet no man has a right to build and maintain an entirely useless structure for the sole purpose of injuring his neighbor. The argument has force, and appears irresistible in the light of the moral law that ought to govern all human action. And the civil law, coming close to the moral law, declares that— "He who, in making a new work upon his own estate, uses his right without trespassing either against any law, custom, title, or possession which may subject him to any service towards his neighbors, is not answerable for the damages which they may chance to sustain thereby, unless it be that he made that change merely with a view to hurt others without advantage to himself.”

Thus the civil law recognizes the moral law, and does not permit the owner of land to do an act upon his own premises for the express purpose of injuring his neighbor, where the act brings no profit or advantage to himself. The law furnishes redress, because the injury is malicious and unjustifiable. The moral law imposes upon every man the duty of doing unto others as he would that they should do unto him; and the common law ought to, and in my opinion does, require him to so use his own privileges and property as not to injure the rights of others maliciously, and without necessity.

It is true that he can use his own property, if for his own benefit or advantage, in many cases to the injury of his neighbor; and such neighbor has no redress, because the owner of the property is exercising a legal right which infringes on no legal right of the other. Therefore, and under this principle, the defendant might have erected a building for useful or 40

ornamental purposes, and shut out the light and air from complainant's windows; but when he erected these "screens" or "obscurers" for no useful or ornamental purpose, but out of pure malice against his neighbor, it seems to me a different principle must prevail. I do not think the common law permits a man to be deprived of water, air, or light for the mere gratification of malice. No one has an exclusive property in any of these elements except as the same may exist or be confined entirely on his own premises. If a pond of water lies entirely within his lands, without inlet and outlet, he may do with it as he pleases while he keeps it upon his own premises. He may also use as he pleases what air or light he can keep and hold within his dominion upon his own lands. But to the air and light between the earth and the heavens the right of each man is more or less dependent upon that of his neighbor. His neighbor must bear the inconvenience and annoyance that the legal and beneficial use of his premises engenders in this respect, if such use falls short of what the law treats as a nuisance; but the right to use one's premises to shut cut or curtail the use of either of these elements by his neighbor, out of mere malice and wickedness, when such use is not beneficial to him in any sense, does not exist in law or equity. The complainant in this case had a right to the use of the air and light about his houses, and over defendant's lands, until such right came in conflict with the defendant's enjoyment of his property. This air and light was free and unconfined, and the common property of all. * * *

But it must be remembered that no man has a legal right to make a malicious use of his property, not for any benefit or advantage to himself, but for the avowed purpose of damaging his neighbor. To hold otherwise would make the law a convenient engine, in cases like the present, to injure and destroy the peace and comfort, and to damage the property, of one's neighbor for no other than a wicked purpose, which in itself is, or ought to be, unlawful.

The right to do this can not, in an enlightened country, exist, either in the use of property, or in any way or manner. 

There is no doubt in my mind that these uncouth screens or "obscurers" as they are named in the record, are a nuisance, and were erected without right, and for a malicious purpose.

What right has the defendant, in the light of the just and beneficent principles of equity, to shut out God's free air and sunlight from the windows of his neighbor, not for any benefit or advantage to himself, or profit to his land, but simply to gratify his own wicked malice against his neighbor? None whatever.

The wanton infliction of damage can never be a right. It is a wrong, and a violation of right, and is not without remedy. 

The right to breathe the air, and to enjoy the sunshine, is a natural one, and no man can pollute the atmosphere, or shut out the light of heaven for no better reason than that the situation of his property is such that he is given the opportunity of so doing, and wishes to gratify his spite and malice towards his neighbor.

It is said that the adoption of statutes in several of the states making this kind of injury actionable shows that the courts have no right to furnish the redress without statutory authority. It has always been without a remedy. In all the cases where this class of injuries have occurred, proceeding alone from the malice of the defendant, it is held to be a wrong accompanied 41

by damage. That courts have failed to apply the remedy has ever been felt a reproach to the adminstration of the law; and the fact that the people have regarded this neglect of duty on the part of the courts so gross as to make that duty imperative by statutory law furnishes no evidence of the creation of a new right or the giving of a new remedy, but is a severe criticism upon the courts for an omission of duty already existing, and now imposed by statute upon them, which is only confirmatory of the common law. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 38, par. 22. (Par. 5)

HUBBARD v. PRESTON 

Michigan Supreme Court, 1892, 90 Mich. 221, 51 N. W. 209. 

LONG, J. On November 9, 1890, defendant shot and killed plaintiff's dog.

An action was commenced in justice's court, where defendant had judgment. 

On appeal to the circuit court for Wayne county, the cause was tried before a jury. The only question submitted to the jury on the trial in the circuit court was the value of the dog, which the jury found to be $25, and verdict and judgment were entered for that amount. Defendant brings error. 

On the trial the defendant introduced testimony tending to show justification for the killing. The court permitted the testimony to be introduced, but held that it did not amount to a justification. The only question raised in this court is whether the court should have submitted that branch of the case for the determination of the jury.

We think that the court was in error in not so doing. It appeared that the defendant did not keep a dog; that he lived on Bagg Street, city of Detroit, and for eight days prior to the shooting he and his family had been greatly annoyed by the congregation of a large number of dogs about his premises, barking, quarreling, and fighting there; that they came every night upon his lawn, about his house, when it became dark (on two occasions he counted 12 dogs), and that they kept up their cries all night at intervals; that he complained to the police on three different days prior to the killing, but without any relief, and he had driven them away on several nights: that the noise made by them kept the members of his family awake, and seriously annoyed them; that he did not know the owners; that on the night he killed plaintiff's dog he drove them away twice, but they returned; that he could not get near them, but they would return; that they became an intolerable nuisance, and finally about 8 o'clock in the evening, he went out with his revolver, and shot among them, while on his lawn. He did not know who owned any of them, and did not shoot at any particular dog. 

The defendant had a right to protect his family from such nuisance; and it was a question for the jury whether he used such means as were reasonable and necessary, under the circumstances, to rid himself of it. 

The judgment must be reversed, with costs, and a new trial ordered. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 31, par. 5. 42

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 2 

Questions 

1. If X is on your land, and you don't want him there, what may you do to remove him? 

2. Suppose he persists in walking across your land, claiming a right to do so, how should you proceed? 

3. If you are a farmer, and a heavy storm flattens fences so that 

Y's horses and cattle and hogs come over into your corn field and damage your corn, is Y liable? 

4. If Z, in mining coal beneath his land, crosses the boundary line by mistake and mines some of your coal, what may you do about it? 

5. An aviator, while 800 feet up, accidentally drops a bag of sand on your front yard, damaging some expensive shrubs, is he liable for the damage? 

6. Suppose he was flying only 100 feet up, so that your cattle are scared by the airplane, and he does this several times, so as to reduce their milk production, is he liable? 

7. You have a building on your land close to the boundry between yourself and X; X is about to erect a building on his land close to your building, what, if any, is his duty in the matter? 

8. The branches of Y's apple tree overhang your land and bear apples. Are you entitled to pick the apples? 

9. Your neighbor, Z, doesn't like you, so, in order to vent his spite, he builds a chicken house on his land close to your land. 

What rights, if any, have you? 

10. You are anxious to sell your farm, but Z spreads a report that the soil is thin and unproductive; it is winter, so it is difficult to refute his statements; what rights, if any, have you in the matter? 43

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 3 DUTIES OF LAND OCCUPIERS AND OWNERS 1. Toward Persons Using the Adjacent Highway 

Suppose B is in the possession of land and, because of a storm, earth has slid onto the highway from his land, and injures A; is B liable to A? No, because he is under no duty to keep the highway in front of his premises in repair and in a safe condition to travel. 

But, in some states, municipalities may impose upon land owners and occupiers the duty to keep in repair the sidewalk in front of their premises.

Suppose that B, in the process of building a house, makes an excavation near the public highway and, on a dark night, A wanders from the way, falls into the excavation and is injured; is B liable? 

Yes; he owes a duty to guard an excavation on his land which he makes so close to the highway as to render it dangerous to those who are reasonably using the highway. He should have put up a lantern or some other sort of light at night to warn travelers.
