Similarly, if B has a wire fence along the highway, he is under a duty toward travelers to maintain it in a safe condition. 

2. Toward Adjoining Land Owners and Occupiers

If B makes an excavation on his land close to A's land, he must use due care to avoid damaging A's buildings or land. He must also use due care-that is, the care of a man of average prudence under similar circumstances-if he erects a building or a wall close to A's land, or if he allows an old wall to stand close to A's land after a fire had destroyed the building. And if he removes quick-sand from beneath the surface of his land close to A's land, he must use due care not to damage A's land or buildings; but this does not apply to B's withdrawal of percolating waters from beneath his land. 

If B makes an excavation on his land, close to a building on A's 44

land, he must give notice to A of the intended excavation, in order to afford A a reasonable opportunity to protect his building. After such notice, the responsibility for and the cost of shoring up and taking precaution to prevent damage to the building falls on A. 

Where B has let the contract for erecting a building, with proper plans and specifications, to X, an independent contractor, this relieves B of any liability for negligence of X, but not the duty to give notice to A.

There is such a strong social interest in the cultivation of land that an occupier who cultivates land in the ordinary way is not liable for the spreading of thistle seeds to adjoining land or for the filling up of his neighbor's pond due to cultivating a hillside. 

In some states there are statutes imposing a duty to cut certain noxious weeds on his land in order to prevent their spreading to other land.

If surface water collects on A's land he must, in disposing of it, use due care that it does not cause damage to other land in the vicinity. 

3. Toward Adult Trespassers-Trespassing Animals 

Suppose that on B's farm, remote from a highway, there is an old well which either has no cover at all or is covered merely by rotten boards. B sees A, a trespasser, approaching the well; he does not warn A, and A falls in and is injured; is B liable to A? No; an adult trespasser upon land remote from a highway must take the premises as he finds them. B is under no duty, either to keep his premises safe for trespassers or to warn them of dangers thereon, even though the warning would require little effort. B may be regarded by his neighbors as morally culpable—an undesirable citizen-but incurs no legal liability by failure to warn A. Similarly, B is under no duty to keep his farm in repair for trespassing animals. So, where plaintiff's trespassing horse ate yew leaves on defendant's land and died therefrom, defendant was held not liable. 

If B has reason to suspect that someone is stealing his chattels and he sets a spring gun and kills a would-be felon he is excused therefor, because he would be excused if he had been present and killed in order to prevent a felony. But if his spring gun should kill one who was merely trespassing with no intent to steal or burglarize, then B is liable for wrongfully causing the trespasser's death; he could not, if he had been present, lawfully kill one who was merely trespassing and he may not lawfully do with a spring gun what he may not lawfully do with a gun held in his hand. 45

Suppose B is driving his auto truck on his own land and he sees A trespassing on the land; may B ignore his presence? No; as soon as he sees A he is under duty to use care, in driving his truck, so as not to hit A. The question arises most frequently in the case of railroad companies and trespassers on their tracks. As to whether there is a duty on the part of the occupier to look out for trespassers while changing, or when about to change, the condition of premises, there is disagreement among the courts. The better view is that he does owe such a duty, where trespassing has been so frequent in the past that there is reasonable ground for expecting that there will be trespassers.

The same principles apply to the case of trespassing animals. 

4. Toward Trespassing Children 

Suppose A has on his land, remote from a highway, something which is likely to attract children and likely to cause them personal injuries; does A owe any duty to keep children off his premises or to protect them in any way? Do they have any greater rights than trespassing adults?

In some states they do. Most of the cases in which such a duty has been imposed have been cases of railroad turntables. In a 

Minnesota case a boy of seven was injured while playing upon defendant railroad company's turntable, which was located in an open space near its passenger depot and not fenced or guarded in any way; the turntable thus located was very attractive to young children. It was held that the railroad company was under a duty to the seven year old child, because the latter was not an ordinary trespasser; and, since the turntable was attractive to children, the railroad company owed them a duty to protect them from a danger from which they could not be expected to protect themselves. 

Attempts have been made to apply the principle of the turntable cases to other attractive and dangerous things, but with little success. Thus it has been held that moving railroad cars, an old fruit tree, a high lumber pile, and a pond in an unfenced lot, were not within the rule; but an abandoned roadhouse has been held to be within the rule. In one case the doctrine was applied to cottonseed hulls which were attractive to cattle. 

5. Toward Licensees

Suppose that B asks and gets permission from A to walk across A's land; does A owe B any duty to warn B of any dangers on the land? Yes, he is under a duty to warn B of any defects in the con- 46

dition of the premises, of which he knows and which B is not likely to discover. For example, if on a dark night B asks permission to walk across A's back yard, and there is an unlighted excavation there, A owes him the duty to warn him of its presence there. 

Suppose A owns a vacant corner lot and people have been accustomed to drive across it as a short cut to the intersecting highway. A decides to fence in the lot; is he under any duty to warn the public of such change in the condition of the premises? Yes; he must put out lanterns or flares at night so that drivers will not drive against the newly constructed fence. Similar reasoning applies if 

A excavates close to where the people have been driving on the lot. 

6. Toward Invited Persons 

Where a person has been invited, but not on business in which the land occupier is or may be pecuniarily interested, does the latter owe any duty to the former as to the condition of the premises? 

Suppose, for example, A has invited B to take dinner with him, and B is injured by the falling of a glass door. In England B has no greater right than if he had been a mere licensee-one who was there by permission; in this country he has, in some states, the same rights as if he had been invited on business, but there are very few cases.

Towards a person who has been invited, either expressly or by implication, upon business which is or may be of pecuniary interest to the land occupier, the latter is under a duty to use reasonable care to keep the premises in a safe condition, and if they are temporarily out of repair, to warn of the defects of which he knows or ought to know. Thus, if A is in the shoe business and B comes in to buy shoes, and there is a dangerous unlighted stairway, A is under duty to warn B of the danger. And the duty exists even though 

B does not buy, and even when he comes back after buying to complain of the quality of the goods he bought or to complain that he has been short changed; and the duty exists toward the customer's children who accompany him on the visit. 

Suppose, while B is on A's premises, either as a licensee or a social guest or a business visitor, he becomes ill; is A under any legal duty to take care of B? In a Minnesota case, B, a business visitor, asked A, a farmer, to let him stay all night because of being ill, but was refused. It was quite cold; A placed B in B's sleigh, put the lines around B's shoulder, and started the horses toward the next town. The sleigh struck a rock, throwing B out, and he was severely frozen. The court held that A was liable; but the case has 47

been severely criticized as imposing an affirmative legal duty on merely moral grounds. There are statutes in some states imposing a duty, in case of an automobile accident, to carry the injured person to the nearest hospital even though there was no fault in causing the accident. 

7. Duty of Vendor or Lessor of Land 

If A erects a nuisance-such as a pig sty, in a residential neighborhood-and then sells and conveys the land, he does not escape liability therefor by such transfer. As to a defect in the condition of the premises, not amounting to a nuisance, for example, a defectively hung gate which is likely to fall, the vendor ordinarily ceases to be held liable after the purchaser takes possession of the land and becomes aware of the defect. 

If A leases to B, a tenant, some land on which there is a nuisance, he does not thereby escape liability for it. So, if the premises are in such a condition that ordinary use by the tenant-for example, of a defective cesspool-would create a nuisance, or if the lessor contemplates that the lessee will erect a nuisance-for example, a lime kiln in a residential neighborhood-the landlord as well as the tenant is liable.

Even where the defect in the condition of the premises is not serious enough to amount to a nuisance, the landlord may be liable for an injury occurring to a third person, properly on the premises, after the tenant takes possession. For example, where the lessor knows the premises are to be used by the public who will occupy the position of business visitors to the lessee, the lessor is under the same liability as if he himself had invited the public to use the premises; one of the cases thus holding was that of a bathing establishment with a defectively constructed toboggan slide. But if the premises are in repair at the time the lease takes effect and no nuisance or negligent act of the lessee is to be reasonably contemplated, the landlord is under no liability, either to the tenant or to a third person, to keep the premises in repair. 

Where, as in the case of apartment houses, the landlord has rented only a part of the premises, he owes the same duty to that part of the premises retained-the front yard, the stairways and the halls-which a land occupier owes to a person invited on business, so that if the plastering falls off the hall ceiling, a tenant's guest who is injured thereby may be in a position to recover from the landlord. The tenant stands in the position of a business visitor here, because the renting of the premises to him is a matter of 48

profit to the landlord; and the presence of guests invited by the tenant must reasonably have been contemplated by him. If the halls and stairways are out of repair, and the landlord knows about it and fails to repair, he cannot defend himself by saying that the tenant has no right to complain because he took the risk of injury by staying in the apartment; the tenant is justified in assuming the risk of injury; he need not move out of his apartment to avoid the danger.

Even where the landlord is under no duty whatever in regard to keeping the premises in repair, yet if he undertakes to do a particular piece of repair work he must use ordinary care in performing it; and if, having so undertaken the work, he leaves the premises in a dangerous condition, he will be liable to the tenant or the tenant's guest who is injured thereby. This principle was applied to a landlord who undertook to fix a privy floor but left it in a dangerous condition so that the tenant's wife fell through the floor and suffered a serious injury resulting in a miscarriage. But if the failure of the landlord to carry through his undertaking, as planned and expected, does not result in leaving the premises in a dangerous condition, there is no liability. For example, where he undertook to replace an old furnace with a new one, and there was a long delay in putting in the new furnace, and the tenant became ill with pneumonia and died, the landlord was not liable; because the tenant might have temporarily left the premises till the new furnace could be installed. 

8. Duty of Bailor of a Chattel

Suppose that A is asked by his neighbor B to lend him a step ladder. A lends him the ladder. The third step on it is defective because of decayed wood. B uses the ladder and because of the defective step he falls and is injured. Is A liable to B? Only if A knew of the defect and failed to warn B. But if A was in the business of renting out stepladders and B paid or agreed to pay for its use A would be liable to B if the defect was such that A could, by the exercise of ordinary care, have discovered it but failed to do so. 

The duty to know and to warn B exists not only towards B but the liability extends also to B's employees or members of B's family who might reasonably be expected to use the ladder. 

The above reasoning applies, of course, to any other chattel, such as an automobile. 

Suppose that A has an automobile that needs to be repaired. 

He takes it to B, a repair man, who undertakes to repair it, and 49

while so repairing it, is injured by the explosion of the gasoline tank due to a slight leak therein. Is A liable to B? Yes, at least if

A knew of the leak. The same reasoning applies, of course, to any chattel which A takes to B to be repaired. 

9. Duty of Vendor or Manufacturer of a Chattel 

If A sells a chattel to B and because of a defect in the chattel B is hurt, is A liable to B? Yes, if A knew of the defect and did not warn B. Or, if the chattel was dangerous to life—for example, an automobile with a defective wheel which breaks while the automobile is being driven-A is liable if by the use of ordinary care he could have discovered the defect. Logically, A should be similarly liable even though the chattel was not defective enough to be dangerous to life, for any damage to B caused by defects which he could have discovered by the exercise of ordinary care, but the law thus far has not imposed such a duty and this holding has been defended because of the interest of the law in the free marketing of chattels,

The above reasoning has been applied to a manufacturer of a chattel-for example, to the manufacturer of an automobile with a defective wheel and to the manufacturer of a cake with a nail in it. 

And where there is a duty it extends to the members of B's family or to his employees if they might reasonably be expected to use the chattel. 50

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 2) 

MILLS v. NEW YORK & HARLEM R. R. CO. 

New York Superior Court, 1864. 2 Robertson 326. 

The action was to recover damages for the loss of a number of horses. 

The complaint alleges that the defendants carelessly, negligently and improperly placed a large number of their horses, then infected and suffering from infectious, contagious and dangerous diseases, known as farcy and glanders, in their shed adjoining the stables of the plaintiff, and allowed them to remain there for a space of time. That said horses so infected and diseased, by reason of the want of proper care, by the defendants, communicated the said disease to the plaintiff's horses; and that through the negligence, carelessness, and improper conduct of the defendants in placing said horses in the yard and shed of the defendants, adjoining the stables of the plaintiff, the said disease was communicated to the plaintiff's horses, and they have died.

The answer of the defendants alleges that their said horses were placed upon the defendants' premises, contiguous to their railroad, and that if the plaintiff has sustained any damage, it was occasioned by his own negligence and not by the negligence of the defendants. 

The action was tried by a justice of this court and a jury. A motion was made to dismiss the complaint, which was denied, and the defendants excepted. Several requests were made by the defendants to the court to charge the jury, which were refused and the defendants excepted; they also excepted to such parts of the charge as were inconsistent with such requests. 

The jury found a verdict for the plaintiff, upon which judgment was entered and the defendants appealed. * * *

By the Court, MONELL, J. The ground of the plaintiff's action, as stated in his complaint, and attempted to be proved on the trial, was that the injury to his horses was occasioned by the negligent act of the defendants. 

It is not now claimed that the defendants are liable for any other reason. 

The motion to dismiss the complaint was not put upon the ground that the evidence of the plaintiff failed to establish any negligence of the defendants, but on the ground that the defendants had a right to use their own property in any way they thought proper, except where the use was a nuisance. This 

I do not understand to have been disputed. The judge charged the jury, substantially, that the use of the defendants' sheds as a shelter for their diseased horses was not a nuisance, and therefore they had a right to use them for such purpose . . ., and no exception was taken to the charge. But every man is responsible for the negligent use of his own property, and the right of enjoyment is limited to a prudent and careful use . . . 

The sheds or pens in which the defendants placed their diseased horses were separated from the plaintiff's stables by a wooden partition, and the heads of the plaintiff's and defendants' horses were towards and near the partition; so that except for the partition they could have come into immediate contact. In the rear of the stables a board fence separated the respective lots. It was in evidence that the defendant's horses gnawed the 51

boards and the fence, and made holes through the partition. One witness testified that the defendants' horses ate through the partition a hole half a foot in size, which came through upon the manger of the plaintiff's stable. 

There was a water tank in the street outside of the defendants' premises, at which their diseased horses were allowed to drink. The doors or gates were left open, and the horses were suffered to go out. This tank was also used for watering the plaintiff's horses.

The weight of the evidence is, that the disease which the defendants' horses had (the glanders) was contagious and not infectious. The holes gnawed by the defendants' horses through the wooden partition, and into the fence between the lots, afforded a means of easy contact with the virus deposited by the defendants' horses, and was sufficient to convey the disorder. It was the duty of the defendants to have immediately closed the holes, and to have been vigilant in using all necessary means to prevent the disorder from being conveyed by contact to the plaintiff's horses. This they do not seem to have done, for the plaintiff himself nailed boards over the holes. 

The defendants knew that the disorder was contagious, and they were bound to exercise all the care that prudence and rightful regard for the interests of others required, or else to place their horses so remote from the wooden partition that contact would not have been possible. 

Again, suffering the defendants' horses to go to the street tank for water, was not only unnecessary, as they had water in their yard, but it was highly negligent. There could be no more sure way of communicating the disease, and whatever may have been the right of the defendants to use their premises for hospital purposes, for their diseased horses, they had not the right to let them go at large in the street, or allow them to drink at a public tank. 

This is within the principle established by some of the English cases I am of the opinion that there was sufficient evidence of negligence of the defendants to go to the jury, and therefore that the motion to dismiss the complaint was properly overruled.

The learned justice in submitting the case to the jury, instructed them that "if the plaintiff's horses, without any negligence on his part, and before he was aware, or ought, as a careful man, to have known of the defendants' diseased horses, died as a result of the contact with defendants' diseased horses, and if at the same time the defendants were guilty of negligence, the plaintiff is entitled to recover." And they were told that they must be satisfied that the defendants knew of the existence of the holes, or that as careful men they ought to have known of their existence. 

The only question, therefore, in the case which could determine the defendants' liability, namely, the absence of all negligence on the part of the plaintiff, and the negligence of the defendants, was properly and fairly submitted to the jury, and I can find no reason for disturbing their verdict. 

All the requests to charge (although refused to be made in the precise language in which they were submitted) were substantially made to the jury. A judge is not bound to use the language of a request to charge; if he gives the substance of it to the jury it is sufficient. 

I am in favor of affirming the judgment. To concede the right of the defendants to place a large number of sickly and diseased horses upon their own premises, in immediate contiguity with the neighboring stables of the plaintiff, is conceding all that the defendants can expect, and it is the only 52

concession the law makes. In the enjoyment of that right, they must look to it that their neighbor is not injured by their negligence. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 44, par. 30. 

AUGUSTA RAILWAY COMPANY v. ANDREWS 

Georgia Supreme Court, 1892. 89 Ga. 653, 19 S. E. 713.

SIMMONS, J. According to the declaration, there was in the city of 

Augusta, at the time of the alleged injury, a system of electric wires operated by the defendant, the Augusta Railway Company; there was also another system, consisting of the fire-alarm wires of the Augusta Fire Department; and the plaintiff was employed in putting up wires for a third, that of a telephone company. In stringing the wires on the poles, it became necessary at a certain point for the plaintiff to place the telephone wire above and across the fire-alarm wire, and for that purpose he ascended a pole of the fire-alarm system, to the height of the wire, and while attempting to place the telephone wire above and across the fire-alarm wire, received from the latter a shock which caused him to fall to the ground, a distance of some twenty-three feet; by which means he was seriously injured. He charges that his injuries "were caused solely by the carelessness of the defendant company in so negligently constructing, using and operating what is known as its feed-wire * * * as to permit and allow the same to come in contact with said fire-alarm wire, at the intersection of "two named streets of the city; and negligently and carelessly failing to separate and keep separated at a safe and proper distance its said feed-wire and said fire-alarm wire at the time and point indicated;" "that there was being transmitted over said feedwire at the time petitioner received said injuries a powerful and deadly current of electricity used to propel the cars of the defendant, which current was carried over said fire-alarm wire from said point of contact to the place where petitioner was working as aforesaid, and thence into and through his body;" and that the "fact of contact of said feed-wire and said fire-alarm wire was known, or by proper diligence might have been known to the defendant." The declaration was demurred to on several grounds, one of which was that it set out no legal cause of action. The demurrer was overruled, and the defendant excepted. 

Whether, so far as concerned the safety of the public who pass along the streets and under the wires, it was the duty of the railway company or of those in charge of the fire-alarm system, or both, to place guard-wires under and over their electric wires, to prevent contact, it is unnecessary now to decide. Under the facts alleged, we are clear that the plaintiff was not entitled to recover. He does not allege any fact going to show that the defendant company was under any duty or obligation to protect him at the time or place of the injury. He does not allege that he had permission from those operating or in charge of the fire-alarm system to climb its poles in the prosecution of his business. Without permission and without notice even, so far as appears from this declaration, he climbed the pole and became a trespasser upon the fire-alarm system. He had no right to go upon the pole without permission; and when he did so, he took the risk incident to the trespass. If he had obtained permission from those in charge of the fire-alarm 53

system to climb their poles to carry on his business, he would have been in a position somewhat analogous to that of a servant of the licensors, and if, while acting in pursuance of the license, he had been injured by the negligence of the railway company, he might be entitled to recover. Or if he had been upon the street, or in any place where he had a known right to be, and had been injured by the negligence of the railway company, he would be entitled to recover. Whatever may be the reciprocal duties of electric companies between themselves, as to guard-wires, etc., each must see to it, up to the measure of full diligence, that the public is protected upon the streets from the danger of contact with its wires when charged with the deadly electric fluid. If a person, however, leaves his proper place in the street or highway, and climbs a pole twenty-three feet high which supports an electric wire, taking with him a wire to throw across the one on the pole, and does this without permission from the company whose system he has thus entered upon, and by reason of the contact of that company's wire with the feed-wire of another company is injured, he can not recover from either company. If the plaintiff had given the railway company notice that he was going up the pole, and it had reasonable grounds to believe that he was on the pole, and it had known or ought to have known that its wire was in contact with the other wire, it might be liable to him for injuries received by him on account of its negligence. But the plaintiff does not allege that the defendant had notice of his being on the pole, or that it had any grounds for believing that he would be on the pole. We therefore think that, in any view of the case, the court should have sustained the general demurrer and dismissed the action. * * * 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 45, par. 31. 

RAILROAD COMPANY v. STOUT United States Supreme Court, 1873. 84 U. S. (17 Wall.) 657. 

Error to the Circuit Court for the District of Nebraska.

Henry Stout, a child six years of age and living with his parents, sued, by his next friend,¹ the Sioux City and Pacific Railroad Company, in the court below, to recover damages for an injury sustained upon a turntable belonging to the said company. The turntable was in an open space, about eighty rods from the company's depot, in a hamlet or settlement of one hundred to one hundred and fifty persons. Near the turntable was a travelled road passing through the depot grounds, and another travelled road nearby. On the railroad ground, which was not inclosed or visibly separated from the adjoining property, was situated the company's engine-house, and about a quarter of a mile distant from this was the turntable on which the plaintiff was injured. There were but few houses in the neighborhood of the turntable, and the child's parents lived in another part of the town, and about three-fourths of a mile distant. The child, without the knowledge of his parents, set off with two other boys, the one nine and the other ten years of age, to go to the depot, with no definite purpose in view. When the boys arrived there, it was proposed by some of them to go to the turntable to 1. A plaintiff minor must sue by a "next friend" who will control the proceedings and be responsible for costs. A defendant minor defends by a guardian ad libem (for purposes of the litigation) appointed by the court. 54

play. The turntable was not attended or guarded by any servant of the company, was not fastened or locked, and in attempting to get upon it the foot of the child (he being at the time upon the railroad track) was caught between the end of the rail on the turntable as it was revolving, and the end of the iron rail on the main track of the road, and was crushed. 

One witness, then a servant of the company, testified that he had previously seen boys playing at the turntable, and had forbidden them from playing there. But the witness had no charge of the table, and did not communicate the fact of having seen boys playing there, to any of the officers or servants of the company having the table in charge. 

One of the boys, who was with the child when injured, had previously played upon the turntable when the railroad men were working on the track, in sight, and not far distant.

It appeared from the testimony that the child had not, before the day on which he was now injured, played at the turntable, or had, indeed, ever been there.

The table was constructed on the railroad company's own land, and, the testimony tended to show, in the ordinary way. It was a skeleton turntable, that is to say, it was not planked between the rails, though it had one or two loose boards upon the ties. There was an iron latch fastened to it which turned on a hinge, and, when in order, dropped into an iron socket on the track, and held the table in position while using. The catch of this latch was broken at the time of the accident. The latch, which weighed eight or ten pounds, could be easily lifted out of the catch and thrown back on the table, and the table allowed to be moved about. This latch was not locked, or in any way fastened down before it was broken, and all the testimony on that subject tended to show that it was not usual for railroad companies to lock or guard turntables, but that it was usual to have a latch with a catch, or a draw-bolt, to keep them in position when used. ***

On the question whether there was negligence on the part of the railway company in the management or condition of its turntable, the judge charged the jury- "That to maintain the action it must appear by the evidence that the turntable, in the condition, situation, and place where it then was, was a dangerous machine, one which, if unguarded or unlocked, would be likely to cause injury to children; that if in its construction and the manner in which it was left it was not dangerous in its nature, the defendants were not liable for negligence; that they were further to consider whether, situated as it was as the defendants' property in a small town, somewhat remote from habitations, there was negligence in not anticipating that injury might occur if it was left unlocked or ungarded; that if they did not have reason to anticipate that children would be likely to resort to it, or that they would be likely to be injured if they did resort to it, then there was no negligence."

The jury found a verdict of $7,500 for plaintiff, from the judgment upon which this writ of error was brought. ***

Mr. Justice Hunt delivered the opinion of the court. 

1st. It is well settled that the conduct of an infant of tender years is not to be judged by the same rule which governs that of an adult. While it is the general rule in regard to an adult, that to entitle him to recover damages 55

for an injury resulting from the fault or negligence of another, he must himself have been free from fault, such is not the rule in regard to an infant of tender years. The care and caution required of a child is according to his maturity and capacity only, and this is to be determined in each case by the circumstances of that case.

But it is not necessary to pursue this subject. The record expressly states that "the counsel for the defendant disclaim resting their defence on the ground that the company was not negligent, and claim that the injury to the plaintff was accidental or brought upon himself." 

This disclaimer ought to dispose of the question of the plaintiff's negligence, whether made in a direct form, or indirectly under the allegation that the plaintiff was a trespasser upon the railroad premises, and therefore can not recover.

A reference to some of the authorities on the last suggestion may, however, be useful.

In the well-known case of Lynch v. Nurdin, 1 Adol. & Ell. (N. S.) 29, the child was clearly a trespasser in climbing upon the cart, but was allowed to recover.

In Birge v. Gardner, 19 Conn. 507, the same judgment was given and the same principle was laid down. In most of the actions, indeed, brought to recover for injuries to children, the position of the child was that of a technical trespasser.

In Daly v. Norwich and Worcester Railroad Company, 26 Conn. 591, it is said the fact that the person was trespassing at the time is no excuse, unless he thereby invited the act or his negligent conduct contributed to it. 

In Bird v. Holbrook, 4 Bing 628, (see also Loomis v. Terry, 17 Wend. 496; 

Wright v. Ramscot, 1 Saund. 83; Johnson v. Patterson, 14 Conn. 1; State v.

Moore, 31 Conn. 479), the plaintiff was injured by the spring guns set in the defendant's grounds, and although the plaintiff was a trespasser the defendant was held liable.

There are no doubt cases in which the contrary rule is laid down. But we conceive the rule to be this: that while a railway company is not bound to the same degree of care in regard to mere strangers who are unlawfully upon its premises that it owes to passengers conveyed by it, it is not exempt from its negligence or from its tortious acts. 

2nd. Was there negligence on the part of the railway company in the management or condition of its turntable? 

The charge on this point (see supra) was an impartial and intelligent one. 

Unless the defendant was entitled to an order that the plaintiff be nonsuited, or, as it is expressed in the practice of the United States courts, to an order directing a verdict in its favor, the submission was right. If, upon any construction which the jury was authorized to draw from it, the conclusion of negligence can be justified, the defendant was not entitled to this order, and the judgment can not be disturbed. To express it affirmatively, if from the evidence given it might justly be inferred by the jury that the defendant, in the construction, location, management, or condition of its machine had omitted that care and attention to prevent the occurrence of accidents which prudent and careful men ordinarily bestow, the jury was at liberty to find for the plaintiff.

That the turntable was a dangerous machine, which would be likely to 56

cause injury to children who resorted to it, might fairly be inferred from the injury which actually occurred to the plaintiff. There was the same liability to injury to him, and no greater, than existed with reference to all children. When the jury learned from the evidence that he had suffered a serious injury, by his foot being caught between the fixed rail of the roadbed and the turning rail of the table they were justified in believing that there was a probability of the occurrence of such accidents. 

So, in looking at the remoteness of the machine from inhabited dwellings, when it was proved to the jury that several boys from the hamlet were at play there on this occasion and that they had been at play upon the turntable on other occasions, and within the observation and to the knowledge of the employees of the defendant, the jury were justified in believing that children would probably resort to it, and that the defendant should have anticipated that such would be the case.

As it was in fact, on this occasion, so it was to be expected that the amusement of the boys would have been found in turning this table while they were on it or about it. This could certainly have been prevented by locking the turntable when not in use by the company. It was not shown that this would cause any considerable expense or inconvenience to the defendant. 

It could probably have been prevented by the repair of the broken latch. 

This was a heavy catch which, by dropping into a socket, prevented the revolution of the table. There had been one on this table weighing some eight or ten pounds, but it had been broken off and had not been replaced. 

It was proved to have been usual with railroad companies to have upon their turntables a latch or bolt, or some similar instrument. The jury may well have believed that if the defendant had incurred the trifling expense of replacing this latch, and had taken the slight trouble of putting it in its place, these very small boys would not have taken the pains of lifting it out, and thus the whole difficulty have been avoided. Thus reasoning, the jury would have reached the conclusion that the defendant had omitted the care and attention it ought to have given, that it was negligent, and that its negligence caused the injury to the plaintiff. The evidence is not strong and the negligence is slight, but we are not able to say that there is not evidence sufficient to justify the verdict. We are not called upon to weigh, to measure, to balance the evidence, or to ascertain how we should have decided if acting as jurors. The charge was in all respects sound and judicious and there being sufficient evidence to justify the finding, we are not authorized to disturb it. Affirmed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 46, par. 32. (Par. 6)

MILLER v. PECK DRY GOODS CO. 

Missouri Court of Appeals, 1903. 104 Mo. App. 609, 78 S. W. 682.

ELLISON, J. This action is for personal injury received by the plaintiff as the result of falling out of a window of defendant's building to the street twenty-five feet below. At the time of injury plaintiff was a child between one and two years old. The judgment in the trial court was for the plaintiff. 57

It appears that defendant is the proprietor of a large retail business in Kansas City known as a department store. That in the prosecution of such business it has and maintains a building several stories in height, at the northwest corner of Eleventh and Main streets. That on the third floor of the building, twenty-five feet above the sidewalk, defendant maintains a large general reception room to which the public, including women accompanied by little children, are invited to come. That on the south side of the room is a large window, extending down to within three or four inches of the floor; the lower sash of which was between five and six feet high and hung on pivots with sliding bolts at the top and bottom to hold it in place. 

That when unbolted it would, with slight pressure, swing outwardly from the bottom. That some time prior to the injury to plaintiff there were crossbars or rods fastened from the bottom of the window frame up to a height sufficient to prevent children from falling out of the window should it be open. These were maintained by the defendant in the summer months, but at the time of the accident had been removed. 

On November 5, 1901, plaintiff's mother took her to defendant's reception room. They were accompanied by a lady friend and two other small children, one being the friend's and the other a sister of plaintiff. The mother desiring to do some shopping left plaintiff and the other child in charge of the friend while she went out into the body of the store to make her purchases. 

While she was gone, the two older children were playing about the room near the window, the plaintiff taking part in a limited way. They were on and off of a couch near the window and at times stood on the window sill, which, as before stated, was nearly flush with the floor. During this play of the children plaintiff sat on the window sill and leaning back against the glass, pressed the sash out which caused her to fall to the pavement below. 

Though the fall was twenty-five feet and the pavement was stone, yet plaintiff escaped death; but her injuries were such as to afford no ground of complaint at the amount of the verdict. 

To hold defendant liable for the injury, the petition charges four specific acts of negligence. First, that the sash was left so that it would swing out when only slight pressure was applied, such as by a little child leaning against it. Second, that the sash being swung on pivots defendant did not have any bars, or grating, or other fixtures in the window so as to prevent small children like plaintiff from falling out. Third, that defendant failed to notify plaintiff's mother or the lady friend left in charge of the children of the condition of the window. Fourth, that defendant kept the room for the reception of mothers and small children well knowing that a child "could, and might, easily fall out of the window." 

The principal point urged by defendant is that plaintiff did not make a case and that its demurrer to the evidence ought to have been sustained. 

This is based on the fact that the window sash was provided with a bolt at the top and bottom with which it was ordinarily fastened and that when so fastened it was perfectly safe. That up to an hour and a half of the time of the misfortune to plaintiff, it was known to have been bolted. That somewhere between that time and the accident, some one without the knowledge of defendant or its servants had unfastened the bolts and opened the window and then left it shut or swung back in place, but neglected to fasten it. That defendant could not, in reason, be expected to foresee that that would be 58

done and in consequence that a child might fall out. Keeping in mind, in this connection, that the petition charges that it was negligence in defendant to maintain such a window, at such a place, without crossbars or rods to keep children from falling out of it, if it should be open, or should be shut but unbolted, the defendant's contention amounts to a statement that the trial court, as a matter of law, should have declared such omission was not negligence. We are of opinion that the trial court's action in refusing to sustain the demurrer was proper. In view of the fact that the room was one almost constantly occupied by women and small children, it seems to us that to maintain, twenty-five feet above the ground, a window which extended to the floor, with no bars across it, in the sole dependence that it would remain bolted, made a state of case which should be left to the jury that they might say whether that was such an act, or series of acts, as a man of ordinary prudence and carefulness would commit. It certainly should occur to any careful man that a window hung on pivots so as to swing out from the bottom, coming to the floor of a room in the third story of a building, and not opening out onto a balcony, the room to be occupied by large numbers of women and small children, should have more safety guard than merely two bolts which could be so easily withdrawn. 

The law as to the duty that a person owes to those he invites onto his premises has been so uniformly stated as that it has grown to be uncontroverted that he must keep his premises in such condition as to be reasonably free from danger. And that if necessary he maintains dangerous places in the prosecution of his business he must have them guarded, or give warning of their existence. It has been discussed and stated by the Supreme Court in O'Donnel v. Patton, 117 Mo. 13. By the St. Louis Court of Appeals, through judges Thompson and Goode, in Welch v. McAllister, 15 Mo. App. 492; 

Sykes v. Railway, 88 Mo. App. 193, 204; and Kean v. Schoening, 77 S. W. 335. 

And by this court through Judge SMITH in Hartman v. Muehlebach, 64 

Mo. App. 578.

The difference between this case and ordinary cases lies in that the plaintiff, being a mere helpless infant, was not on defendant's premises for the transaction of business with defendant. But that difference in fact makes no difference in point of law. For, it being shown that defendant maintained the room as a reception or waiting room for women patrons of the store who were accompanied by their children, the invitation which defendant gave out to the trading public must be held to include such children as plaintiff who were taken there by their parents. From this, it follows that the same duty which the owner owes to his customer he likewise owes to the 

Judgment affirmed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 46, par. 34. (Par. 7)

MCGINLEY v. ALLIANCE TRUST CO. 

Missouri Supreme Court, 1901. 168 Mo. 257, 66 S. W. 153. 

VALLIANT, J. Action for damages by a member of a tenant's family against a landlord for personal injuries suffered by reason of alleged 59

negligent construction and negligent failure to repair a stairway in the landlord's possession.

The statements in the petition are to the effect that defendant was the owner of certain tenement houses in Kansas City divided cff into flats or apartments, which were rented to tenants, each tenant renting and occupying exclusively a flat or suite of rooms, and using in common the back porches or galleries and stairways appurtenant for ingress and egress, the rooms or apartments alone being rented to and in the exclusive use of the respective tenants, while the porches or galleries and stairways were in the possession and control of the landlord, were not exclusively appurtenant to the apartments of any one tenant, but were designed for and used by all the tenants in common for ingress and egress to and from their apartments respectively. 

That plaintiff's father rented one of these flats or suites of rooms and lived in it with his family of which the plaintiff was a member. Several months after the family had been living there, on a hot evening, June 14, 1896, the plaintiff and her two sisters were sitting on the steps of one of these stairways eating a luncheon, when their mother, aiming to come into the house by means of this stairway, ascended the steps and there not being room enough for her to pass while the three sisters were sitting as they were, the plaintiff arose to make room for her mother to pass and in doing so leaned against the railing of the stairway which broke loose or gave way and she fell to the ground below, about sixteen feet, and suffered a serious injury. The accident resulted from the fact that the railing was negligently constructed, in that it was not braced as it should have been and was fastened with nails that were too small for the purpose and which had been exposed to the weather for three or four years and the nail holes had become rotten and the railing was negligently suffered to remain thus out of repair, the defendant knowing or by ordinary care would have known its condition and the plaintiff did not know it. There was an answer of denial and contributory negligence.

Upon the trial the testimony on the part of the plaintiff tended to prove the facts as above mentioned. There was no evidence that the defendant in its contract with plaintiff's father expressly reserved possession and control of the porches and stairways, but the circumstances tend to justify that inference. The express contract was only for a renting by the month of the suite of rooms. The porches and stairways were necessary appurtenances, but as such belonged as well to apartments rented to and occupied by other tenants as to the suite of rooms rented to and occupied by plaintiff's father. In the contract there was nothing said on the subject of repairs or of the condition as to safety of the premises. At the conclusion of the plaintiff's evidence the defendant offered an instruction to the effect that the plaintiff was not entitled to recover which instruction was given, a judgment of nonsuit followed, and the plaintiff appeals.

I. The question for our determination is whether a landlord is liable in damages to a member of his tenant's family under the circumstances above indicated. A member of a tenant's family in such case stands in the same relation to the landlord as the tenant himself. The question may therefore be more briefly stated thus: Is a landlord liable in damages to his tenant under such circumstances?

A landlord in under no obligation to make repairs on the leased premises during the term unless he has contracted to do so and therefore he is not liable for consequences that may result from a failure to so make repairs. But a 60

statement of this familiar proposition does not answer the question before us. 

These porches and galleries were not a part of the premises rented to the plaintiff's father; he had only a use of them in common with all the other tenants similarly situated. His right to use them is implied from the situation; he could make no use of the apartments he had rented without them. * 

There can be no doubt that the landlord had the right to enter upon these porches and galleries and stairways and make needed repairs and no one tenant nor all of them could forbid him. Yet it was not stipulated in his contract that he might do so. He had a right to so enter by virtue of his estate. 

And it is equally clear that no one else had such a right and if any one had attempted to make repairs the landlord could have forbidden him to do sɔ. 

To the general proposition stated above that a landlord is not bound to make repairs unless he binds himself by contract to do so it may be added that he is not bound to make repairs on the leased premises where the lease covers only a part of the building. This was said in the opinion in Ward v. 

Fagin, 101 Mo. 666, and it is the generally accepted doctrine. (18 Am. & Eng. 

Ency. Law (2nd Ed.) 220.) But this addition to the proposition above stated does not answer the question in this case. The plaintiff does not complain of the failure of the landlord to repair that part of the premises which he had leased to her father, but the complaint is of negligent construction and failure to repair the part of the building in the possession and under the control of the landlord which it was necessary for her father's family to use, in common with all the other tenants, to render the premises her father did lease available for the purpose for which the lease was made. 

The law on this subject has been discussed in nearly all the courts in England and America, as is shown by the great array of authorities which the learning and research of counsel have enabled them to present in their briefs. 

Whilst the decisions are not uniform, yet we are satisfied that the summary made by the law-writer in 18 Am. & Eng. Ency. Law (2nd Ed.) 220, is correct as to the weight of the authorities and right in principle. We quote what the learned author says: "The rule laid down by the weight of authority is that when the landlord leases separate portions of the same building to different tenants and reserves under his control those parts of the building or premises used in common by all the tenants, he is under an implied obligation to use reasonable diligence to keep in a safe condition the parts over which he so reserves control."

Then after citing in a note a long list of cases decided supporting that doctrine, the author proceeds: "Thus it is held by the weight of authority that an implied duty is imposed upon the landlord to keep in repair common passage ways and approaches retained under his control and used by the several tenants as the means of access to the portion of the premises demised to them, and that the landlord is liable for injuries received by a tenant because of the landlord's negligence in performing this duty. But there are a few cases holding the contrary rule. The character of the liability has been said to be the same as that of any owner of real estate who holds out invitations or inducements to others to use his property, to exercise reasonable care and skill to render the premises reasonably fit for use for the uses which he has invited or induced others to make of them." In the notes to this text are given references to many decisions, English and American, in which the subject is discussed and which we think clearly support the conclusion of the text writers upon them. This subject is also ably discussed in a note to Dollard v. Roberts, 14 61

L. R. A. 238, and the same conclusion is reached as that above quoted. 

The authorities cited by those two law-writers will afford the inquirer on this subject all the information he will desire. An attempt is made in some of the cases to draw a distinction between tenement or apartment buildings having common hallways, stairways, etc., in which the landlord employs janitors to attend to them and buildings of the same kind where no janitors are employed, and also between such buildings in which there are elevators operated by the landlord and those in where there are none. (2 Wood. on L. and Ten. (2 ed.) p. 870.) But we are unable to appreciate any such distinction on principle. As a matter of evidence, it is easier to prove that the landlord has retained control of the common passages when he has janitors and elevator operators in his employ, but their absence would not affect the principle if the fact of the landlord's control can be established by other evidence or be reasonably inferred from all the circumstances. In determining the question as to his control, we start out with the proposition that the landlord's estate in the land makes him the controller of the premises until he parts with that right. The evidence in this case tended to show that the landlord had exclusive control, for the purposes of construction and repairs, of the porches, galleries and stairways in question and that being so the law attaches to him liability for a failure to perform his duty in those respects and the court should not have sustained the demurrer to the evidence on the ground that he was not liable under such circumstances. 

II. It is said, however, that the plaintiff and her sisters were not using the stairway for ingress and egress when the accident occurred, and that the landlord, if liable at all, is only so when the stairway fails to serve that purpose. Whether the use then being made of the stairway was such as the landlord, when he rented the rooms to plaintiff's father, and when he thereby impliedly invited them to make use of it, had a right to anticipate would naturally be made of it, is a question that appeals for its answer to the common sense of the triers of the fact in the light of the evidence, and of the ordinary experience of mankind. There was sufficient in this case to have left that question to the jury. * *

The court erred in sustaining the demurrer to the evidence, and therefore, the judgment is reversed and cause remanded to be tried according to the law as herein expressed. 

All concur. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 48, par. 37. 62

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 3 

Questions 1. In excavating on your land to put up a building, you dig close to the highway, and on a dark night X falls into the hole. Are you liable? 

2. A farmer cultivates a hillside in the ordinary way; at the foot of the hill there is a mill pond on Y's land; the dirt washed down from the hill, fills up the pond. Is the farmer liable to Y? 

3. Your neighbor's horse has escaped from his pasture and is on your land, where you are hauling produce in a truck; may you drive just as if the horse were not there? 

4. Burglars have broken into your house, and you think they may break in again, so you set a spring gun for them. What risk do you take in doing so? 

5. You have on your land an old apple tree with limbs close to the ground so that the children of the neighborhood frequently come and climb up the tree, and one day one of them falls and is hurt; are you liable? 

6. On a dark night, X asks your permission to go across your back yard and you give the permission. He falls into an unguarded excavation and is hurt; are you liable? 

7. X keeps a dry goods store in which he fails to light a stairway leading to the basement. Y, a customer, and Z, a tramp, both fall down the stairway and are hurt. Is X liable to either one? 

8. Jones keeps a shoe store; Smith comes in and buys a pair of shoes and pays for them; but he finds on getting home that the shoes are too small, so he brings them back the next day to exchange them, and a chandelier falls on his head and injures him. Is Jones liable to him? 

9. A, a stock-feed salesman, calls on B, a farmer, to sell him some feed; while there, he becomes ill and asks B to let him stay all night; B refuses, and, on A's way to the nearest town ten miles distant, he suffers seriously from the severe cold. Is B liable? 

10. X rents an apartment in Y's apartment house. The ceiling in the hall becomes likely to fall at any time; X notifies Y but Y does nothing about it. A week later it falls and injures X. Is Y liable? 63

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 4 CONTRACT TO SELL LAND 1. Making the Contract A. Offer and Acceptance 

A contract comes into existence when A makes an offer to B and B accepts the offer. The offer must be communicated to B, but the acceptance need not be communicated to A. If A in making his offer authorizes B to send his communication by a particular means—for example, by mail-and B sends his acceptance in that way, there is a contract at once even though the acceptance is never received. If A makes no stipulation as to how the acceptance is to be sent, B is authorized to use the same means which was employed by A; if A's offer is by mail he implicitly authorizes B to accept by mail; and if it is by wire he implicitly authorizes B to accept by wire. 

But there must be an offer and an acceptance; cross-offers are not enough, though identical in terms. Thus if A, by mail, offers to sell B his home farm for $10,000 and B at the same time offers, by mail, to buy it for $10,000, there is no contract. 

Suppose A, in offering by mail to sell his farm to B, offers by mistake to sell it for $10,000 but meant $12,000, the mistake being that of his typist, and B duly accepts the offer. Is there a contract? 

Yes, though there is no actual meeting of the minds, the expression of mutual assent being enough. If, at the time of acceptance, B knew that there was a mistake, because of previous communications from A, this is fraudulent conduct on B's part, which will prevent his enforcing the contract.

After B receives A's offer he must accept promptly, usually the same day or the next; if he does not, the offer comes to an end. If A says the offer is to remain open for-say, five days-B has the five days within which to accept, unless A should in the meantime rerevoke the offer. The offer comes to an end also if B makes a count- 64

er offer; for example, if B should make a counter offer of $9500; but a mere inquiry by B, as whether A will accept $9500, is not considered a counter offer.

B. Must the Contract Be in Writing? 

In very early English law, jurors were a group of witnesses from the neighborhood, whose verdict was based upon their own knowledge of what had occurred. But the jury slowly changed into what we have today-a group of persons who do not depend upon their own knowledge in giving their verdict, but base it upon the testimony of witnesses, and other evidence produced before them in open court. It was during the course of that very important transition that the English Parliament, in 1676, passed what is usually known as the Statute of Frauds, which required that in certain classes of contracts no action could be maintained, unless there was a written memorandum signed by the party to be charged in the particular litigation. Thus, if A contracts to sell land to B, and only A signs the written memorandum, B may enforce the contract against A, but A cannot enforce it against B.

Most of the provisions of the English statute have been enacted substantially in most American States. Those dealing with contracts are: (1) a promise by an executor or administrator to pay out of his own pocket a claim against a decedent's estate; (2) a promise in consideration of marriage; (3) an agreement not to be performed within a year from the making thereof; (4) a contract to answer for the debt, default, or miscarriage of another; (5) A. a contract for the sale of any interest in land; B. a contract for the sale of goods, wares, and merchandise, for the price of 10 pounds or upwards, unless the buyer accepts or receives a part of the goods, or pays a part of the purchase money, or gives something in earnest to bind the bargain.

The above classes of cases were selected because, in those cases, there would be a strong temptation to get witnesses to commit perjury, and also because the courts had not at that time worked out any satisfactory method of controlling verdicts; today, if a verdict is clearly against the evidence, the trial court will set it aside. Besides, parties to a suit, and others who were interested in its outcome, were not competent witnesses; today, with minor exceptions, parties may testify freely, their interest merely going to the credibility and weight of their testimony. 

A contract to convey land need not be a formal contract in writing; it is enough that there be a written memorandum of the con- 65

tract and such a memoradum may be in letters of offer and acceptance. 

2. Enforcing the Contract at Law-Measure of Damages 

If A, on January 1, contracts to sell his farm to B for $10,000, settlement to be made June 1; and if B refuses to perform the contract, and the farm is now worth less than $10,000, how may A enforce the contract?

In the first place, he must be able to show that he, himself, was not in default. He does this by showing that, on June 1, he tendered to B a warranty deed to the farm, or that he would have done so, had not B already repudiated the transaction. If it were B suing, instead of 

A, B would have to show that, on June 1, he tendered the $10,000, or that he would have done so, had not A already told him that he would not convey.

Assuming that A proves to the jury that he was not in default and that B was in default, how much is A entitled to recover? The difference between the actual value of the land-say $9000—and the purchase price of $10,000; this would be $1000, which is usually referred to as the loss of the bargain.

If the land is now worth $12,000, and it is B who is suing, and B shows that he, himself, is not in default, and that A is in default, he should logically be entitled to a verdict and judgment for $12,000 less $10,000, or $2,000, the loss of the bargain, and this is the rule of most states. But, in England, where land titles are centuries old and frequently involved, and A's default is innocent (that is, he is merely unable to make out a good title), B is entitled to a verdict and judgment merely for the amount of expense he has incurred in connection with the transaction. This English rule is followed in New 

York and a few other states. 

3. Enforcing the Contract in Equity-Defenses 

If either A or B wishes to have the contract performed specifically, he must file his bill in equity, asking for specific performance. A need not tender a deed or, if B is suing, B need not tender the purchase money, because an equity court can make a conditional decree that B pay the purchase money upon receiving the conveyance from A, or, if B is suing, that A deliver the conveyance upon receiving the purchase price from B.

To a suit for specific performance, the defendant may set up several defenses. Suppose that A, the vendor, sues and B insists that the land is worth far less than the purchase price of $10,000for example, that it is worth only $2,000. While mere inadequacy of con- 66

sideration is no defense at all to an action at law for damages, it may be a defense to a suit in equity for specific performance, if combined with some evidence of fraud or unfair conduct on the part of the plaintiff; and if the inadequacy is so extreme as to "shock" the chancellor's conscience-for example, $2,000 as against $10,000that may be enough in itself to deny specific performance. Though, theoretically, in such a case, A is entitled to a verdict and judgment at law for $8,000, a jury is not likely to give the full amount. 

If there was no consideration at all-if for example, A promised under seal to convey the land to B as a gift-B could in some states recover at law, where a promise under seal is valid without any consideration, but equity will not grant specific performance to B. 

Even though A's title to the property is one which a trained lawyer or a court would regard as good, B will not be compelled to accept it, if the average layman would regard it as doubtful, unless the court has all the parties before it and is able to remove the doubt. In other words, B, the purchaser, is entitled to insist upon a marketable title one which he can easily dispose of. It is no objection, that 

A acquired the title by adverse possession for the period of the Statute of Limitations-usually twenty or fifteen years. 

Although the fact that the contract to sell was oral is ordinarily a defense to a suit for specific performance, yet if, under the oral contract, B has gone into possession and made valuable permanent improvements, the contract is usually specifically enforceable by either A or B; in some states, merely going into possession is enough. 

But mere payment of the purchase price is not enough. 

If there were no contract to sell, but merely a promise to make a gift of the land to B, and B thereunder goes into possession and makes valuable improvements, B is entitled to get the land; but merely going into possession is not enough. 

If B has merely bargained for an option to buy the land—say, within sixty days-and fails to accept within that time, he cannot get the land, because there is no contract to enforce. But if there is a contract to sell, a delay of weeks, or even months, is no defense unless the property has increased substantially in value, and the delay is the fault of B, the buyer, in which case an equity court may deny him specific performance.

If, after making the contract, B first learns that there is a defect in A's title, he is justified, after waiting for a reasonable time for A to remedy the defect, in repudiating the contract.

Where, after making the contract, B, the buyer, becomes insolvent; that is, his obligations become greater than his assets, he is 67

not entitled to insist upon the credit provided for in the contract; he must pay cash.

Since specific performance is regarded as an extraordinary remedy, either party may be barred by conduct not amounting to fraudfor example, by innocent misrepresentation-where enforcing the contract specifically might work a grave hardship on the other party.

Suppose that B learns that there is oil under A's land, and then contracts to buy it from A without disclosing the fact. Is B entitled to specific performance? Yes, unless B stood in a fiduciary position toward A-such as agent, trustee, etc.; or if B was an oil land expert and A was an ignorant, unlearned man. 

Suppose that, at the time of seeking to have the contract specifically enforced, the plaintiff—whether seller or buyer is under age, will equity give specific performance? No, because the court could not prevent the minor from upsetting the transaction because of his minority. Since an equity court cannot protect the other party against the result of such action, it will not give specific performance against him. If action is brought after the minor becomes of age, the defense, of course, cannot be used. Of course, if the plaintiff minor sues at common law for damages, he is entitled to recover; see post, lesson 17.

Suppose that A, the seller, thought he had 80 acres in the farm he contracted to sell to B, but a survey shows that there are only 75 acres. May he have specific performance? Yes, a deduction being made from the selling price for the deficiency. This is called the doctrine of partial performance with compensation. And B may insist upon specific performance with compensation, even in cases where the deficiency is very great-for example, where A thought he owned all the land but it was found that he had only a half-interest. These latter cases have been criticized because, in such a case, A might not have sold at all if he had known he had only a half-interest. When the defect is of such a nature that it is difficult to estimate the proper compensation to be made to B-for example, an inchoate or contingent dower right held by A's wife who refuses to release such right-the better view is that courts should refuse to allow any compensation to B; if he wants the land he must pay the full price. 

4. The Buyer's Property Right in the Land 

From the moment a contract is entered into, which equity would enfor e on the part of both seller and buyer, the buyer is treated as the equitable owner of the land, with the right to become the legal 68

owner upon paying or securing the price, while the seller is treated as holding the title merely as security for the payment of the purchase price.

Hence if A, the seller, should, in violation of his agreement with B, transfer the title to X, B may insist upon getting the title from X, unless X paid full value for the land without notice of B's property right therein. So, if A should die, B may enforce his right against 

A's heirs.

If A should become bankrupt, his assignee in bankruptcy takes subject to B's property right; B may insist upon having the land specifically, and need not come in with A's general creditor's; and 

A's assignees in bankruptcy may enforce the contract against B if they so desire.

But if it is B who becomes bankrupt, A cannot enforce specific performance against B's assignees in bankruptcy, because A's right is not to any specific property, but merely to a sum of money—the purchase price-out of B's general assets. And although A's remedy at law-to get a judgment for the amount of the loss of the bargain -is not adequate, it would be too great a hardship to throw upon B's assignee in bankruptcy the burden of getting rid of the property for the benefit of B's creditors. But, although B's assignees in bankruptcy cannot be compelled to accept the property and pay the purchase price, they may, if they prefer, enforce specific performance against A, where the property has go greatly increased in value that they can sell at a profit for the benefit of B's general creditors. 

If A, on January 2, contracts to sell his house and lot to B for $10,000, settlement to be made on May 1, and if on February 15, the house burns, must B pay the $10,000 for the lot without the house? 

In most states he must do so unless there is a provision in the contract that the risk of loss by fire shall remain on A until the transfer of legal title. A careful realtor or conveyencer will see to it that such a stipulation is made in the contract, because, although B has an insurable interest in the house from the date of the contract, he is not likely to insure, because it is the popular understanding that risk goes with the legal title. In some states, if there is no such stipulation, B is allowed to receive from A whatever insurance money A may recover.

If B is slow in coming to a settlement with A, and A wishes prompt action from B, either in making the settlement or in rescinding the contract, A may serve notice on B to make settlement within some specified time-for example, 60 or 90 days. If B fails to comply with the notice, and the court decides that the period allowed is 69

under all the circumstances, reasonable, A will be free from the contract and B's property right will be at an end. Or, A may appeal to a court of equity to fix a date within which B must make the settlement or have his property right in the land foreclosed and destroyed. 

5. Other Contracts-Contracts in Consideration of Marriage 

As stated above, by the statute of frauds, a promise in consideration of marriage-for example, a promise to pay the intended wife or husband $10,000-is not enforceable unless there is a memorandum in writing signed by the party to be charged in the particular litigation, here the promisor who is being sued. 

The statute of frauds does not apply to mutual promises to marry. But in New York and a few other states there are recent statutes abolishing causes of action for breach of promise to marry and denying relief in the state courts for breach of promise actions arising outside the state. The reason for passing these statutes was that many actions were being brought for purpose of blackmail where no contract to marry had ever been made. 

6. Same-Contracts Not to Be Performed Within a Year 

The statutory requirement of a memorandum in case of agreements not to be performed within a year applies only if the contract cannot be performed within that period. A contract by A to support 

B for the rest of B's life is valid though it is oral, because B may die during the first year and thus the contract might be completely preformed within the year. On the other hand, an agreement by A to support B for two years is not enforceable without a memorandum because, although B may die during the first year, the contract would not be completely performed since the contract was to support for two years. However, in such a case, even if there had been a memorandum A has no cause of action against B's estate because B's death is a valid excuse to both parties to the contract. 

7. Same-Contracts for the Sale of Goods, Wares and 

Merchandise to the Amount of £10 or Upwards

A contract for the sale of goods, wares and merchandise-which covers all personal property-to the amount of £10 or upwards differs from the other contracts covered by the statute of frauds in that no memorandum is required if the buyer accepts or receives a part or all of the goods or pays a part or all of the purchase money or gives something in earnest to bind the bargain. The custom of 70

giving a very small amount to bind the bargain has fallen into disuse. If at the time the contract is made the buyer pays anything it is considered to be a part of the purchase price. In most states the limit of value fixed is $50, but the amount varies from $33 to $200 and in one or two states this particular provision of the statute of frauds as to contracts to sell personal property-has been omitted. 

If A makes a valid contract to sell a chattel to B and later B refuses to accept the chattel or if A later refuses to let B have the chattel, a cause of action arises by which the defaulting party becomes liable to the other party in damages for the loss of the bargain, which the difference between the contract price and the market value on the day set for delivery. If the personal property bargained for is unique-for example, an heirloom, or shares in a corporation which cannot be bought in the open market-and A refuses to perform, B may obtain an equitable decree that A give up the property upon B's paying the purchase price. 

If at the time A made the contract to sell to B the personal property is ready for delivery, title passes to B at once unless the contract shows that title was not meant to pass at that time. Where title thus passes at once to B, A can compel him to pay the full purchase price therefor. Or, if A should refuse to let B have the property B may, upon payment or tender of the price get the property by an action of replevin. Even if title does pass to B he is not entitled to the property until he pays for it, unless A has given him credit, A otherwise having the right to hold the property as security for the purchase price; such a right is called a vendor's lien. 71

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 1) 

ADAMS And Other v. LINDSELL and Another 

In the King's Bench, June 5, 1818 Reported in 1 Barnewall & Alderson, 681 Action for non-delivery of wool according to agreement. At the trial at the last Lent Assizes for the county of Worcester, before BURROUGH, J., it appeared that the defendants, who were dealers in wool at St. Ives, in the county of Huntingdon, had on Tuesday, the 2d day of September, 1817, written the following letter to the plaintiffs who were woolen manufacturers residing in Bromsgrove, Worcestershire: "We now offer you eight hundred tods of wether fleeces, of a good fair quality of our country wool, at 35s. 

6d. per tod, to be delivered at Leicester, and to be paid for by two months' bill in two months and to be weighed up by your agent within fourteen days, receiving your answer in course of post."

This letter was misdirected by the defendants to Bromsgrove, Leicestershire, in consequence of which it was not received by the plaintiffs in Worcestershire till 7 p.m. on Friday, September 5th. On that evening the plaintiffs wrote an answer, agreeing to accept the wool on the terms proposed. 

The course of the post between St. Ives and Brosgrove is through London, and consequently this answer was not received by the defendants till Tuesday, September 9th. On the Monday September 8th, the defendants, not having as they expected, received an answer on Sunday, September 7th (which, in case their letter had not been misdirected, would have been in the usual course of the post) sold the wool in question to another person. Under these circumstances the learned Judge held that, the delay having been occasioned by the neglect of the defendants, the jury must take it that the answer did come back in due course of post; and that then the defendants were liable for the loss that had been sustained; and the plaintiffs accordingly recovered a verdict.

Jervis having in Easter Term obtained a rule nisi for a new trial, on the ground that there was no binding contract between the parties. 

Dauncey, Puller, and Richardson showed cause. They contended that, at the moment of the acceptance of the offer of the defendants by the plaintiffs, the former became bound. And that was on Friday evening, when there had been no change of circumstances. They were then stopped by the Court, who called upon

Jervis and Campbell in support of the rule. They relied on Payne v. Cave, and more particularly on Cooke v. Oxley. In that case, Oxley, who had proposed to sell goods to Cooke, and given him a certain time, at his request, to determine whether he would buy them or not, was held not liable to the performance of the contract, even though Cooke, within the specified time, had determined to buy them, and given Oxley notice to that effect. So here the defendants who have proposed by letter to sell this wool, are not to be held liable, even though it be now admitted that the answer did come back in due course of post. Till the plaintiffs' answer was actually received, there could be no binding contract between the parties; and before then the de- 72

fendants had retracted their offer by selling the wool to another party. But 

THE COURT said, that if that were so, no contract could ever be completed by the post. For if the defendants were not bound by their offer when accepted by the plaintiffs till the answer was received, then the plaintiffs ought not to be bound till after they had received the notification that the defendants had received their answer and assented to it. And so it might go and ad infinitum.
