5. Curtesy and Dower

At common law, if H survived W, he was entitled to a life estate in all W's real property, provided that there was a valid marriage, that W was the owner of the property during the marriage, and that issue was born alive capable of inheriting; this right was called curtesy and it existed as to all estates of inheritance—that is, fee simple or fee tail-and was later extended to real property in which W had only a trust or other equitable interest. By statute, in many states curtesy has been either seriously modified or abolished, H being given a specific interest in W's land as a statutory substitute therefor.

At common law, if W survived H she was given a life interest in one-third of H's real property, provided that there was a valid marriage and that H owned the property during the marriage. By statute in many states W has been given dower also in land in which H had only a trust or other equitable interest. 

At common law, W's right to dower could not be defeated by a conveyance by H but W might release her inchoate dower right by joining with H in his conveyance, to X, or by releasing to X by a later conveyance to him. 102

Where H has made a provision for W by will she is usually held to be entitled to that, in addition to dower, unless H has made it clear that she must elect between them.

Like curtesy, dower has been either seriously modified or abolished by statutes which make other provisions for W. 

6. Future Estates 

If A, having a fee simple estate, conveys anything less than that; for example, a term for years to B, or a life estate to B, or a succession of life estates to B, C and D, what A has left is called a reversion.

If A conveys to B for life, and then to C in fee, C has a vested remainder; it is regarded as vested because C is in existence ready to take, and his estate will come into possession at once upon the death of B, an event that is certain to happen at some time. 

If A conveys to B for life, remainder to the heirs of C who is now alive, the remainder is not vested but contingent, because the heirs of C cannot be determined until C's death; the children of a living person are not his heirs but only his heirs apparent. 

If A conveys to B for life, with remainder in fee to those of C's children who may survive C, and if at that time C has two children and later has three more, the remainder to the three unborn children is contingent upon their coming into existence and surviving C, but the estate to the two children is held to be at once a vested remainder subject to its being divested by the birth of other children and also by failure to survive C.

Suppose Jones has accumulated a considerable fortune; he does not have much confidence in his children and therefore wishes to create contingent estates in his grandchildren still unborn; how long may such a contingency be postponed? The rule-usually called the rule against perpetuities, but better described as the rule against the remoteness of the vesting of future interests in property-is usually stated as follows: "No interest is good unless it must vest, if at all, not later than twenty-one years after some life in being at the creation of the interest." Thus, a devise by A to all his descendants who may be born within twenty-one years after his death, is valid. And when A devises land to B for life, remainder to such children of B as shall be living at B's death who shall attain twenty-five, and then B dies before A, leaving children all of whom are over four years old, the gift to the children is valid, because they all must attain twenty-five years of age within twenty-one years after A's death. 103

So, where A died in 1926 having made a bequest to "My descendants who shall be living twenty-one years after the death of all lineal descendants of Queen Victoria now living" the bequest was held valid, though at that time there were over a hundred such descendants.

On the other hand, where A devised property to B "from and after the date of the probate of this will," the gift was held bad because there was no certainty that the will would be probated within twenty-one years after A's death. If A makes a devise or bequest to the first son of B to become a clergyman, or-if no son of B shall become a clergyman-then over to C, the gift to C is bad; because, if B should have sons, it might not be known until the death of the last surviving son, whether or not one of them might become a clergyman-an event which might happen more than twenty-one years after B's death.

Vested interests, whether remainders or reversions, are not affected by the rule, even though the event upon which an interest may come into possession may be very far in the future-for example, a reversion or a remainder after an estate for ninety-nine years, or, where A devises to B, a bachelor, remainder to his children as tenants in common for life, remainder to C in fee, the remainder to C is vested in interest at once, though it may not come into possession till more than twenty-one years after B's death. 104

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 1) 

MCCULLOCH v. HOLMES 

Supreme Court of Missouri, 1892, 11 Mo. 445.

BARCLAY, J. This is an action of ejectment involving the title to a piece of land in Lincoln county.

The facts are admitted. 

The case turns upon the effect to be given to the following clause in a deed from Oliver Holmes (the common source of title) and his wife to Azra A. 

Holmes, dated, April 11, 1855, and duly recorded about the same time, viz.: "To have and to hold *** unto him the said Azra A. Holmes for and during his natural life and then to his two children, Laura Eliza and Mary Emily Holmes, and their heirs and assigns forever, and if either of said children shall die without issue in the lifetime of their father, then all of said lands is to go to the survivor."

The chronology of the principal facts, affecting the controversy is as follows: 

First. Mary Emily Holmes died without issue, some years before Azra A. Holmes. 

Second. Azra died in April, 1888.

Third. He left his widow, the defendant in possession, and his daughter, Laura Eliza, one of the plaintiffs, who has intermarried with Mr. McCulloch, the other plaintiff.

The fact that the limitation above quoted appears only in the "habendum” clause of the conveyance to Azra A. Holmes does not deprive it of its legal force or effect. All parts of the deed should be considered in gathering its meaning, and the true intent it designs to express, through out, should be effectuated.

In the premises (the first part) of this deed "A. A. Holmes" is named as grantee, while in the habendum the extent of his estate is defined, and the remainder now in consideration carved out. 

All parts of an instrument are to be construed as consistent with each other, if such construction is possible. 

Where land is conveyed to an individual, without adding to his name, as grantee, the word "heirs" or other words of inheritance, the fee thereby passes to him under the law of Missouri, "unless the intent to pass a less estate shall expressly appear or be necessarily implied in the terms of the grant." Rev. St. 1889 par. 8834. The intent to pass a less estate is very evident in the present case, and is lawfully expressed. * * *

Even under the strictest common-law rules of conveyancing, a stranger to those mentioned in the premises of a deed might be introduced in the habendum as a grantee in remainder. 1 Wood on Conveyancing (6 Eng. Ed.) Habendum (B), p. 336.

The limitation in the case at bar is not uncertain or obscure. Azra took a life estate in possession. During its currency, one of the possible remaindermen died. The other, the present plaintiff, survived Azra. She is plainly entitled to the estate, the contingency having been resolved in her favor. The 105

trial court so held. Its judgment is affirmed. SHERWOOD, C. J. and BLACK 

AND BRACE, JJ., concur. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 170, par. 1. (Par. 2) 

WATSON v. WATSON 

Supreme Court of Errors of Connecticut, 1839, 13 Conn. 83.

This is an action of ejectment tried at Hartford, September term, 1838, before Bissell, J.

In the life-time of Ann Watson, and until her death, the demanded premises were owned by her in fee; and the plaintiffs are her children and heirs at law, by John Watson, to whom she was lawfully married, and who is still living. The plaintiffs claimed, that John Watson had not an estate by the curtesy in the premises; and to establish this point, they offered in evidence the following writing, under his hand and seal dated the 23rd of February, 1837, after the death of his wife: "Know all men, by these presents, that I, John 

Watson, do hereby publish, declare and make known, to all whom it may concern, and especially the heirs and children of my late wife, Ann Watson, that I have not, at any time hitherto, and now do not claim, demand, possess, or in any manner or to any extent whatever, have, or pretend to have, any right, title or interest in three pieces of land (describing the premises) but do now fully, absolutely and without any reservation, disclaim and reject any and all right, title and interest in the same, which I might or could have had, by operation of law or otherwise, by reason of my surviving my said wife, or any title to said premises which she had during her life." This writing was signed and sealed by John Watson, attested by two witnesses, acknowledged before a justice of the peace, and recorded in the town records. It was admitted by the plaintiffs, that John Watson was tenant by the curtesy of the demanded premises, and that they could not recover in their action, unless by operation of this writing, he had no such estate. The defendent objected to the admission of it in evidence to the jury; and the court rejected it; and a verdict passed for the defendant. The plaintiffs thereupon moved for a new trial. 

WAITE, J. The object of a disclaimer, is, to prevent an estate passing from the grantor to the grantee. It is a formal mode of expressing the grantee's dissent to the conveyance before the title has become vested in him. In some cases, it may be highly proper; as where a deed is made conveying an estate to one for life, with a remainder to another in fee. Here, in the absence of all evidence to the contrary, the law would presume the assent of the grantee in remainder, upon delivery of the deed to the grantee for life, for the benefit of both. But if the remainder-man chooses not to take the estate, he may disclaim, and thereby remove all presumption of assent. So, where a deed is executed to several persons, and delivered to one for the benefit of all, if one dissents, he may disclaim, and furnish evidence that his share still remains in the grantor. Treadwell et al. v. Bulkley et al., 4 Day, 395.

But if the grantee once assents, and the title thereby becomes vested in him, he cannot, by any disclaimer, revest the estate in the grantor. 

For if he could, the disclaimer would have the effect of a deed, which it cannot have; the object of the latter being to transfer property, of the former to prevent a transfer.

But in a case of descent, the heir cannot, by any disclaimer, prevent the 106

estate from passing to him. It vests in him immediately upon the death of the ancestor; and no act of his is required to perfect his title. He cannot, by any act, cause the estate to remain in the ancestor; for the latter is incapable of holding it, after his death. Nor can he, by a disclaimer, transfer the estate to any other person, as the heir of the ancestor; for, as has already been observed the object of a disclaimer is not to convey, but to prevent a conveyance. 

He is, therefore, in the same situation, upon the death of the ancestor as a purchaser, who has assented to the conveyance. In both cases, a transfer can only be made by some instrument adapted to the conveyance of real estate. 

A devisee, however, stands in the same situation as a purchaser. If he dissents, the estate passes to the heir, in the same manner as if no will had been made. It is entirely optional with him to take or refuse the estate devised. Townson v. Tickell et al., 3 Barn. & Ald. 31. 

In the present case, the disclaimer was made by one who was entitled to the property as tenant by the curtesy. Is he, in this respect, like a grantee, or an heir? This species of estate has sometimes been classed with those acquired by purchase. But it is rather an estate thrown upon the tenant by operation of law. Co. Litt. 18b. It partakes more of the character of an estate acquired by descent than by purchase. Immediately upon the death of the wife, an estate vests in him. Like the heir, he cannot, by refusing to take it, cause it to remain in the wife; nor can he by a disclaimer, transfer it to others. The estate thus vested in him, becomes immediately liable for his debts; and he cannot, by any refusal to take the property, defeat the claims of his creditors.

The disclaimer cffered in evidence could have no effect in showing a title in the plaintiffs; and was properly rejected by the court. 

We, are therefore, satisfied, that no new trial should be granted. 

New trial not to be granted. (Par. 2) 

RIGHT d. FLOWER v. DARBY et al. 

Court of King's Bench 1786, 1 Term Rep. 159.

Ejectment tried at the last assizes at Salisbury, before Hotham Baron, when a verdict was found for the plaintiff, subject to the opinion of the 

Court of King's Bench on the following case:

That the lessor of the plaintiff was seised in fee of the premises in question. 

That on the 11th day of May, 1781, the defendant Darby took the premises, which are a house in Salisbury, and occupied them as a public-house from that time under a parol demise at 10 pounds per annum; the rent to commence from Midsummer then next following. The defendant Darby let part of the premises to the defendant Bristow. That on the 26th of March, 1785, the defendant Darby was served with a notice to quit on the 29th of 

September following:

The question is, whether the lessor of the plaintiff is entitled to recover? 

LORD MANSFIELD, C. J. When a lease is determinable on a certain event, or at a particular period, no notice to quit is necessary, because both parties are equally apprized of the determination of the term. 

If there be a lease for a year, and by consent of both parties the tenant continue in possesssion afterwards, the law implies a tacit renovation of the contract. They are supposed to have renewed the old agreement, which was to hold for a year. But then it is necessary for the sake of convenience, 107

that, if either party should be inclined to change his mind, he should give the other half a year's notice before the expiration of the next or any following year: now this is a notice to quit in the middle of the year, and therefore not binding, as it is contrary to the agreement. 

As to the case of lodgings, that depends on a particular contract, and is an exception to the general rule. The agreement between the parties may be for a month or less time, and there to be sure much shorter notice would be sufficient, where the tenant has held over the time agreed upon, than in the other case. The whole question depends upon the nature of the first contract.

ASHURST, J. There is no distinction in reason between houses and lands, as to the time of giving notice to quit. It is necessary that both should be governed by one rule. There may be cases where the same hardship would be felt in determining that the rule did not extend to houses as well as lands; as in the case of a lodging-house in London, being let to a tenant at Lady-Day to hold as in the present case: if the landlord should give notice to quit at Michaelmas, he would by that means deprive the lessee of the most beneficial part of the term, since it is notorious that the winter is by far the most profitable season of the year for those who let lodgings.. 

BULLER, J. It is taken for granted by the counsel for the plaintiff, that the rule of law, which construes what was formerly a tenancy at will of lands into a tenancy from year to year, does not apply to the case of houses, but there is no ground for that distinction. The reason of it is, that the agreement is a letting for a year at an annual rent; then if the parties consent to go on after that time, it is a letting from year to year. This reason extends equally to the present case; an annual rent is here reserved; and upon such a holding it has been determined that half a year's notice to quit is necessary. This doctrine was laid down as early as in the reign of Henry the Eighth. The moment the year began, the defendant had a right to hold to the end of that year; therefore there should have been a half year's notice to quit before the end of the term. 

Judgment for the defendant. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 172, par. 8. (Par. 2)

KING v. DURKEE-ARTWOOD CO. 

Supreme Court of Minnesota, 1914. 126 Minn. 452.

BUNN, J. Defendant was a tenant of plaintiff under a lease from month to month, the leased premises being a store in Minneapolis and a flat above it. 

The rent was payable monthly in advance. April 4, 1913, defendant caused to be served on plaintiff a notice of the termination of the tenancy of May 31st. 

Defendant did not, however, vacate the premises on the date named, but continued in possession until June 30th, when it ceased to occupy the premises for any purpose, and delivered the keys to plaintiff. The holding over was with the knowledge and consent of plaintiff, though there appears to have been no agreement as to the terms upon which the tenant held over. Apparently it was understood that the tenant was to move to new quarters as soon as they were ready, and that it was not the intention of defendant to continue its occupancy of the leased premises beyond the month of June. 

This action was brought to recover the rent of the store for the months 108

of June, July, and August, and of the flat for the months of June and July. 

Plaintiff was unable to rent the store in July or August, but occupied the flat the latter month. Defendant admitted its liability for the month of June, during which it occupied the premises, but claimed that it was not liable for the July and August rent. The trial was to a jury. The court instructed that the tenant's holding over after May 31st made the notice a nullity, that the case was as if no notice had been given, and that defendant was liable for the July and August rent. The verdict was in accord with this instruction. 

Defendant moved for a new trial, and appealed from an order denying such motion.

The decision here depends upon whether the instruction of the court to the jury, above referred to, is a correct statement of the law as applied to the facts of the case. 

1. If the notice was rendered a nullity by the tenant's holding over, it must be on the ground of waiver. It is clear, in this state at least, that a notice by the landlord to a tenant to quit may be waived by the landlord, and that such notice is thenceforth inoperative. ***

It is doubtless true that a tenant may withdraw or waive a notice to quit given by him. And if he does waive it, it is plainly correct that the situation is as if no notice had been given. Clearly, notice was necessary in order to terminate this tenancy at will or from month to month. 

Did the tenant, by holding over after the time named in that notice, waive or withdraw such notice? There are some statements in textbooks, and at least one reported case, that lends support to the view that such holding over is only presumptive evidence of waiver, and does not operate as a matter of law to continue the tenancy. In Cyc. the law is thus stated: "No continuance of the tenancy is necessarily implied from the mere fact of a tenant continuing in possession after the expiration of a notice to quit given by such tenant. It is for the jury to decide whether or not the tenant, by remaining in possession, intends to waive the notice and continue the tenancy." 

24 Cyc. 1336.

But one case is cited as authority for the text, and that is Jones v. Sheares, 4 Ad. & El. 832. This case was determined by the court of King's bench in 

1836. The lessee of the coal under certain lands gave a notice, as under the lease he had a right to do, that at the end of two years he would deliver possession and end the term. He continued for two months after the two years to work the coal, and it was claimed by the lessor that the notice was thereby waived. The lessee insisted that the working was not carried on with any view of continuing the tenancy, that the coal mined was taken from the pillars which supported the roof, and that this was customary on leaving a mine. It was held that the question whether the lessee intended to waive the notice and continue the tenancy was for the jury. Mr. Tiffany states that a tenant holding over after the time named in his notice of intention to quit is liable in use and occupation as a tenant holding over, but that such retention of possession does not necessarily operate as a waiver or withdrawal of the notice, so as to effect a continuance of the former tenancy. 

2 Tiffany, Landl. & T. 1464.

But the decided weight of authority is that a tenant who holds over the expiration of his term may be held liable as tenant for a further period without reference to his actual wishes on the subject, and necessarily without reference to his intention to become a tenant for a further term. * * 109

It would seem to be immaterial whether the term expires because of the termination of the period named in the lease, or by a notice tɔ quit, given where the lease requires it or where the tenancy is at will. * * * The doctrine is that the landlord has the option to hold the tenant for another period, and the tenant has nothing to say about it. The rule is perhaps a harsh one, but it is too well settled generally and by our own decisions to justify departing from it.

The length of the term for which the tenant will be held depends in the absence of statute, on the character of his prior tenancy. In the case of a prior tenancy from month to month, such as was the tenancy in the case at bar, the tenant, on the election of the landlord to so treat him, becomes or remains a tenant from month to month. And this would be so as to urban property in case of a holding over without any new agreement after the termination of a lease for a definite time. 

We are forced to the conclusion that defendant, by holding over after the time specified for the termination of the tenancy, waived the notice given by it. The trial court was therefore correct in instructing the jury that the notice was a nullity, and that defendant would be held just the same as though such notice had never been given. 

2. It follows that notice was necessary in order to terminate this tenancy. 

It is admitted that no such notice was given. Defendant relies on the statute, before cited (Gen. St. 1913, para. 6812), in support of its claim that it is liable only for one month's rent. But the cases of Stees v. Bergmeier, 91 

Minn. 513, 98 N. W. 648, Quade v. Fitzloff, and Slafter v. Siddell, supra, are conclusive that this statute affords defendant no relief. It was enacted to do away with the harsh rule whereby, at the option of the landlord, a tenant under a lease for a definite term who remained in possession after the termination of his lease was bound for another term on the same conditions. If the statute applies at all to a holding over after the termination by notice of a tenancy at will or from month to month, it cannot be construed as making a new tenancy for a single month. In Slafter v. Siddell, the lease was for a year, and the tenant held over without any new agreement. The statute was applied, and it was held that the tenancy was from month to month. Clearly, when the original tenancy is one from month to month, and the tenant has the right to give a month's notice and relieve himself from further liability, the statute was not intended to, and does not, convert such tenancy into one for a month only. Indeed, when we have said that the notice was a nullity, it is equivalent to saying that the tenancy at will was not terminated. It remained in force until the tenant quit after the expiration of the time named in a new notice. 

It follows that the trial court was correct in holding that the tenancy had not terminated, and that defendant was liable for the July and August rent. Order affirmed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 171, par. 6. (Par. 3) 

MUSTAIN v. GARDNER 

Supreme Court of Illinois, 1903. 203 Ill. 284.

BOGGS, J. The appellants are the heirs-at-law of one Ola I. Mustain, who departed this life on the 11th day of December, 1900. They filed a bill 110

in equity in the circuit court of McDonough county against Sarah A. Gardner and Charles H. Gardner, her husband, for the partition of lots 3 and 4 in block 5, in Davis' addition to the village of Blandinsville. The title to the said lots was in one John T. Mustain at the time of his death. He left a will containing but two clauses, which are as follows: "First-It is my will that my funeral expenses and all of my just debts be fully paid. "Second-To my beloved daughter, Ola I., and my beloved wife, Sarah A.

Mustain, jointly, I give, devise, and bequeath lots three (3) and four (4) in block five (5), in Davis' second addition to the late town (now village) of Blandinsville, to them and to their heirs and assigns forever.” 

The chancellor construed the second clause cf the will to devise the premises to the devisees therein named as joint tenants, and held that upon the death of the said Ola I. Mustain the title thereto inured as an entirety to the said Sarah A. Gardner, nee Mustain, as the surviving joint tenant, and sustained a demurrer to the bill and dismissed the case. 

The only question presented by this record is whether the devisees took as joint tenants or as tenants in common. Joint tenancies are looked upon with disfavor in this State. For this reason section 5 of the act concerning conveyances was adopted. This section reads as follows: "No estate in joint tenancy in any lands, tenements or hereditaments, shall be held or claimed under any grant, devise or conveyance whatsoever, heretofore or hereafter made, other than to executors and trustees, unless the premises therein mentioned shall expressly be thereby declared to pass, not in tenancy in common, but in joint tenancy; and every such estate, other than to executors and trustees, (unless otherwise expressly declared as aforesaid,) shall be deemed to be in tenancy in common." The devise does not expressly declare that the estate thereby created and devised is an estate in joint tenancy and not an estate in common. But such a declaration is not indispensable. It is enough if it shall appear from the phraseology of the devise that the testator understood the nature and incidents of the two estates, and the language employed be such as to clearly and explicitly show that the premises are not to pass in tenancy in common. *** In the absence of an affimative declaration that the estate devised is in joint tenancy, an estate in tenancy in common will be devised, unless it clearly and explicitly appears from the language employed that the testator understood the nature and incidents of the different estates and intended to create a joint tenancy. The quality of survivorship is the distinguishing feature of a joint tenancy, and where the grant or devise expressly imparts that quality to the estate, as did the deed under consideration in Slater v. Gruger, supra, it will be deemed effectual to create a joint tenancy, though the negation indicated by the statute be omitted. 

The devise under consideration does not, in terms, negative the presumption which arises from the statute that it was the intention of the testator to create an estate in tenancy in common, and does not, in terms, declare it to be the intent to create a joint tenancy; nor do we find anything in the language of the devise to indicate that the testator understood the nature and incidents of the different estates, or either of them, and desired that an estate having the peculiar characteristic of survivorship should pass by the devise. The word "jointly," found in the devise, cannot be accepted as sufficient to show, clearly and explicitly, that the testator intended that the estate devised should possess the attribute of survivorship. Tenants in com- 111

mon or coparceners hold the estate “jointly” until a severance is effected. *** It is entirely consistent with the use made by the testator of this word "jointly" to construe it as indicating only an intent to devise the estate to both devisees, and as it cannot be construed to declare, explicitly and clearly, the intent that the estate, as an entirety, should inure to the survivor of the devisees, it cannot avail to take the devise out of the operation of the statute. 

The statute must be given effect and the estate devised declared to be an estate in tenancy in common. 

The decree is reversed and the cause remanded to the circuit court, with directions to that court to overrule the demurrer to the bill and require the defendants to plead, answer or demur thereto. Reversed and remanded, with directions. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 175, par. 13. 112

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 6 

Questions 

1. What is meant by a freehold estate? 

2. What was an estate tail and why were such estates created? 

What is their present status? 

3. What are the rights and duties of a tenant for life? 

4. Upon the death of a life tenant of a farm, who is entitled to the crops then growing? 

5. Does a share-cropper have a property right in the land or a mere contract right? A lodger? The lessee of an apartment? 

6. When does a tenancy at will arise? How may it be terminated? 

7. How does a tenancy from year to year arise? How may it be terminated? 

8. How is a joint tenancy created? A tenancy by the entirety? 

A tenancy in common? 

9. What was curtesy? Dower? What has happened to these interests in most states? 

10. How long can the creator of a contingent estate postpone its vesting? 113

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 7 TRUSTS IN LAND 

1. Purposes for Which a Trust May Be Created

Where one has accumulated considerable property but no longer wishes to manage it, he may convey or transfer it to a trustee who will manage it and turn the income from it over to the transferor. 

Or, he may wish to provide that in the case of his death there will be some capable adult who will manage his property for the benefit of his widow and his young children. His property may consist of land or chattels or other personal property, or of a business which has been prosperous; and he may wish-in case of his death-to have the business continued, instead of having it sold. Or, if his children are quite young, he may wish to create a trust, to accumulate until the children shall reach majority. Or, he may create a trust for some charity, such as a school, or library, or hospital or church. The foregoing is not an exhaustive list but covers most of the purposes for which trusts are created. 

2. Origin and History of Uses 

In England during the twelfth century, so much land had been given to monasteries and other religious corporations, and thereby taken off the land market, that, in 1217, Parliament passed the Statute of Mortmain, forbidding such bodies to hold land. The religious corporations at once took steps to evade the statute. This they accomplished by having, in each case, land conveyed to a close friend of the monastery or other religious body, who allowed the religious body to use the land as if it were their own. At first, this obligation on the part of the transferee of the land was merely a moral one; if he decided to use it for himself, he might do so. 

But about 1450 the English equity court compelled him to carry out his undertaking specifically. This compulsion made, of course, 114

his obligation a legally binding one instead of a mere moral obligation as it had been before. The person to whom the land was conveyed was called the feofee to uses-the conveyance being by way of feoffment (pronounced "fefment")-and the beneficiary was called the cestui (sometimes pronounced "cety") que use. This was a convenient term from the Norman French and meant merely, the one for whose use or benefit the land was held—that is, the beneficiary.

Once established uses were employed for many other purposes. 

Since the law of forfeiture of property for treason applied only to the legal title, and not to the equitable use, it became common among the English landed gentry, who were frequently engaged in civil wars, to convey the legal title to some elderly bachelor, or other steady, reliable person, to hold for their use and their families. In that way they avoided the danger of losing their property in case they should be defeated in war and, later, convicted of treason. 

3. Statute of Uses-Trusts 

The wide employment of uses led to many evils, so Parliament, in 1534, passed the Statute of Uses. One might have expected that they would forbid conveyance of land in use, or would have declared uses to be void; but instead, the statute provided that whenever A should hold the legal title for the use or benefit of B, the legal title should henceforth be in B, and not in A. Thus A holds the legal title only for an instant of time and then it passes to B. That was long before the modern recording statutes, so title was merely what Parliament said it was, and by the Statute of Uses Parliament in substance defined title in these cases as being the exclusive right to the use or benefit of the land. The statute was effectual—at least for the time, and so far as it was construed to extend-in preventing the separation of the legal title and the equitable or beneficial interest, but its most important result was its effect on the law of conveyances. To convey a legal title to B, after the statute, it was necessary merely to create a use. This could be done either directly, by a bargain and sale of the use to B or by a feoffment to A for B's use; the statute of uses then operated to vest the legal title in B and this was called "executing the use." 

The statute was construed as not affecting uses in chattels or other personal property, or uses where the conveyance imposed active duties upon the transferees such as managing the property and making investments for the benefit of a third party. Those uses which were not affected by the statute came to be called trusts, 115

in order to distinguish them from the merely passive uses where no active duties were involved, which had become so important in the law of conveyances. 

In 1660, about a century after the Statute of Uses was passed, it was held that if land should be conveyed to A, for the use or benefit of B, for the use or benefit of C, the statute would operate only once-to place the legal title in B-and that the second use, for C, took effect as a trust; and since there were no active duties imposed on B, it was merely a passive trust. Thus B became a passive trustee for C, thereby concealing C's interest in the land. This was a clear case of judge-made law, because, prior to that decision, it had been held that the use for C was void, as repugnant to the use for B. In some states today, there are statutes forbidding the creation of passive trusts in land, thus following out the policy of the original Statute of Uses and preventing the concealment mentioned above. 

4. Creation of Express Trusts-Classification of Trusts 

Express trusts may be created in any one of three ways: (1) A conveys property to B, in trust for A; (2) A conveys property to B, in trust for C; (3) A declares himself trustee of property for C. 

In every express trust there are three elements: a trustee, trust property and a beneficiary or in other words, a cestui que trust (the one for whose use or benefit the land is held). 

According to form, trusts are classified as either express or not express. In the second class there are included both resulting and constructive trusts.

A logical classification of trusts, according to substance or intent, would result in the following divisions: (1) actually intended trusts, whether the intent is expressed or inferred from circumstances (when such an inference is to be drawn will be explained later); (2) trusts-so-called-where the obligation is imposed by equity to prevent unjust enrichment of the defendant, without regard to the intention of the parties, and often contrary to such intent; (3) a middle class, in which the obligation is imposed by equity, according to what would probably have been the intent of the creator of an express trust, if he had thought about the factual situation which later developed unexpectedly. The second class (2) is always called constructive trusts, because they are obligations imposed or constructed by equity without regard to the intentions of the parties. 

The term, resulting trusts, is usually applied to those trusts in which the obligation is imposed according to the real or the supposed 116

probable, unexpressed intention of the parties, thus including class (3) and part of class (1). Resulting and constructive trusts will be discussed in later sections. 

5. Consideration—The Law of Uses 

It was the law of uses, before the Statute of Uses, that consideration-the receipt of some benefit, either money or land-was necessary for the creation of a use. If A conveyed property to B, for the use of C, the receipt of the property by B was consideration for his obligation to hold it for the use of C. If A desired to create a use in 

C, without conveying the legal title, his having the property could not be consideration because he already had the property and, hence, unless A received something of value in exchange for his promise to hold for C's use, such a promise was ineffectual to create a use in C.

After the Statute of Uses was passed, and uses became important in the law of conveyancing, the rule requiring consideration to create a use was so modified that one might create a use in favor of a relative by blood or marriage, by a mere declaration; the Statute of Uses would then operate to transfer the legal title to the relative in whom the use had been created. Such a conveyance is called a covenant to stand seized; the near relationship upon which it was based is usually referred to as "good consideration;" but there is no consideration in the true sense of exchange. Sometimes, consideration in the true sense of exchange is called "valuable" consideration to distinguish it from the so-called "good" consideration. 

Where a use is raised or created by the receipt of a valuable consideration, or by the recital in a deed of a valuable considerationusually one dollar-the conveyance is called a bargain and sale. 

If a deed is made without reciting consideration it is valid if made to a near relative; if not made to a near relative it is not valid unless actually delivered on the land, in which case it operates as a deed of feoffment. 

6. Consideration-The Law of Trusts 

Until 1811 a valuable consideration was as essential to the creation of a trust as it usually was in the creation of a use. In that year it was decided-in a well known English case-that if A declares himself trustee for C, this mere declaration is sufficient to make A trustee and to vest the equitable interest in C, even though C is a stranger; furthermore, it was held that there need be no delivery of the instrument creating the trust.

It has long been the law of gifts, that the donor must either 117

deliver the property—as in the case of gifts of chattels or make a conveyance, by deed or will, in cases of either chattels or land; a mere declaration of gift is not enough. After it was held that a mere declaration of trust was enough to create a trust in either chattels or land, it was natural that the disappointed donee of every imperfect gift would try to induce the court to construe the transaction to be, for most purposes, as satisfactory as a transfer of the legal title. In a few English cases, the disappointed donees were successful; but the better view is that the doctrine is applicable only to cases where there is a clear intent to become a trustee; hence if, for any reason such as lack of delivery, an attempt to make a gift should fail, the attempt cannot take effect as a declaration of trust. 

7. Statute of Frauds 

Unless it is otherwise provided by statute, a trust of either real estate or personal property may be created orally. In a few states, the Statute of Frauds provides that trusts in land shall be created in writing; but, by the English Statute of 1676, which has been copied substantially in many states, the requirement is not that such trust be created in writing, but merely that "all declarations or creations of trust or confidences of any lands, tenements, or hereditaments, shall be manifested and proved by some writing signed by the party who is by law enabled to declare such trusts." It applies to all interests in land, but it does not apply to trusts in chattels; and resulting and constructive trusts are expressly excepted. 

Under the usual statute of frauds, a writing is not necessary to the creation of the trust, but merely to its enforcement. Hence, the trust memorandum is sufficient, if it is made at any time before it is necessary to make proof at the trial; and it is effectual, even if made after the trustee's bankruptcy, if the trust was created before the bankruptcy.

If A conveys land to B, intending it to be in trust for C, A may comply with the statute by expressing the trust in the conveyance, whether by deed or will. If he fails to do this, then B is the only one who can comply with the statute, since he is the party to be charged with the trust. If A declares himself trustee of land for 

C, the only person who can comply with the Statute is A. 

8. The Cestui Que Trust or Beneficiary of the Trust— 

Charitable Trusts

Any person capable of holding the legal title to property may be a cestui que trust of such property-that is, beneficiary of a 118

trust in such property.

A charity is anything which tends to the improvements of mankind in general, such as schools, churches, lodges, hospitals, libraries, etc. The rules as to charitable trusts differ in two important respects from the rules as to private trusts; (1) the beneficiaries need not be definite, and (2) the trust may last forever. A devise of land to 

X upon trust to apply the income each year for the benefit of the worthy poor of Portland, is valid, though the parties to be benefitted are not specified, and though the income is to be applied without any time limit. In fact, it is one of the essential features of a charity that the person or persons to be ultimately benefitted thereby should not be specified, because it must be for the benefit of the public; if, in the above case, A had named a dozen individuals as beneficiaries, it would not have been a charity but a private trust. 

In two other respects, a charitable trust differs from a private trust. Largely because it is doing the work which the State would otherwise be compelled to do, it is not liable for any damage caused by the acts of its employees; but the present tendency is to limit this doctrine so as to bar only the beneficiaries of the charity; so that when a plumber is called in by the manager of a public hospital he has the same rights to recover damages for personal injuries due to the defective condition of the premises as he would have if the hospital were private. If it becomes impossible to carry out the original charitable intent of the creator of the charitable trust, the property will be devoted to a similar charity. 

If a charitable corporation is the immediate beneficiary of the trust, it is the proper party to bring suit against the trustee to enforce the trust; otherwise, the state will enforce it, usually by a suit conducted by the attorney general. 

9. The Cestui Que Trust-Private Trusts 

If a trust is for a private purpose, it is a fatal objection if it is to last indefinitely; for example, a trust to apply the income of a particular fund to the upkeep of a monument would likely be held invalid, apart from statute. If the trust had been limited to the period permitted by the rule against perpetuities-twenty-one years after some life in being, at the creation of the trust-it would probably have been held good. In some states, by statute, perpetual trusts for the upkeep of burial grounds have been made valid. 

The fact that there is no beneficiary capable of enforcing a private trust is not objectionable provided the purpose of the trust is definite; for example, a trust to erect a monument to the first hus- 119

band of the testator's wife was held valid, the trustee being willing to perform the trust; if the trustee had been unwilling, there would have been a resulting trust in favor of the testator's kin, the trust property being distributed just as if there had been no will. But where the object of the trust is indefinite, the trust has been illogically declared invalid, though the trustee is willing to carry it out; for example, a trust "to dispose of the ultimate residue to such objects of benevolence and liberality as the Bishop of Durham shall most approve," was held invalid in a well known English case. The testator in that case might have accomplished the desired result by bequeathing the property to his next of kin subject to a power in the Bishop of Durham to appoint to such objects of benevolence and liberality as he should most approve. 

10. Appointment and Removal of Trustees 

The creator of a trust may validly appoint anyone a trustee (for example, a minor, an insane person, a bankrupt, or an alien); there is no limitation. But a court is likely to remove such a person, if objection is made and incompetency shown. At one time it was thought that a corporation could not be a trustee, because, being impersonal, it had no conscience. Today, however, most trust property is held and administered by corporations, which have the advantage of continued existence so that such trusts are not affected by death as are those held by individual trustees. 

If a trustee dies or if for any other reason a trusteeship becomes vacant, and the instrument creating the trust does not provide for filling the vacancy, the duty of appointing a trustee devolves upon the court. In performing this duty the court will try to appoint one who will have enough business ability to be capable of managing the trust estate and who will be fair to all beneficiaries of the trust. 

Hence a court would not appoint a minor or insane person and would not appoint a non-resident of the state, an insolvent or one of the beneficiaries of the trust, unless there should be unusual circumstances to justify such an appointment. 

If a trustee is guilty of serious misconduct or is for any reason incapable of performing the trust duties, he should obviously be removed. Ordinarily the proper equity court will first appoint a new trustee and then will command the old trustee to convey the trust property to the new appointee. If the trustee should be a minor or insane, a transfer by him would not be binding, even if made by order of the equity court; because any transfer by such incompetents has always been held to be voidable; hence, in such a case the 120

court will not order a transfer, but will issue an order enjoining the trustee's further handling of the trust property, and will appoint a conservator who will manage the property till a valid transfer can be made that is, when the minor becomes an adult or when the insane trustee becomes sane again. In many states, statutes have been passed which enable a court to vest the title in a new trustee without any transfer by the old trustee, so that in those states it is unnecessary to appoint a conservator. 

No one is under any obligation to accept the office of trustee, which may impose heavy duties in some cases. If a conveyance is made to T, in trust for C, the title passes to T as soon as the conveyance is made, without waiting for T's consent, because the conveyance is usually by way of gift to the beneficiaries, and consent is presumed. But if T, upon learning of the conveyance, refuses to accept, such disclaimer places the title back in the transferor or his representative-heir or executor-just as if the conveyance had never been made. But if T once accepts, it is too late to disclaim; if he wishes to be relieved of the trust he must then persuade the proper equity court to accept his resignation, or, if the beneficiaries of the trust are all competent adults, get their consent to his being relieved of the trust. 

Neither disclaimer by T, nor his refusal to act after acceptance, will defeat the interest of the beneficiary of the trust. An equity court will not allow a trust to fail for want of a trustee, but will promptly appoint some one who is willing to serve and carry out the trust. The beneficiary's interest is likewise saved, where the person or corporation is forbidden by law to be a trustee, or where the person named in a will dies before the testator, or where the conveyance is upon trust but no trustee is named. 121

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 3) 

KELLOGG v. HALE 

Illinois Supreme Court, 1880. 

CRAIG, J. * * * The trust here was not in writing. The deed was absolute in terms, and purported to convey the title to Peck, but when the transaction is viewed in the light of the evidence, it appears that the property was conveyed to Peck in trust. He was to hold the title, lease the property, collect the rents, sell or reconvey, or make such disposition of the property as Kellogg might order. Was this such a trust as the statute of uses would execute? The answer to this question may be found in the former decisions of this court.

In Meacham v. Steele, 93 Ill. 146, where a question of this character was under consideration, it is said: "Where the conveyance imposes on the trustee active duties with respect to the trust estate, such as, to sell and convert into money, or to lease the same and collect the rents, issues and profits thereof, and pay them over to the beneficiary, it creates a trust which the statute does not execute." Here Peck, the trustee, had the entire charge of the property. It was his duty duty to rent, and collect the rents, pay the taxes, keep up the repairs, and in addition to this, upon request, sell and convey the property. The facts seem to bring the case directly within the rule announced in the case cited. 

In Kirkland v. Cox, 94 Ill. 400, the effect of the statute of uses was under consideration, and it was held where an estate is conveyed to one person, for the use of, or upon a trust for, another, and nothing more is said, the statute immediately transfers the legal estate to the use, and no trust is created, although express words of trust are used. But this has reference only to passive, simple or dry trusts. In such case the legal estate never vests in the feofee,¹ but is instantaneously transferred to the cestui que use as soon as the use is declared. The facts surrounding the conveyance in this case do not bring the trust within what may be called a passive, simple or dry trust. The duties of the trustee had not been performed under the trust imposed by the deed and contract. Those duties were active, and so continued until the lands were conveyed, under the order and direction of 

Kellogg. Perry on Trusts, para. 395, in speaking in regard to special or active trusts, says: "If any agency, duty or power be imposed on the trustee, as by a limitation to a trustee and his heirs to pay the rents or to convey the estate, or if any control is to be exercised or duty performed by the trustee, *** the operation of the statute is excluded, and the trusts or uses remain mere equitable estates." But the citation of other authorities on the question is useless. We are satisfied that the deed made to Peck passed the title to the property to him, unaffected by the statute of uses. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 266, par. 2. 

1. Perhaps it would be more accurate to say that it vests only instantaneously in the feoffee. 122

(Par. 4) 

DOYLE v. MURPHY 

Illinois Supreme Court, 1859, 22 Ill. 502.

WALKER, J. It is urged, in connection with other grounds for the reversal of the degree of the lower court, that the court had no jurisdiction of the subject matter. While by the defendants in error, it is insisted that Maurice 

Doyle was a trustee, and being such, a court of equity has undoubted jurisdiction over the trust fund. That the court has such a jurisdiction in cases of strict trust, there is no doubt. But it does not therefore follow, that the court will assume jurisdiction in every case where a mere confidence has been reposed, or a credit given. The various affairs of life in almost every act between individuals in trade and commerce, involve the reposing of confidence or trust in each other, and yet it never has been supposed that because such a confidence or trust in the integrity of another has been extended and abused, that therefore, a court of equity would in all cases assume jurisdiction. When one person sells property on credit, or loans money to another, confidence is reposed and a trust is entertained that the money will be paid by the debtor, and yet no case has gone so far as to hold that it was such a trust, as gave to a court of equity jurisdiction under the head of trusts. If this were so, there would be no case where property or money was obtained on a credit, in which the court would not have jurisdiction. 

But on the other hand, when property is conveyed or given by one person to another, to hold for the use of a third person, such a trust is thereby created, as authorized the court to entertain jurisdiction to compel its application to the purposes of the trust. And the property may be pursued into the hands of all persons who have obtained it with notice of the trust, or where it has been converted into money, may be recovered, or where the money arising from the sale of trust property or funds has been invested in other property, a court of equity will compel the trustee to account for the property thus acquired, and treat it in every respect as if it were the original trust property. In this case the bill alleges and one of the complainant swears, that money was delivered to Maurice Doyle to pay certain debts of Catherine Byrne, which he failed to so apply. If he failed to pay this money,' there was such a breach of contract, as would have authorized Catherine Byrne to maintain an action for money had and received, and probably the creditors to whom the money should have been paid might have maintained the action. But according to no rule or adjudged case that we are aware of, was it a trust fund, authorizing Mrs. Byrne, or her representatives to recover as a trust fund. * * 

The decree of the circuit court must be reversed, and the bill dismissed. 

Decree reversed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 267, par. 4. 

ALDRICH v. ALDRICH 

Massachusetts Supreme Court, 1895, 172 Mass. 101.

The bill alleged that the testator died on March 14, 1895, leaving a will by which he gave "all the rest and residue of my estate, after the payment 2. The context shows that the court evidently considered that Maurice Doyle's obligation was not to pay the particular money to the creditors but mer ly to pay them out of his own general assets. The creditor's remedy against Doyle is at common law as beneficiaries of the contract made for their benefit. 123

of debts," to his wife. After appointing her executrix, and requesting that she be exempt from giving sureties on her bond and be not required to file any schedule of property in the probate court, he proceeded, "I give all my estate to my said wife to the end that she may be able to maintain a home for herself, and one where she can receive all our dear children, as we have been accustomed to do during our joint lives. I am confident she will manage with good discretion and fidelity what is committed to her, and that when she shall no longer need the property it will be equally divided among all our children, or their representatives." * * *

MORTON, J. If the testator had intended to create a trust in favor of his children at his wife's death, there can be no doubt that he knew how to do it in clear and unmistakable terms, and it is almost inconceivable that, if such was his purpose, he should have expressed himself in the manner in which he has done. 

There is no doubt that words of recommendation, or of confidence, entreaty, hope, or desire, have been held sufficient under some circumstances to create a trust. But, speaking generally, this was because in such cases such a construction was supposed to carry out the intention of the testator. If an arbitrary rule seems to have been laid down at one time in regard to what would constitute a precatory trust, there can be no doubt, we think, that the tendancy of later decisions has been, if not to relax the rule thus laid down, at least not to extend it. 

In the present case there is what clearly would constitute in law, if it stood alone, an absolute gift of the estate to the wife. Then follows, after one or two intervening clauses, the one on which the plaintiff relies. This was intended by the testator, it seems to us, to express his reason for the gift to his wife and his confidence in her, and not to cut down or affect the absolute character of the gift which he had previously made to her. 

It is true that he says in substance that he expects that the property, when she shall not longer need it, will be divided equally between the children and their representatives. But there is nothing which renders it obligatory on her to do this, and therefore one of the features of a precatory trust is wanting. *** 

The cases which he has cited do not resemble in all respects the one at bar, and there are English and American cases which seem to support the view for which the plaintiff contends. But the question is, whether, taking the will as a whole, it was the intention of the testator to create a trust, and we are of opinion that it was not, and that the construction which we have adopted is in harmony with the more recent English and American cases. 

Bill dismissed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 269, par. 9. (Par. 6)

LEEPER v. TAYLOR 

Missouri Supreme Court, 1892, 11 Mo. 312. 

BLACK, M. * *The defendant's answer admits the execution of the declaration of trust, and its proceeds upon the theory that the instrument was duly delivered to Mr. Lipscomb for the defendant's father and mother. 

It is useless to discuss the question of the execution and delivery of the instrument, for both are admitted by the answer. 124

The point is made, however, that it is void for want of consideration. It may be conceded that a court of equity will not enforce an executory agreement based upon a voluntary consideration. But a settlor possessed of a legal title may create a valid trust therein by a declaration that he holds the title in trust for the other person. A transfer of the title is not necessary. Here the trust was duly declared by an instrument in writing and under seal. It is a perfect, complete trust; and such a trust will be enforced, notwithstanding the consideration is voluntary. * * * If a trust has been completely declared, the absence of a valuable consideration is immaterial. A perfect or complete trust is valid and enforceable, although purely voluntary. But it recites a valuable consideration, and the circumstances show clearly enough that it was executed in consideration of the absolute deed of the premises previously made by the beneficiaries. But, be this as it may, the trust is perfect, and must be enforced, though based upon voluntary consideration only. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 270, par. 11. (Par. 8) 

BARKLEY v. DONNELLY 

Missouri Supreme Court, 1892, 112 Mo. 561.

THOMAS, J. This is a suit in equity by the heirs of Mary A. Troost to recover the beneficial interest in certain property in Kansas City, attempted to be donated by the will of Mrs. Troost to certain religious and charitable objects, and asking a construction of the will for this purpose. 

The petition sets out the will in haec verba (these words) which after devising considerable property to the plaintiffs, and making a few other special bequests, contains the following: 

"Item 18. I give and devise to the city of Kansas the parcel of land * * * containing from five to ten acres * * * called 'Fry Place,' to hold the same in trust, however, for the following uses and none other; for a home and place for the maintenance and education of poor children, and the same shall be called the 'Gillis Orphan Asylum' ***” 

Another contention of plaintiffs is that the trusts upon which the Fry Place, and the lots upon which the Gillis opera house has been built were devised, can not be carried out for two reasons: First, because the beneficiaries thereof are so indefinite and uncertain that they can not be ascertained, there being no power in the city of Kansas or the Trustees named "to determine who are 'poor children,' or to select from the innumerable multitude upon the earth those that shall partake of the testatrix's 'bounty'." 

This proposition can not be maintained. The bequest is for a charity and for charitable uses. The beneficiaries then are 'poor children' who are objects of charity, and such 'poor children' are and have always been a well-recognized and a well-defined class. 

But it is urged that the bounty must wholly fail, because the charity of this lady extended to an "innumerable multitude," and was as broad as the earth. 

We can not yield assent to this doctrine. When the asylum is built, and is filled to its capacity with poor children, it would be inhuman to turn those out who are under its protecting care, because it would not receive all who might apply, and who might be found to be worthy. The charitable institutions of earth will not be closed, ought not to be closed, simply because they have not the means or capacity to relieve all the suffering that flesh is heir to. The poor 125

we have always with us, and our first and most sacred duty is care for those who are unable to care for themselves and courts of equity have always been liberal in the construction of wills devising property for charitable uses. 

Among others, the following devises have been upheld: "To furnish releif to all poor emigrants and travelers coming to St. Louis on their way bona fide to settle in the West" (Chambers v. City of St. Louis, 29 Mo. 543.); "for the worship and service of God" (Attorney General v. Pearson, 8 Meriv. 352); "for objects and purposes of charity, public and private" (Saltonstall v. Sanders, 11 Allen 446); "for the benefit of the Christian religion, to be applied in such manner as in the judgment of the executor, would best promote the object named" (Miller v. Teachout, 24 O. St. 525); "such charitable institutions of the city of St. Louis, Missouri, as the executor might deem worthy” (Howe v. Wilson, 91 Mo. 45); for “such charitable purposes, as the trustee might deem best" (Powell v. Hatch, 100 Mo. 592); "for the suffering poor of the town of Auburn” (Howard v. American Peace Society, 49 Me. 288). * 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 271, par. 13. (Par. 9)

MASON v. BLOOMINGTON LIBRARY ASSOCIATION 

Illinois Supreme Court, 1909, 237 Ill. 442

HAND, J. It is first contended that the first paragraph of the will creates a perpetuity and is void, and that the court erred in appointing a trustee and in directing that the amount remaining of the $500 mentioned in that paragraph, after the puchase of a monument, should be turned over to a trustee to be kept at interest, the interest to be expended in the care of the family burial lot where the testratrix should be buried.

The law is well settled in this country that a perpetual trust can not be created to take care of a private burial lot unless the creation of such trust is authorized by statute.* * * In this state the legislature has provided (Hurd's 

Stat. 1905, p. 223), that trusts may be created for such purposes in the hands of the board of directors provided for by "An act to provide for the proper care and management of county cemetery grounds," but there is no statute in this state which provides for the creation of such a fund in the hands of a private trustee. A trust created under a statute authorizing a trust to be created in perpetuity for the purpose of caring for and keeping in repair a cemetery, burial lot or monument is characterized by the court in Morse v. Inhabitants of Natick, 176 Mass. 510, as a statutory trust in contradiction to a charitable trust. The cases of Green v. Hogan, 153 Mass. 462, and Jones v. Habersham, 107 U. S. 174, are not, therefore, in point. In Bates v. Bates, supra, the court said an examination of the authorities (and many cases are cited) "Will show that it has been repeatedly held that a bequest to provide a fund for the permanent care of a private tomb or burial place could not be treated as a public charity and thus made perpetual, and that such bequest would be void." It was also pointed out in that case that there was in force in that state a statute similar to the statute in this state hereinbefore referred to, but it was said "these statutory provisions have here no application.” And in Coit v. Comstock, supra, it was said: "It has been held in numerous decisions that bequests for the purpose of keeping burial lots or cemeteries in good order or repair are not given in charity, and therefore are not protected by the statute 126

of charitable uses.” And in Johnson v. Holifiel supra, it was said: "It seems to be well settled by the course of decisions that a bequest of money, the interest thereon to be perpetually applied to preserving and keeping in repair the graves and monuments of testratrix and other named persons, is repugnant to the rule against perpetuities, and void.”

We would be glad to hold, were it possible so to do, the trust attempted to be created by the testratrix by the first paragraph of her will valid. We, however, are forced by the current and great weight of authority to hold that a trust like the one in question is not a gift to any public use and that its purpose is purely private and secular. Our conclusion is, therefore, that the trust attempted to be created by the first paragraph of the will is void, and that the portion of the $500 mentioned in that paragraph, remaining after the purchase of the monument, should be treated as a part of the residuary estate of the testratrix and disposed of under paragraph 9 of said will. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 271, par. 14. 127

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 7 Questions 

1. What are some of the reasons for creating a trust in land? 

2. Why was the Statute of Mortmain passed? How was it evaded? 

3. Why was the Statute of Uses passed, and what was its most important effect? What was the difference between a use and a trust? 

4. In what three way may express trusts be created? 

5. What is meant by the term, "constructive trust?" 

6. May one validly declare himself trustee for another, by way of gift? 

7. A, by deed, conveys land to B, upon an oral trust for C; is the trust enforcible? If not, how may it be made so? 

8. What is a charitable trust? In what three ways does the law, as to such trusts, differ from the law, as to private trusts? 

9. A left $10,000 to B, upon trust, to buy a burial plot and erect a monument thereon to his friend X, and $25,000, the income of which was to be used to take care of the grave and the monument. Are these provisions in A's will valid? 

10. Smith, by will, created a trust of land for his widow and young children, and appointed his friend Jones as trustee. After serving as trustee for two years, Jones became insane. What is the proper procedure in such a case? 128

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 8 TRUSTS IN LAND-Continued 1. Purchase-Money-Resulting Trusts 

Suppose that A buys a farm from X but directs the conveyance to be made to B; later, A asks B to convey the farm to him (A). Is 

A entitled to compel B to make the conveyance? That, as we shall see, depends upon the relationship, if any, between A and B. 

A few centuries ago, conveyancing customs in England were somewhat different from those in England and in this country today. For about a century before the passing in 1534 of the Statute of Uses in England, a large part of the land in England was held in use. Because of this custom it was natural for one who bought land -A to have the title conveyed to a third person-B-to hold for the use or benefit of A. About a century later (1650) when the modern passive trust came into existence, the same presumption was applied, although in the meantime the custom of conveyancing had changed and it was not usual for land to be held in trust. The only basis for the modern presumption that B is to hold on a resulting trust for A, is that A is more likely to intend that B-if not a dependent of A-shall hold the property in trust for A, than that he intended a gift to B.

In order that a purchase-money-resulting trust should come into existence, the purchase-money must be furnished by A not later than the time of the conveyance by X to B, but this requirement is satisfied if A had bound himself to B to pay the purchase money. 

Hence if A borrows from B the money wherewith to buy the land from X, and has X convey the land to B, there is a presumption that B is the trustee of the land on a resulting trust for A, A having the right to repay the loan to B and get the land, B being entitled to hold the land merely as security for such repayment. Where A furnishes not all but part of the purchase money and B furnishes the 129

rest, and title is conveyed to B, there is a presumption of a resulting trust as to a proportionate part of the land; so, if A furnishes one-third of the purchase money and B two-thirds, there is a presumption that B holds the land, two-thirds for himself and onethird on a resulting trust for A.

It is not necessary that B have any knowledge of the transaction at the time that title is conveyed to him. If he does know of it and orally agrees with A to hold the land in trust for A, such oral agreement is not enforceable but it would tend to show that A has not intended to make a gift of the land to B. 

If A, being in pecuniary difficulties, buys land from X and has the conveyance made to B to defraud A's creditors, such fraud prevents A from getting the land from B, but it does not prevent A's creditors from having the land sold and the proceeds applied to pay their claims against A. 

Where B is not a dependent of A, the presumption of a resulting trust for A may be rebutted by showing that A actually intended to make a gift of the land to B. Where B is a dependent of A, such as a wife or minor child, there is no presumption of a resulting trust to A; in such a case, A has the burden of showing that he actually intended a trust for himself and not a gift to B. 

The presumption of a purchase-money-resulting trust is difficult to justify, either on the basis of probability or convenience, and should be abolished; but it ought always to be possible for A to show that he did intend such a trust. A purchase-money trust should not be abolished, but the presumption should be abolished. 

2. Resulting Trust When Intended Trust Fails 

Suppose A devises land or bequeaths personal property to T upon trust for C and C dies before A, for whom shall T hold in trust? In some states there are statutes which require T to hold in trust for C's heir or next of kin; but apart from such statutes there is a lapse of the beneficial interest and T holds in trust for A's heir or next of kin.

Suppose A devises land or bequeaths personal property to T, upon trust for a purpose which has been declared illegal either by statute or by decision-for example, a devise upon a trust which violates the rule against perpetuities. In such a case there is a resulting trust for A's heir or next of kin. And there is a similar resulting trust where A conveys property to T upon trust but where no purpose is mentioned in the conveyance; or where the purpose mentioned is carried out by T but there is still some property left. 130

All the above resulting trusts are imposed upon the basis that, if A had thought about the possibility of the failure of his intended trust, he would probably have preferred that the beneficial interest in the property should go to himself, or to his heirs or to his next of kin, rather than to the trustee-especially if the trustee happens to be a corporation. Such a rule seems justified both on the basis of probability and of fairness. Of course, if in any of the above cases A received pay from anyone for the property conveyed by him there is no resulting trust and the transferee may keep the property. 

3. Gratuitous Conveyance by Deed Upon Oral Trust 

Suppose that A gratuitously conveys land to T upon an oral trust to hold the land either for C or for A, and T insists upon holding it for himself; what may C or A do about it? C has no remedy because of the statute of frauds; but where the conveyance was upon an oral trust for C, A may have a constructive trust of the land declared for himself if T refuses to carry out the oral trust for C; this is done in order to prevent T from being unjustly enriched. But where the conveyance is upon an oral trust for A, what may A do about that situation?

Since it was the general custom in England during the century just before the Statute of Uses-passed in 1534—to have land held in use, if A made a gratuitous conveyance to B without declaring any use either in B or a third party, it was presumed that B was to hold to the use of A-a resulting use, as it was called. After the 

Statute of Uses was passed, if there was a resulting use to A in such a case, then the conveyance by A to B would be utterly futile because A would at once get the legal title and the situation would be the same as if no conveyance had been made. After the modern passive trust was recognized it was held not only that there was no presumption of a resulting trust for A but that no resulting trust in A's favor could be recognized. But if A did not intend any gift to B it seems clear that B, having paid nothing for the land, should not be allowed to keep it, and hence the English courts impose upon B a constructive trust for A, to prevent B from being unjustly enriched at A's expense. In this country many courts, because imposing a constructive trust for A leads to the same result as enforcing the oral trust for him, have refused to follow the English rule and have allowed B to keep the property, on the ground that it would violate the Statute of Frauds to allow A to get it, but the present tendency is to follow the sound English rule. 131

4. Conveyance by Will Upon an Oral Trust 

If A devises land to B but with an unexpressed intention that B shall hold the land in trust for C, and B does not know of such intention till after the death of A, then B is entitled to keep the land for himself; to hold otherwise would violate the statute of wills which requires such intention to be expressed in the will. But if B does learn of A's unexpressed intent before A's death, B is not allowed to keep the land for himself; but instead of imposing a constructive trust upon B for the heirs of A—as would be done if the conveyance had been by deed instead of by will (see paragraph 3)the courts impose a constructive trust for C. This obviously violates the Statute of Frauds, but courts do it because they are very desirous of carrying out the intention of A, the testator, and it is now too late for A to do it himself. The doctrine has now been extended to a conveyance by deed from A to B, made in contemplation of death.

Where A's heir orally promised A that he would hold certain land in trust for C if A would not devise such land to C, the latter may have A's heir declared a constructive trustee for him and compel such heir to convey the property to him. 

5. Property Acquired by Homicide 

If X, a beneficiary under A's will, should murder A to prevent A from revoking his will, does the fact of murder prevent X from getting and keeping the property?

On this point there are three different views. Some states hold that X may keep it for himself. Other states hold that the murder revoked that part of the will in favor of X so that nothing passes under the will to X. Under this view, if the fact of the murder was not discovered till after X sold and conveyed the property to Y, the latter would be compelled to give up the property. The objection to this view is that the statute of wills makes no provision for such a revocation. The third and sound doctrine is that although the legal title to the property passes to X according to the terms of the will, an equity court will impose upon him a constructive trust for the benefit of A's heir or next of kin in order to prevent him from profiting by his crime. If before the fact of the murder was discovered, X should sell and convey to Y, Y could keep it. 

If A is about to make a will disinheriting his prospective heir X or reducing the amount he would otherwise get as heir and X murders A to prevent his making such a will it seems obvious that X should not be allowed to profit by his crime. Where A allowed it 132

to be known that he expected to make a will in favor of Y, then a constructive trust should be imposed upon X in favor of Y and X should be compelled to convey the property to Y. Where A did not let it be known whom he intended to benefit by his will, then a constructive trust should be imposed upon X for the benefit of those next entitled after X. Where the beneficiary of a life insurance policy murders the insured in order to collect the insurance money, the insurance company should be compelled to pay the insured's estate; if it has in good faith already been paid to the murderer, the latter should be compelled to pay over to the insured's estate what he received from the company. 

6. Purchase by Trustee-Tracing Trust Funds 

If T, trustee of land for C, should sell it-rightfully or wrongfully-and wrongfully invest the proceeds in the purchase of other property for his own benefit, C, the beneficiary, may have T declared a constructive trustee of such property for the benefit of C, and compel T to transfer that property to him. If such a remedy will not make C the beneficiary whole-for example, if T made a poor investment-C will have a personal claim against T for the difference; whereas, if he made a good investment so that the value of the property is greater than that of the original trust property, the constructive trust remedy enables C to avail himself of such increase in value.

If T wrongfully mingles trust money with some of his own the trust attaches to the entire amount, C having an equitable lien on it or any part of it for the amount of the trust money thus commingled; hence if part of the mixture be lost C may enforce his claim against what is left.
