If T deposits trust money in a bank with his own funds in his own name and later withdraws part of it and spends it, such withdrawals are charged against his own interests, so that so long as enough is left in the bank account to equal the amount of the trust funds, C is protected by giving him an equitable lien thereon. But if all is withdrawn or if the amount in the bank is reduced below the amount of the trust funds, C will lose entirely or pro rata respectively, if T becomes insolvent; for while T is liable personally for his breaches of trust to the full extent of C's loss, yet in a contest between C and the general creditors of an insolvent T, C cannot ordinarily be a preferred claimant unless he is able to trace the trust funds and show where they now are. Where the amount first withdrawn by T has not been spent by him but has been invested in property, C is entitled to an equitable lien on this 133

property; this is highly important to C if the balance of the account is later spent by T. 

The above reasoning applies also to cases where there is a constructive trust based upon other wrongs besides breaches of trust. 

If bank officials accept deposits after they know their bank is insolvent, such conduct is fraudulent. Hence, if the depositor can trace his money in such a case he is entitled to claim it as against the general creditors of the bank; but if it has been paid out again to meet checks presented for payment he is not entitled, apart from statute, to any such preference. In some states he is protected by statute giving him relief without his being able to trace the money deposited by him.

If at any time T should invest commingled funds or any part thereof in property, and such property should increase in value, C should be allowed to avail himself of all the profit or of his proportional share thereof, respectively. 

7. Taking Renewal of Lease-Gratuities Received by 

Trustee or Other Fiduciary

If a trustee or agent or other fiduciary receives, because of his fiduciary position, gratuities from a third person-for example, if an agent to buy receives gratuities from the seller in order to get the business-the agent must account to his principal for the amounts thus received, even though no harm was suffered by his principal.

Even if a trustee or other fiduciary uses his own funds, any profit which he makes thereon because of his fiduciary position must be accounted for to his beneficiary. Though a lessee for years may have no option to renew a lease, the probability that the landlord will renew is regarded as a valuable property right; so where T held a lease and renewed it for himself instead of for C, the beneficiary of the trust, it was held that C could have T declared a constructive trustee for him of the new lease even though the landlord had refused to renew the lease to T as trustee for C on the ground that C was a minor. 

8. Trustee With Authority to Sell or to Buy

If T has authority to sell land for C at a public sale and he bids in the land for himself, C may have T declared a constructive trustee for C's benefit, no matter if the price was fair to C. As trustee it was his duty to get as much as he could for the property; if he were allowed to bid and keep for himself he would be occupying the in- 134

consistent position of wanting to get the property as cheaply as possible. What he should have done was to notify the court that he wished to bid at the sale and ask the court to appoint some one else to conduct the sale.

But there is no flat rule against T's buying the trust property from C at a private sale; but C may have such sale set aside by an equity court unless T is able to convince the court that he divested himself of every advantage which he had gained from his position as trustee, by disclosing to C whatever knowledge he had acquired in regard to the value of the property. 

Where A turns over money to B with which to buy property and take title in A's name and B buys the property with his own fundsfinding that he can get it at a great bargain—and takes title in his own name, A may have a constructive trust imposed upon B and get the property upon reimbursing B for what B paid for it. 135

N 

LESSON 8 CASES 

(Par. 1) 

FROEMKE v. MARKS 

Illinois Supreme Court, 1913, 259 Ill. 146.

Mr. JUSTICE DUNN: This is an appeal from a decree requiring the appellant to convey to the appellee certain real estate in the city of Chicago. 

Neither the pleadings nor the decree have been abstracted and we have before us only the evidence, from which it appears that the premises in question were purchased and the consideration therefor paid by the appellee but at his request the title was conveyed to the appellant, who was the father-inlaw of the appellee's brother, and who claims to not have known of the conveyance to him until some time after it was made. Under these circumstances a resulting trust arose in favor of the appellee. A trust results from the fact that one man's money has been invested in land and the title taken in the name of another. It is immaterial whether the purchase was made by the one or the other, and it may have been made by either without the knowledge of the other. If the fact exists that one man's money paid for the land and the title was taken in another, a trust is raised in favor of the person whose money was used to purchase the land. * 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 275, par. 21. (Par. 1) 

CURRENCE v. WARD et al. 

West Virginia Supreme Court of Appeals, 1897, 43 W. VA. 367. 

BRANNON, JUDGE. Under decree of the circuit court of Randolph county, certain land of Melvin Currence was sold, and purchased by William L. Ward and Wirt C. Ward, and afterwards, under decree of resale for non-payment of purchase-money, the land was again sold, and purchased by the same purchasers. The sales were confirmed; before all the purchase-money had been paid, or a deed made conveying the land to the purchasers, Currence instituted the present suit against the said purchasers and Lewis C. Conrad, to whom they had sold the land, alleging that the Wards had purchased in the land for his benefit, and that Conrad purchased with notice of his rights, and seeking to have it decreed that said purchase by the Wards was in trust for his benefit, and to set aside the sale by the Wards to Conrad; and the decree gave Currence the relief he asked, and the executor of William L. Ward appealed.

Observing in the books expression to the effect that to raise a resulting trust the money paid by the one claiming it must be an aliquot part of the whole, and Currence not having paid an aliquòt part, it occurred to me that this might be an obstacle to relief, but an examination has satisfied me to the contrary. So it be clear what amount is paid, it would seem to be immaterial whether it be an aliquot part of the whole. Forty-nine is just as well a certain fraction of one hundred as fifty. This theory likely originated in a statement by LORD HARDWICKE in Crop v. Norton, 9 Mod. 233. He likely referred, not to payment of part of the purchase-money, but to where several paid, in- 136

timating that there could be no resulting trust in favor of two or more paying, but only where one was the beneficiary. This even is not a tenable view, because in the later case of Wray v. Steel, 2 Ves. & B. 388, it was held that there could be a resulting trust in favor of several. This is now established. *** As to Lord Hardwicke's statement, Chancellor Kent said it was not correct when only a single individual claimed the benefit, for the cases recognized the trust where the money of A formed only a part of the consideration for the land purchased in the name of B. The land in such case is to be charged a pro tanto (proportionally). * * * McGowan v. McGowan, 74 Am. Dec. 668, has no bearing on part payment of purchase-money, but holds that the payment must be made for a distinct part or interest in the land. The books are indefinite as to this aliquot part expression, sometimes referring to a definite part of the land, sometimes of the money. Though the theory on which a resulting trust rests is that the payment made becomes converted into land, and is not a lien on it, that refers rather to the time of payment (that is, that it must be before the legal title is taken), rather than to the portion of the money paid, or of the interest in the land springing therefrom; for where both have paid they can have joint interest in the land proportionately to the money paid (that is, the party who is to hold as trustee and the beneficiary), just as where there are two or three beneficiaries paying separate amounts. So if one pay forty-nine dollars and another fifty-one dollars, their interest would be 49/100 and 51/100 respectively, just as reasonably as if each had paid fifty dollars. It is only necessary that it be clearly shown what part he has paid; but there must be certainty as to the interest in the land. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 275 par. 21. 

(Par. 1) WELTON v. DEVINE 

New York Supreme Court, 1854, 20 Barb. 9.

This was an action to recover the possession of a farm in the town of Rochester, in the county of Ulster. It was tried at the Ulster Circuit, in November, 1852, before Mr. JUSTICE WRIGHT. The plaintiff gave in evidence a deed of the premises to herself from Peter Elmendorf and wife, bearing date the 26th of November, 1829. The consideration expressed in the deed was $500, paid by the grantee. Also, the record of the appointment of trustees of the estate of 

Enos Welton the husband of the plaintiff, as an absconding debtor, dated February 28, 1842. Also a deed from the trustees to Edmond Brugh, dated July 1, 1842, conveying all the estate, right, title and interest of Enos Welton in the premises. The defendant claimed to hold the premises under this title. 

The plaintiff further proved by her son that she and her husband kept separate purses; that she had money which she concealed from her husband. 

That the money with which the land was purchased was furnished by her to her husband when he went to make the purchase. Upon his cross-examination the same witness stated that he know of no way the plaintiff had of getting money, except from her husband. At the time of the commencement of this suit Enos Welton was dead.

The parties having rested, the court decided that the testimony establishing a resulting trust in favor of Enos Welton in the state purchased in the name of Hannah M. Welton, and non-suited the plaintiff. 137

To this decision the plaintiff excepted. The cause was heard, at the general term, upon a motion for a new trial, upon a bill of exceptions. 

HARRIS, J. It is by no means certain, from the testimony in the case, that 

Enos Welton furnished the money with which the land in question was purchased. But assuming this to be undisputed, it would not establish a resulting trust. The presumption that he who supplied the money to make a purchase intends it for his own benefit rather than that of another, does not apply in cases, like that of parent and child or husband and wife, where the purchase may fairly be deemed to be made for another from motives of natural love and affection. The presumption in such cases is, that the purchase is intended as an advancement, unless the contrary is established by proof. * * *

In this case, there is no evidence to rebut the presumption that the deed taken by the husband in the name of his wife, was intended as a provision in her favor. Without such evidence a resulting trust could not be established. 

The non-suit, therefore, should be set aside, and a new trial awarded, with costs to abide the event. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 276, par 21. (Par. 3) 

HAIGH v. KAYE 

In Chancery, 1872, L. R. 7 Ch. App. 469. 

SIR W. M. JAMES, L. J. * *The defendant admits that there was a conveyance given to him purporting to be executed in consideration of 850 pounds paid by him to the original plaintiff, G. A. Haigh, by which he became, by purchase, owner of the estate. He admits that there was no such transaction in fact as any sale to him, but that the payment of the 850 pounds was a mere form, and that the plaintiff paid the expenses of the conveyance to him, or gave him the money to pay them. That being so, he goes on to admit that he was to hold the estate upon trust to pay the rents and profits to the plaintiff, and when the plaintiff called upon him for a reconveyance he was to reconvey it. The plaintiff has called upon him to reconvey the estate, and he suggests by way of answer to that, first of all vaguely and faintly, that this transaction was not altogether a straightforward transaction; that this transaction was entered into with a view to defraud somebody else. The defendant says in effect: "I am to remain in possession of the estate, because we were both of us engaged in a transaction contrary to the law, and you will not take it way from me to give it to a man who was as bad as I was in the matter; in fact it was an illegal and fraudulent transaction against somebody else, and where there is an equal crime the court ought to hold that in paridelicto melior est conditio possidentis." However, the defendant has not raised that defense in the way in which, according to my judgment, such a defense ought to be raised. If a defendant means to say that he claims to hold property given to him for an immoral purpose, in violation of all honor and honesty, he must say so in plain terms, and must clearly put forward his own scoundrelism if he means to reap the benefit of it. Here he has simply said that the plaintiff, fearing an adverse decision in the suit of Haigh v. Haigh, conveyed the property to him. I think that is not sufficient. 

The next objection taken was upon the statute of frauds. The defendant ad- 

1. That is, if both are in equal fault the one in possession has the better right. 138

mits that he took the estate upon the most positive agreement to return it; but in another part of his answer he sets up the statute of frauds, and claims the estate as a right. Now the statute of frauds no doubt says, that a person claiming under any declaration of trust or confidence must show that in writing; but the statute goes on to say that no resulting trust, and no trust arising from the operation of law, is within that enactment. I apprehand it is clear that the statute of frauds was never intended to prevent the court of equity from giving relief in a case of a plain, clear and deliberate fraud. The words of Lord Justice Turner, in the case of Lincoln v. Wright, 4 De G. & J. 16, where he said, "The principle of this court is that the statute of frauds was not made to cover frauds," express a principle upon which this court has acted in numerous instances, where the court has refused to allow a man to take advantage of the statute of frauds to keep another man's property which he has obtained through fraud. * * *

That being so, the statute of frauds and the ground of supposed illegality of the whole transaction being set aside, the defendant comes into possession of this property as a trustee for the plaintiff. Then he says that although he was made a trustee there was a talk about his becoming the purchaser. He does not pretend to say that at that time there was any bargain, but he says that it was understood, before he was called upon to reconvey the property, that if he could make an arrangement to purchase it he was to have it. 

I am of opinion that the defendant has failed to prove his case, and therefore that the decree is quite right in declaring that he is to be treated as a trustee of the property, and must reconvey it to the representative of the original plaintiff. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 276, par. 23. 

CALDWELL v. CALDWELL 

Kentucky Supreme Court, 1870, 70 Ky.

ROBERTSON, C. J. ** * In the year 1863, Alexander Caldwell, a citizen of Campbell county, Kentucky, shortly before his death, published his last will, whereby he contemplated a proximate equality in the distribution of his estate among his six children, one of whom, James Caldwell, was then a soldier in the Confederate Army. The testator, sympathizing with that son, and the cause he had espoused, indicated a desire to secure to him "the home place" of nearly three hundred acres of land, which would not have exceeded the value of the devises to each of his other five children then near him. But apprehending that James, if he should even survive the war, might by his rebellion against the Government of the United States forfeit his estate, he devised the legal title of the "home place" to his other five children, on a latent trust that if James should ever return, and be capable of holding the title, they should convey it to him. And this, according to satisfactory oral testimony, they understood and tacitly agreed to fulfil. 

On his return, about the close of the war, there being no danger of forfeiture, two of these devisees, Daniel and William Caldwell, true to the trust, each conveyed to him one-fifth of the land, of the whole of which he thereupon took possession with the apparent acquiescence of the other three devisees of the home tract; and this possession he appears to have retained without disturbance or complaint for more than three years, when the three re- 139

cusant devisees and the husband of one of them refusing to convey their interests to him, he on the 10th day of September, 1869, brought this suit against them for enforcing their obligation under a resulting trust. His petition charging the trust was denied by their answer; and on that issue the circuit court decreed a release to James of their title, and by this appeal they seek to reverse that decree.

Implied trusts being excepted from the statute of frauds and perjuries, if the facts established such a trust in this case, no written memorial of it was necessary for enforcing it, nor was the oral testimony incompetent on the alleged ground that it contradicts the will. 

Extraneous testimony is incompetent to supply an unintentional omission or to contradict an expressed intention in a will. But the facts established by extrinsic evidence in this case have no such aim or effect. They are consistent with the testator's intention, and with the concession that the will is just what he intended it to be, and they supply nothing which unintentionally omitted. 

He intended to pretermit his son James as an express devisee of the land, and to rely for his benefit on the plighted honor of the express devisees. Might he not have done so securely on a promise that the five devisees would pay to James ten thousand dollars? And would proof of such agreement contradict the will, or supply any unintentional omission in it, without which omission his purpose of making James a co-equal beneficiary might have been frustrated by impending forfeiture?

The competency of oral testimony for establishing and enforcing such trusts as that claimed in this case is prescriptively recognized by undeviating authorities, among a great multitude of which we only cite the following: 

Drakeford v. Weeks, 3 Atkins 639; Barrow v. Greenbough, 3 Vesey 152, Strickland v. Aldridge, 9 Vesey 519; Maislar v. Gillespie, 11 Vesey 639; 2 

Powell on Devises 415. To these citations we might, if deemed needful, add many others in England and America, and even in this court, illustrative of the same principles.

On the like principles the most familiar case of resulting trusts is upheld. 

Why else, where an unqualified title to land is conveyed to one, the law has adjudged that holds it in trust for another on oral proof that the latter paid the consideration, in the absence of any fact authorizing a countervailing presumption? In such a case there is no sale of land by the one to the other requiring writing, and the extraneous fact is admitted not to contradict the deed, but to prevent a fraud.

So here, had not the actual devisees been understood by the testator as accepting the devise in trust for James, an essentially different will would have been made, excluding their power over the land as devised; and consequently their refusal to execute the trust is a constructive fraud on the testator as well as on their brother James.

The proof of the trust is corroborated by the conduct of Daniel and William, by the testator's purpose of equality, and by the apparent recognition of it by the acquiescence in the claim and possession by James for years since his return.

We are satisfied that will was made as it is, with the mutual understanding of the trust as claimed.

Wherefore, we conclude that the judgment of the circuit court is right, and therefore affirm it. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 277, par. 24. 140

(Par. 5) 

PERRY v. STRAWBRIDGE 

Missouri Supreme Court, 1907, 209 Mo. 621.

GRAVES, J. * ** This in an exceedingly interesting case. The question for determination, bluntly stated, is, Can a husband who murders his wife inherit the one-half part of her estate under para. 2938, Revised Statutes, 1899? 

To this state it is a new question, and with few exceptions, a new one in all the states. But few courts of last resort have been called upon to pass upon the question as to what effect the criminal act of a prospective legal heir will have upon his or her rights, under positive statutes governing descents and distributions. Of those which have passed upon it we frankly confess that the holdings of a majority thereof are against the views which we entertain and will hereafter express. We are not satisfied with the reasoning of those cases and have been unable to reach the conclusion that a mere prospective legal heir, or devisee in a will, can make certain that which was uncertain, by his own felonious act, in the cold-blooded murder of the party from whom he or she expects to inherit. We do not believe that these courts have fully applied and used the canons of statutory construction which we have the right to use and ought to use to avoid a result so repugnant to common right and common decency. The construction as has been given such statutes bruises and wounds the finer sensibilities of every man. In the case at bar, the murdered woman, younger in years, might have outlived the prospective heir. The propery involved in this very suit might have been used by her for her own comforts even though she had died first. Being hers it might have been sold and the proceeds disposed of by gift or otherwise. Can it be said that one, by highhanded murder, cannot only make himself an heir in fact, when he had but a mere expectancy before, but further shall enjoy the fruits of his own crime? To us this seems abhorrent to all reason, and reason is the better element of the law.

In fact, the pathway of judicial literature from the earliest period down to the present is literally strewn with cases, which like beacon lights have guided the hand of justice in preventing unjust, unrightous, absurd, unreasonable and abhorrent results from the use of general words and expressions in statutes. To cite and quote more would be but to become tedious. We have gone thus far on account of the newness of the particular question of this case. 

Under these authorities we should not and will not hold that "widower" as used in para. 2938, supra, means one who has created a condition by murderous hand and heart. This case is without the statute. "Widower," as there used, means one who has been reduced to that condition by the ordinary and usual vicissitudes of life, and not one who, by felonious act, has himself created that condition. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 277, par. 25. 141

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 8 

Questions 1. What is meant by a purchase-money-resulting trust and when does such a trust arise? When and why is there a presumption of a resulting trust? 

2. A, being in failing circumstances, buys land from X and has title conveyed to B in order to defraud his (A's) creditors. 

What, if any, are the rights of the creditors? 

3. A died having devised his farm to X upon trust for B; B died before A. Who is entitled to the land? 

4. A, by deed, conveys a piece of land to X upon an oral understanding that X will hold it for A. X claims to hold it for himself. What may A do about it? 

5. Suppose A by will devises land to X intending that X hold the land for B. Who is entitled to the land (a) if X did not know of this before A's death? (b) if X did know? 

6. A made his will devising his land to his nephew Y; later Y did something which displeased A, and A threatened to revoke his will; to prevent this Y murdered A. Before the fact of the murder is discovered Y sells and conveys the land to Z. May Z keep it? 

7. D had a son with whom he quarreled and then threatened to make a will devising his land to his niece F; in order to prevent this E murdered D. What rights, if any, has F? 

8. T sold some trust land and invested the proceeds in other land intending to keep it for himself. May he do so? 

9. T takes some trust money and some of his own money and invests all of it in a farm in his own name. What may the beneficiary of the trust do about it? 

10. T is authorized by the trust instrument to sell the trust land at a public sale; he does so and bids on some of it and buys it for himself at a fair price; what may the beneficiary of the trust do about it? 142

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 9 TRUSTS IN LAND-Continued 1. Bona Fide Purchaser from T—(1) Paying Value 

If T, trustee for C, transfers, in violation of his trust, some or all of the trust property to X, under what circumstances, if at all, may X keep the property?

When, about 1450, the English equity court first gave a remedy to C against one who held for the use of C or in trust for C, no remedy was given against his transferee, X, unless X expressly undertook the trust. This, of course, did not completely protect C, so later the English equity court took a further step that in case of such a transfer by T the court would presume that X took the property upon the original use or trust. Still later this presumption hardened into a flat rule that X was bound whether he did or did not undertake to be bound by T's obligations with respect to the land. This obligation which was imposed upon X in order to give more complete protection to C came to be called a constructive trust -constructive, because it was imposed without regard to the intention of either T or X; and later this doctrine of constructive trust was extended to all cases where X would otherwise be unjustly enriched with specific property at the expense of the plaintiff, thus including cases where the conveyance to X was in fraud of the plaintiff's right to have a contract or conveyance reformed or corrected, or to have a conveyance rescinded because of fraud or mistake.

But X was not always bound by this doctrine of constructive trust. 

If he received title to the property and paid value for it before he had notice of the prior equitable right based upon a trust or specific performance of contract or reformation or rescission, he was protected. Having paid value for the property and obtained title thereto, the English equity court decided that it would not be just to take 143

the property away from him. This limitation upon the doctrine of constructive trust came to be called the doctrine of bona fide (good faith) purchase for value without notice.

Now suppose that X buys and gets the conveyance without notice of plaintiff's equitable claim to the property and then gets notice of such claim? In such case, not having paid, X is not considered as having as strong a claim to the property as the plaintiff and an equity court will compel him, at the suit of the plaintiff, to convey the property to the plaintiff, even though X has given his note for the purchase money secured by a mortgage on the property. Where he has paid enough of the purchase money—say, one-half—so that a mortgage on the property would be good security for the rest, it would seem that X should be allowed to keep the land; but the rule is usually stated that he must have paid it all. Where in good faith, without notice of plaintiff's right, he has paid a part and then the plaintiff compels him to surrender the property, he is given an equitable lien on the property to secure him for his claim to be reimbursed by plaintiff for what he has paid. 

Suppose that X, in buying the property from T or the holder of the title to any property to which plaintiff has an equity, instead of paying cash, gives up a claim which he had against T-for example, a claim for money borrowed from X by T; is this sufficient value to protect X? Yes, and the tendency is to apply this principle where X merely accepts trust property as collateral security for the payment of his claim against T. Suppose that X buys from T who has authority to sell and gets title and pays him money for it, must X see to it that T applies the money properly in the performance of his trust? The early English rule was that he must do so; but that is no longer the rule either in England or in the United States. 

2. Bona Fide Purchaser from T-(2) Getting Title 

Suppose that X pays all the purchase money in good faith but receives notice of the trust or other equity before he actually gets the title? Although, having paid all the purchase money in good faith, his claim to the property is as strong as that of C, the beneficiary of the trust or other equity, C's equity is preferred because it is prior in time and if C insists, X must give up the land upon being reimbursed for what he has paid. Another reason for preferring C to 

X is that it would place a premium on dishonesty to allow X to profit by his dishonesty by getting title after notice of C's equitable claim to it.

If X does not buy the trust property but merely lends money to 144

T upon security of the trust property he is protected to the extent of his advances if they were made in good faith and he received the mortgage without notice of C's equitable claim. 

3. Bona Fide Purchaser From T―(3) Without Notice 

As stated above, X is not protected as a bona fide purchaser unless he gets title and pays value without notice. But suppose he does this and then conveys the property to Y who knows that T committed a breach of trust in conveying the trust property to X; may 

Y keep the land? Yes, because if Y were not protected X might find it difficult to sell his property-especially land-where the breach of trust or other misconduct giving rise to an equitable right had become well known in the neighborhood of the property. 

In order to constitute notice to X it is enough that he know facts that would put a prudent man upon inquiry. If X knew that the man from whom he was buying was a trustee he is usually chargeable with notice if it turns out that T had no authority to make the sale. 

If X accepts a quit claim deed instead of a warranty deed he cannot usually claim to be without notice. 

4. Death of T; of C

If there is only one trustee and he dies leaving heirs the title to the trust land-apart from statute-descends to them; but since they pay nothing for the land they take it subject to the trust for C. If there are several co-trustees and one of them dies his title goes to the other trustees.

If T dies without heirs his title goes to the state but subject to C's equitable claim thereto; in some states by statute the title vests in the appropriate court which will appoint a successor to T and transfer to him the title.

If C dies intestate leaving heirs his equitable interest in the land descends to such heirs. If C dies intestate without leaving any heirs, his equitable interest goes to the state as property without an owner. 

5. Bankruptcy of T; of C-Spendthrift Trusts

If T has no beneficial interest in the trust property and becomes bankrupt, title to the trust property does not pass to his assignee in bankruptcy; if he does have some beneficial interest, then title does pass, but subject, of course, to the equitable claims of the other beneficiaries of the trust.

For centuries the policy of common law courts has been that property shall be freely transferable either by the act of the 145

owner thereof or by act of his creditors; because of this settled policy any provision in a conveyance, either by deed or will, that the transferee shall not be able to transfer the property or that his creditors shall not be able to subject it to the payment of their claims has been held by common law courts to be void. Where the conveyance is of the fee equity courts usually have a similar rule in regard to the interest of a trust beneficiary. But if the trust beneficiary has only a life estate most states uphold such a provision, so that if he should become bankrupt his creditors cannot reach such life interest. Although it is not necessary that the beneficiary be an actual spendthrift such trusts are usually called spendthrift trusts.

The arguments in favor of the doctrine upholding spendthrift trusts as valid are that it ought to be possible to protect from poverty those who are not competent to take care of property themselves, in much the same way as minors and insane persons are protected; and that if such trusts are held to be valid it will tend to relieve the state from the burden of taking care of such incompetents. In no state may the owner of property create a valid spendthrift trust for himself.

Another method of providing for an incompetent is to stipulate in the trust instrument that T is to have an uncontrolled descretion in applying the income from the trust property for the maintenance of C. Since T has an uncontrolled discretion, C has no enforceable interest in the property which he can voluntarily transfer or which his creditors can reach. 

6. Duties of Trustee as to Conveyance of the Trust Property 

In many states if C becomes a competent adult the trust comes to an end automatically unless T has active duties to perform. In other states C may compel T to convey the trust property to him or to a third person. If there are several beneficiaries of the trust they must all concur in order to compel T to convey; he is not bound to convey it piecemeal. 

If the trust instrument directs T to convert the trust land into money or to invest trust money in land, the beneficiary-if a competent adult-may object to the conversion and take the property in its original form.

Usually C-if the only beneficiary-may compel conveyance of the trust property to himself as soon as he becomes a competent adult. But in some states the creator of the trust may validly stipu- 146

late that the beneficiary is not to receive the legal title till some later time-for example, when he reaches twenty-five or thirty years of age. The purpose of the provision is to prevent persons of slight business ability from wasting the property and is therefore somewhat similar to the purpose of a spendthrift trust; but such a provision postponing control does not prevent the beneficiary's creditors from reaching the property. 

7. Duties of Trustee as to Possession, Information and 

Custody 

One who has merely a life interest in the trust property has no right to demand that he be put into possession of it unless it is clear from the trust instrument or from special circumstances - for example, if the trust property consists of the family mansionthat the creator of the trust intended that he should have possession.

Accurate and clear accounts of the trust property must be kept by T and produced for the inspection of C; and if opinions of lawyers are procured by T to guide him in the administration of the trust, C is entitled to the benefit of them also. 

While he is properly performing his duties as a trustee, T, like other fiduciaries, such as bailees, executors, guardians, etc., is not liable as an insurer of the trust property but only for failure to exercise the care of a prudent man under similar circumstances. If the property should be stolen or destroyed or if it depreciates in value he is not liable for the loss unless he was negligent. And he is not liable for loss due to his rightfully placing the property in the hands of a third party-for example, if he rents the trust property to an apparently desirable tenant.

Sometimes it is his duty not to keep personal custody of the trust property. For example, if he receives trust money either because of the payment of a debt due to the trust estate or because of rightfully selling the trust property, he should deposit such money in a reputable bank and not keep large sums on his person or at his residence. And when he makes any deposit of trust money in a bank he should use the word "trustee" or "agent" or some similar word to indicate that the money is not his own beneficially; he should do this so that the bank will not be able-by lending money to T-to get the rights of a bona fide purchaser for value of the money deposited. If T should withdraw trust money and misappropriate it, the bank is not liable to the beneficiary of the trust unless it had notice that T intended to misappropriate it. 147

In most states a public officer who has charge of public fundsfor example, a state treasurer—is liable as an insurer of such funds, so that if they are deposited in a bank and the bank fails the public officer must make good the loss. Why such a heavy liability? It is usually said to be the great public interest in the preservation of public funds. The officer should, of course, protect himself by taking out insurance. 

8. Duties of Trustee-As to Investment of Trust Funds

A prudent man might, if he has considerable assets of his own, speculate with part of them; but in most states a trustee must so invest the trust funds as to preserve the trust capital intact and may not speculate. Where T has been directed by the creator of the trust to make or continue certain investments he may ordinarily do so even though the investments would not otherwise be approved by an equity court.

In most states, apart from statute, trust funds must be invested in government securities or in first mortgages on real estate. In 

New York and a few other states there are statutes designating proper investments for savings banks and these investments are proper for trustees as well. In Massachusetts and a few other states T may invest a part of the trust funds-exercising the proper business discretion in doing so-in stocks and bonds of business corporations. But there are certain investments that would not ordinarily be approved in any state; for example, the purchase of land, loans on personal security alone, or loans on participating mortgages or on second mortgages or on leasehold mortgages. (A participating mortgage is one that is given to secure funds loaned by two or more lenders.) Where the creator of the trust had part or all of his funds invested in securities that are not legally authorized T should-in the absence of any direction by the creator of the trust-convert the trust property with reasonable promptness, into authorized securities. 

9. Duties of Trustee-As to Collection, Payment and Liability for Breach of Trust

Where there are debts due to the trust estate it is T's duty to collect them as soon as they become due and if there is a loss because he delays unreasonably in doing this he must bear the loss. 

And this is true even if it would have brought financial ruin to the debtor to press him for payment; and it is no defense to show that a good business man would not have exacted payment or that the 148

creator of the trust would not have done so. But it is an excuse if T can show that more was to be gained by temporary delay than by promptly bringing action to force payment. It is also an excuse to show that the debtor was so hopelessly insolvent that it would have been imprudent to spend time and money in trying to collect. 

Where it is T's duty to pay Y he is liable if by mistake he pays the wrong person.

If because of T's misconduct there is a total or partial loss of trust funds he is liable to C for the amount lost, with simple interest from the time of the loss. If T wrongfully makes an investment which happens to be successful C may claim the investment and compel T to account for the profits. If T should wrongfully invest trust funds in business he ordinarily must pay compound interest at the legal rate unless he can prove that the profits made were less. 

10. Delegating Trust Duties 

Suppose T gets tired of being trustee, how may he rid himself of the obligation? Either by persuading the proper equity court to accept his resignation or by getting the consent of all the beneficiaries if they are all competent adults.

While a transferee from T is bound by the trust unless he is a bona fide purchaser for value, this does not mean that he may perform the trust duties; and this applies to all transferees, including heirs, devisees and the executor of a deceased trustee. 

In a case of a private trust no action is valid unless all the trustees concur; if one trustee disagrees with the rest he should submit the matter to an equity court or he should resign and allow the court to appoint a successor. Of course, the creator of the trust may provide that a majority may act, but if there is no such provision and a majority is threatening to act, the minority should ordinarily ask for an injunction, otherwise they will be liable if the threatened action should turn out to be a breach of trust. In trusts for a public purpose, such as a hospital, school, library, etc., a majority of the trustees may validly act unless there is a different provision in the trust instrument.

Any act of T which requires discretion may not be delegated by him. For example, he himself must usually supervise an auction sale of trust property and he must decide upon investments. But mechanical duties and those which require professional or technical skill rather than discretion may be delegated; for example, he may employ lawyers, accountants, engineers, auctioneers, etc. And if it 149

is the usual course of business to employ brokers in selling trust property or in making investments he will not be liable for loss if he is careful in selecting a broker. 150

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 1) 

MOSHIER v. KNOX COLLEGE 

Illinois Supreme Court, 1863, 32 Ill. 155.

MR. JUSTICE BREESE delivered the opinion of the court: 

This was a bill in chancery, filed by Knox College, against William B. Patterson, Timothy Moshier the appellant, and others, to forclose a mortgage executed for the security of the purchase-money of the lands described in the mortgage. The bill is in usual form, alleging the execution of the note and mortgage on the first day of January, 1856, and recording same on the 24th of April, 1857, and the failure to pay the money due. The bill then alleges that the appellant, Moshier, and other defendants, claim some interest in the premises, or some part of them, as purchasers, mortgagees, or otherwise, which has accrued subsequent to the lien of the mortgage, and subject to that lien. The prayer is in the usual form. 

Moshier alone answered the bill, admitting the mortgage and recording of it, as charged in the bill, but denied any knowledge, information or belief, of the amount paid by Patterson, or the sum then due, if any, upon the mortgage, except as derived from the bill. He claimed an interest in the premises by virtue of a deed executed and delivered by Patterson to him on the 5th day of March, 1857, and recorded on the 19th of the same month, and denied that this interest, so created, was subject to the complainant's lien, and denied that it had accured subsequent thereto. 

But apart from all this, the appellees ought to retain this decree, because it is shown the indebtedness was for the purchase-money of the premises, and appellant has not shown he was a bona fide purchaser for the valuable consideration, paying his money at the time on the faith of the title so purchased. 

It was incumbent on the appellant to show not only that he had a conveyance for this land, legal in form, but that he actually paid for the land. It is not sufficient that he may have secured the payment of the purchase-money. 

He must have paid it in fact before he had any notice of appellee's prior equitable title. That is an essential element in the equity, which must exist in order to support appellant's claim, which he attempts to uphold. If he has not paid the purchase-money, no wrong is done him by taking from him a legal title, which has cost him nothing. The answer does not aver that any part of the purchase-money has ever been paid, and he has failed to show that any was paid. It can not, therefore, be said that the appellant had any equity to support his legal title, and consequently, he ought not to retain it against the equitable title of the complainant. This is the view taken by this court in the case of Monn et al. v. Welsch, 18 Ill. 347, and is applicable to the facts of this case. The justice of the case is manifestly with the appellees, and the decree, in their favor was right and must be affirmed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 279, par. 30.

GRIMSTONE v. CARTER 

New York Court of Chancery, 1832, 3 Paige 421, 436.

THE CHANCELLOR. * * * The complainant was in possession of the north half of the lot No.

54, at the time of the purchase by Seymour and 151

Welles, under a written agreement from Carter to reconvey that part of the lot to him, upon the payment of security of the small balance due on the mortgages over and above the $1,600 which Carter was to pay for the south half of the lot. And these purchasers from Carter took their conveyance, and paid the purchase money for the whole lot, after they had actual notice of the complainant's possession; and without making any inquiries as to the extent of his rights, or the nature of that possession. 

It is the settled doctrine of this court that where the equities of the parties are equal, and neither has the legal title, the one who has the prior equity must prevail. Nor will this court permit the party having the subsequent equity to protect himself by obtaining a conveyance of the legal title, after he has either actual or constructive notice of the prior equity. * * To protect a party, therefore, and to enable him to defend himself as a bona fide purchaser for a valuable consideration, he must aver in his plea, or state in his answer, not only that there was an equal equity in himself, by reason of his having actually paid the purchase money, but that he had also clothed his equity with the legal title before he had notice of that fact, it is sufficient to put him on inquiry as to the actual rights of such possessor, and is good constructive notice of those rights. * **

The order appealed from must be reversed, with costs. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 280, par. 31. (Par. 4) 

JOHNSTON v. SPICER 

New York Court of Appeals, 1887, 107 N. Y. 185. 

RUGER, C. J. ** The respondents assert that no claim is made that rights of action escheat to the people, and such seems to have been the theory entertained by general term. In the strict sense of the term escheat, perhaps, this may be so, but we assume it to be the law in this state that all rights of property, of whatever nature they may be, revert to the people when the owner dies intestate, and there is a failure of heirs or next of kin, to take such property. We believe it to be the established rule in all civilized countries that, in such case, the property of a resident dying intestate without heirs, reverts to the sovereign or state, to be administered for the general benefit of the community in which he dies. While there is an absence of specific statutory authority declaring the rights of the state in such property, it is believed to be the uniform practice for it to assume by force of natural law, the control of such property, and to administer it for the benefit of those concerned, and, in the absence of any legal heir, to appropriate the proceeds to the uses of the state. 

It is said, in 4 Kent's Commentaries 425, "It is a principle which lies at the foundation of the right of property that, if the ownership becomes vacant, the right must necessarily subside into the whole community in whom it was originally vested when society first assumed the elements of order and subordination." In a note, it is stated, "the escheats spoken of in the text relate exclusively to land; movables never escheated in the technical sense; and if the owner died intestate and left no lawful representatives, the personal estate remained at the disposition of the crown. In this country it must be in the state, and so the statute law in some of the states has specially provided." In Perry on Trusts (para. 327), it is said that it was 152

held in Burgess v. Wheate (1 Ed. 177), "that if the cestui que trust left no heirs, the trust estate did not escheat, but that the trustee thenceforth held the estate discharged of the trust." "This is upon the principle that there is no want of a tenant to the land, the trustee being clothed with all the rights of ownership, against all the world except the cestui que trust and those claiming under him. But this principle does not apply to chattels where there can be no tenant, nor to leaseholds, nor to an equity of redemption. In the 

United States, trustees would hold personal property subject to the right of the state as ultima heires (ultimate heir) in case the cestui que trust died without heirs or next of kin, and it is conceived they would hold real estate under the same rule.” Washburn on Real Property (vol. 3, p. 49) says: "While escheat was regarded as an incident of federal tenure, it did not extend to the equitable estate of cestui que trust, and, by analogy, it is generally understood that if a cestui que trust dies intestate, without heirs, the trust fails, and the trustee holds an absolute estate in the property free from the claim of anyone. 

But it is settled by the courts of Maryland, and intimated by Judge Kent in respect to New York, that such would not be the case under the statutes and that if a cestui que trust should die without heirs, his equitable estate would escheat to the state.

From this review of the law it would seem that there is no substantial difference between real and personal property in respect to the rights acquired by the state, upon the death of its owner, intestate, without heirs or next of kin. A clear deduction from the authorities seems to lead to the conclusion that the doctrine of escheat applies only to legal estates and does not in a strict sense affect either equitable estates or personal property. 

It seems also to follow from the authorities cited, that upon the death of Ellen Spicer the state took not the land, but succeeded to the equitable right which she had to a conveyance thereof. This right may possibly be subject to the claims of the creditors, or other equities which would have to be adjusted in an action by the equitable owners to recover the possession of the land.

The omission in the provisions of the revised statutes of the words "died seised of" as contained in the revised laws of 1813 relating to escheats is not supposed to have effected any change in the law as the revisors say in their note to this section that it is "new in terms but implied in revised laws (380, para. 2).” A new rule, however, was intended to be introduced by para. 2 of the revised statutes, which provides that all escheated lands shall be held by the state or its grantees subject to the same trusts, etc., to which they would have been subject had they descended. This enactment was intended to obviate the severe rule of the common law by which such lands when escheated were held to belong to the king free from the trust. (Revisors' Notes, 5 N. Y. Statutes at Large (Edm. Ed.), 297.)

With reference to the personal estate of persons dying intestate without next of kin, it appears to have been the uniform practice of the state since its organization to take such property, and hold it either for the benefit of the community at large or some division of the state, or to be returned to such persons as may from considerations of natural justice and equity seem to the legislature to be entitled thereto. 

We think, therefore, that the property left by Mrs. Spicer reverted to the state upon her death, and that it was competent for the legislature to grant the rights thereby acquired, and the right to administer thereon to 153

such person or persons as in their discretion they judged equitably entitled thereto. (Englishbe v. Helmuth, 3 N. Y. 294.) 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 282, par. 35. (Par. 5) 

CHACE v. CHAPIN 

Massachusetts Supreme Court, 1881, 130 Mass. 128.

COLT, J. This is a bill in equity in which the plaintiff seeks to recover a dividend on certain shares of the capital stock of the Old Colony Railroad Company, now standing in the name of Chapin and Braley, as the assignees in bankruptcy of Samuel A. Chace. He claims to be entitled to the income of this stock for life, by virtue of and in accordance with the terms of a trust created by Mrs. Holmes when she transferred to said Samuel a legal title to the stock.

The defendants deny the existence of the trust; and Chapin and Braley, as assignees in bankrutcy, contend that, as holders for value in good faith and without notice, they are entitled to have the stock discharged of any trust that may have existed while the title was in the bankrupt. 

The case was sent to a special master to find the facts, and, upon his report, it is clear that, at the time of his failure, Chace held the stock charged with the trust upon which the plaintiff relies. 

As to the claim of the assignees in bankruptcy, that the trust, if it ever existed, was discharged by the conveyance of Chace to Brayton as trustee, to secure his debts to the Union Mills and others, on the ground that Brayton took as a holder for value without notice, the first answer is, that at common law an assignee under a general assignment for the benefit of creditors takes no better title and no higher right than the assignor himself had, and is not to be regarded as a purchaser for a valuable consideration without notice. If the assigned estate is subject to a trust, the assignee takes subject to the rights of the equitable owner. 

A further answer to the claim is that the conveyance to Brayton was wholly defeated by the subsequent bankruptcy of Chace. The assignees took, not as purchasers of Brayton's title, but under their right as assignees in bankruptcy, and subject to all the legal and equitable claims of others. 

The statute declares that "no property held by the bankrupt in trust shall pass by the assignment." US Rev. Sts., para. 5053. “Assignees in bankruptcy do not, like heirs and executors, take the whole legal title in the bankrupt's property. They take such estate only as the bankrupt had a beneficial as well as legal interest in, and which is to be applied to the payment of his debts." 

Decree for the plaintiff. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 283, par. 37. (Par. 5) 

MOORE'S ESTATE 

Pennsylvania Supreme Court, 1909, 198 Pa. 611.

HANNA, P. J. From the record it appeared that Andrew M. Moore died 

January 26, 1898, leaving a will dated January 21, 1898, which was duly 154

admitted to probate. 

By the will, after bequeathing certain legacies, he created a spendthrift fund for each of his three sons for life, remainder over in each case to the sons or son who might survive the cestui que trust, and remainder, after the death of the last of three sons, to his executors, the Fidelity Insurance, Trust and Safe Deposit Co., Joseph F. Sinnott and Walton Pennewill, Esq., in trust, "to found and maintain such charitable and educational institution or institutions, in my name, as they, in their discretion, may deem wise, proper and expedient."

Before the auditing judge it was claimed that as testator had died within one calendar month from the date of his deed, and that as the charitable bequest had thereby failed, the trust estates for the sons had merged in the remainders vested in them under the intestate laws, and that the sons were entitled to their shares free of the trust. The auditing judge held that the spendthrift trusts were not affected by the failure of the charitable bequest. 

Exceptions to the adjudication were dismissed in an opinion by Penrose, J., which was in part as follows: "Nor is it less clear, notwithstanding the very able and learned arguments of counsel for the exceptants, that the failure of the charitable provisions of the will, intended to take effect at the death of the survivor of the testator's sons, by reason of his death within a calendar month after its execution, did not affect the trusts for the sons, even though, by reason of such failure, the sons themselves became entitled at the expiration of the trusts to the principal of the estate. Whatever may be the law on this subject in other states of the union or in England, it is too well settled to be an open question in Pennsylvania, that a trust to protect from creditors of a beneficiary, or from his own improvidence, annexed to an estate for life or any estate less than one of inheritance, is not only valid, but, as said in Norris v. Johnson, 5 Barr 289, to be regarded with favor, because prompted by "the kindliest feelings of nature, by enabling a benefactor to provide for an unfortunate and improvident person, by means which are his own, and which he ought to be permitted to apply as he pleased," the governing maxim being cujus est dare ejus est disponere (an owner has the right to give as he wishes). 

Ashhurst v. Given, 5 W. & S. 323. The right of alienation, whether by voluntary act or by adverse proceeding, is an inseparable incident of ownership in fee, and in consequence, where the beneficiary is entitled to the absolute interest, such a trust can not be created (Keyser's Appeal, 7 P. F. Smith 236); but it is not an inseparable incident of a life estate, and hence the manifest distinction. It is said, however, that the life estates given to the sons by the will were merged in the fee or absolute interests in the remainder or reversion which they took under the intestate laws by reason of the failure of the gift to charities, and consequently the case falls within the doctrine of Keyser's Appeal, thus entitling the sons to the estate discharged of the trust. But merger can only take place where the interest meeting in the same person are of the same character, either both legal or both equitable; obviously it can not take place where the life estate is under a trust which the law recognizes as valid, and the ownership of the remainder is legal. 

It is a principle so familiar as scarcely to require reference to authority, that where two estates meet, merger will not be permitted if the purposes of the creation of one of them require that they should remain distinct. 

An equitable life estate will not coalesce with a legal remainder to the 155

heirs of the first taker and thus cause a trust to protect from creditors to fail, any more than it will for the purpose of giving to the equitable tenant for life the power to dispose of the estate. * * 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 283, par. 37. 

ITHELL v. MALONE 

New York Supreme Court, 1915, 154 N. Y. Supp. 275.

BENEDICT, J. The motion made by the defendant for confirmation of the second report of the official referee to whom this matter was referred should not be granted. The defendant occupied a fiduciary relation towards the plaintiff, which required, not only the utmost good faith in dealing justly with her principal, but also imposed upon her the obligation of keeping proper accounts of her stewardship. She apparently kept none with any regularity, either accurate or otherwise, but depended upon fragmentary and imperfect memoranda and the recollection of her subagent or her own memory to enable her to make up the unverified statement of account which she presented to the learned referee herein. The testimony in support of that statement is too dubious to have the probative force which it has received in a case where the burden rested upon the defendant to show clearly that she was dealing honestly. 

In White v. Rankin, 18 App. Div. 293, 46 N. Y. Supp. 228, affirmed in 162 

N. Y. 622, 57 N. E. 1128, the circumstances presented many similarities to those here in so far as the nature of the account was concerned. The appellate division in this department (Mr. Justice Hatch writing) said: "It appeared upon the trial before the referee that Rankin kept no regular books, and that his accounts were in a very unsatisfactory state, some being found in a journal book, some in checks, and some in a check book, from which he testified, but which does not seem to have been produced upon the trial. For many of his expenditures he had no vouchers, and his verbal account of a large number of items is confused, in some respects contradictory, and upon the whole very unsatisfactory. The general rule of law applicable to a trustee burdens him with the duty of showing that the account which he renders and the expenditures which he claims to have made were correct, just, and necessary. * * * He is bound to keep clear and accurate accounts, and if he does not the presumptions are all against him; obscurities and doubts being resolved adversely to him. ** * We find no equitable consideration present in this case which call in any respect for a mitigation of this rule."

In the present case the defendant claimed that she made payments which amount in the aggregate to $19,182.87, and for these payments, covering a period of about four years, she produced no vouchers-neither checks, check books, nor receipts, except for $1,527.57 paid to the plaintiff at different times, and another item of $297.50 on certain notes. She claims to have received from rents of many furnished apartments sums aggregating $16,126.40 according to the referee report. As to her receipts, she kept no regular book of accounts, but made up her account from memoranda not in existence at the time of the hearing. To quote from the testimony which her husband, who acted as her agent, gave: 156

"From papers, yes, sir; slips of paper and hearsay; that is what we thought about that time."

Much as I dislike to refuse confirmation to the report of one of the official referees of this court, I think that no account resting upon such shadowy and unsubstantial basis as this should have been approved. Plaintiff's exceptions must be sustained. For this reason there should be a rehearing, and under the circumstances and in justice to the plaintiff it should be before another referee. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 286, par. 41. 

In re SALMON, PRIEST v. UPPLEBY 

In the Court of Appeals, 1889, 42 Chancery Div. 351.

COTTON, L. J. This is an appeal by the plaintiff from a decision of Mr. 

Justice Kekewich dismissing an action brought against Uppleby, a retired trustee of the will of Eliza Salmon, to make him responsible for an improper investment.

There are two questions to be considered. The first is, whether the investment in question was wrongful. It was within the terms of the trust, for it was an investment on mortgage of a freehold estate. In one sense, therefore, it was in accordance with the trusts, and if the trustee took due care as to its sufficiency there would be no breach of trust, and nobody could complain, though it ultimately proved insufficient. The case differs from that of an investment not within the terms of the instrument, which is necessarily a breach of trust, so that if any loss occurs the trustees must be liable for it. 

The question here is, whether Uppleby took proper care in seeing to the sufficiency of the security.

Now as regards the rule which has been so much discussed, as to the amount which may be lent on a given security, the law is thus summed up in Learoyd v. Whiteley, 12 App. Cas. 727, 733: "As a general rule the law requires of a trustee no higher degree of diligence in the execution of his office than a man of ordinary prudence would exercise in the management of his own private affairs. Yet he is not allowed the same discretion in investing the moneys of the trust as if he were a person dealing with his own estate. 

Business men of ordinary prudence may, and frequently do, select investments which are more or less of a speculative character; but it is the duty of a trustee to confine himself to the class of investments which are permitted by the trust, and likewise to avoid all investments of that class which are attended with hazard. So, so long as he acts in the honest observance of these limitations, the general rule already stated will apply. The courts of equity in England have indicated and given effect to certain general principles for the guidance of trustees in lending money upon the security of real estate. 

Thus it has been laid down that in the case of ordinary agricultural land the margin ought not to be less than one-third of its value; whereas in cases where the subject of the security derives its value from buildings erected upon the land, or its use for trade purposes, the margin ought not to be less than one-half. I do not think these have been laid down as hard and fast limits up to which trustees will be invariably safe, and beyond which they can never be in safety to lend, but as indicating the lowest margins 157

which in ordinary circumstances a careful investor of trust funds ought to accept." These rules are there recognized, though they have been impeached by Mr. Warmington and Mr. Wood. In the present case the value of the property was mainly derived from buildings. I do not think that the valuation of the property has been successfully impeached. We must take the property as having been worth 1,750 pounds. The trustees lent 1,300 pounds upon it. 

Now, we must have regard not only to the value, but to the nature of the property. It consisted of small houses let at weekly rents, and we know the class of tenants likely to be attracted by cottage property in Hull. It was certainly not prudent to lend to this extent upon property the value of which depended on laborers' houses being wanted in that part of Hull. 

The investment, therefore, was a breach of trust as having been made improvidently. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 287, par. 42. 

MARKEL v. PECK 

Missouri Court of Appeals, 1910, 144 No. App. 701.

COX, J. Action for damages for breach of contract, trial by jury and verdict for plaintiff. * **

The court, in sustaining the motion for a new trial in this case, recited that it was by reason of the error of the court in giving instruction number one on behalf of the plaintiff. This instruction told the jury that if they should believe from the evidence that on or about said 6th day of March, 1903, said trustees, defendants herein, or a majority of them, authorized Stephen Peck & Bro. to execute the contract for lease, read in evidence, and that said Stephen Peck and Bro. did execute such contract, and that plaintiff had fully performed or offered to perform, its conditions upon his part, and that the defendants, or a majority of them, had refused to perform the same in accordance with the terms thereof, then the verdict should be for the plaintiff, and unless they should so find the facts, the verdict would be for defendants. Appellant insists that this instruction was correct under the evidence, and that the verdict was for the right party, while respondents insist that the instruction was wrong for the reason that the defendants as trustees had no power to delegate their authority, and for that reason, could not appoint an agent to execute a contract and further that the authority of the agent, if permissible at all, must be in writing, and that a trustee, either by himself or an agent, could not execute a lease to begin in future. 

The first proposition that confronts us in this investigation is as to whether or not those defendants, trustees under the will of Charles H. Peck, invested with the power to manage and control the estate committed to their charge, and to execute leases thereon, could delegate that power by appointing an agent to attend to that matter for them. 

The general rule is that a trustee of an express trust, invested with powers, the execution of which calls for the exercise of discretion and judgment on the part of the trustee, can not delegate such powers to any one, and, hence, the performance of any act, requiring the exercise of discretion, must be done by the trustee himself and can not be delegated to an agent. * **

The office of trustee is one of personal confidence and can not be delegated. 

The reason of the rule lies in the fact that the grantor who creates a trust 158

and invests the trustees with powers, calling for the exercise of discretion on the part of the trustee in their execution, selects the trustee by reason of his confidence in the integrity and good judgment of the trustee, and when the trustee accepts the trust, he does so with the implied understanding that he will discharge the duties incumbent upon him, by reason of the trust, according to his own best judgment, and hence, unless the grantor expressly provides that the trustee may delegate the powers conferred, he can not do so. 

He may delegate authority to perform a purely ministerial act; that is, an act not requiring the exercise of discretion, for there is not a delegation of the trust. "The trustee must, at times, act through attorneys or agents, and, if he determines in his own mind how to exercise the discretion and appoints agents or instruments to carry out his determination, he can not be said to delegate the trust, even though deeds or other instruments are signed by attorneys in his name.” * * *

It has been uniformly held in this state that trustees, appointed in a deed of trust, to make sale of land conveyed therein, can not delegate the power to make the sale, and the reason assigned is well stated by WAGNER, JUDGE, In Graham v. King. 50 Mo. 22, as follows: "The office and duties of a trustee are matters of personal confidence and he must exercise a just and fair discretion in doing whatever is right for the best interest of the debtor; he must in person supervise and watch over the sale, and adjourn it if necessary, to prevent a sacrifice of the property, and no one can do it in his stead unless empowered thereto in the instrument conferring the trust. A trustee can not delegate the trust or power of sale to a third person, and a sale executed by such delegated agent is void."

If this rule should pervail in the matter of a sale of land for the purpose of collecting a debt, under a power granted in a deed of trust, in which the duty of the trustee, in executing the trust, is specifically provided, it should, for much stronger reason, apply to a trustee charged with the management of a large estate for a long term of years which necessarily requires the constant exercise of vigilance and discretion.

In this case, the will under which these trustees were acting made no provision whatever for a delegation, by them, of any of the powers conferred upon them under the will, and our conclusion is that they possessed no power to appoint an agent, either verbally or by writing, and shift to the agent the performance of any duty requiring the exercise of any discretion upon their part; and as the execution of a contract, such as the one sued upon in this case, necessarily called for the exercise of some discretion and judgment, it could not be executed in a way to bind the estate or these defendants in their capacity as trustees, by any agent which they might appoint. True, they might if they had agreed upon the contract themselves, settled its terms and agreed upon every question requiring the exercise of discretion or judgment, delegate to an agent the naked power to sign the contract, but that is not this case. The evidence in this case wholly fails to show that the trustees made this contract. The contract purports upon its face to have been executed by an agent of these defendants, and plaintiff tried his case upon that theory. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 288, par. 44. 159

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 9 

1. How did the doctrine of constructive trust arise? 

2. T conveys trust land to X in violation of the trust; under what circumstances may X keep the land? 

3. Suppose X buys trust land from T who acts in violation of his trust; X gets the title and gives notes and a mortgage to T for the purchase price. May X keep the land? 

4. Suppose X pays T all the purchase money in cash and does not get title till after he hears of the trust; may he keep the land? 

5. X buys trust land from T, gets title and pays the full purchase price without notice of the trust; he then conveys the title to 

Y who knows of T's breach of trust in selling the trust land to 

X. May Y keep the land? 

6. If T dies without heirs, what becomes of the legal title to the trust land? If C (the beneficiary) dies intestate without heirs what becomes of the beneficial interest? 

7. What is a spendthrift trust? When is it valid? 

8. May the creator of a trust validly stipulate that C is not to get the legal title to the trust land till he is thirty years old? 

9. T validly sells trust property for $2,000 cash; what should he do temporarily with the cash? What ultimately? 

10. What duties must T perform himself? What duties may he delegate to others? 160

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 10 CONVEYANCE OF INTERESTS IN LAND 1. Forms of Conveyance 

The English Statute of Uses was passed in 1534 (see ante, Lesson VII, I). Before that date present freehold estates—that is, estates in fee simple, estates in fee tail or for life were conveyed by what was called livery of seisin, the conveyance being usually accompanied by a document called a deed of feoffment. The conveyance was a ceremony which took place on the land conveyed; the phrase "livery of seisin" meant, simply, delivery of possession, so that at that time the transfer of freehold interest in land was like the transfer of a chattel today by way of gift, which requires either delivery of the chattel or delivery of a deed to the chattel. 

On the other hand, incorporeal-that is, non-possessory-interests in land, such as reversions, remainders (see ante, Lesson VI, VI), and easements (see post, Lesson XVIII, I) could not, of course, be conveyed by livery of seisin; they were transferred by deeds of grant containing simple words of transfer. And so were estates in land less than freehold that is, terms for years (see ante, Lesson VI, II). A common use of a deed of conveyance occurred where A was in possession, having an estate for years or for life, and B, the owner of the fee in remainder or reversion, conveyed the fee to A by deed of release. Another use of a deed occurred when B, having an easement of way over A's land, conveyed the easement to A by deed of release.

After the Statute of Uses, livery of seisin, which required a public ceremony on the land, fell into disuse and was superseded by methods of transfer which could be secret. Thus the common conveyance of a freehold estate at the time came to be the bargain and sale, the validity of which depended upon the deed containing a recital of consideration-usually, today, of $1.00. Another method 161

of conveyance which could be used between near relatives by blood or marriage—for example, to a son or daughter or son-in-law, etc. -was what was called a covenant to stand seised. 

The great practical advantage of these two forms of conveyance was that they could be secret-no public ceremony was required. 

But only a year later, in 1536, Parliament passed the Statute of Enrollments which required that bargain and sale deeds of freehold interests should be enrolled on a public registry in order to be valid. 

In order to evade this statute, conveyance draftsmen then used a bargain and sale of a lease for one year-which did not need to be enrolled because a lease for a year did not create a freehold estatewith a deed of release of the reversion in fee to the bargainee, thus giving him the complete legal title. This conveyance was called a lease and release, and because it could be secret it came into wide use in England. But in this country the Statute of Enrollments was not copied, so there was no occasion to resort to the lease and release. 

Thus conveyance by bargain and sale came to be most common form of conveyance in the United States. 

In most states today there are statutes providing for short forms of deeds. These contain simple words of transfer like the common law deed used to transfer reversions, remainders, easements and terms for years. These may ordinarily be employed, instead of the bargain and sale or covenant to stand seised, to convey any interest in land. Even apart from such statutes, courts try to uphold conveyances in spite of irregularities in form. For example, if the parties are not related so that the deed cannot take effect as a covenant to stand seised, and there is no recital of consideration, so that it cannot take effect as a bargain and sale, it may take effect as livery of seisin if it was actually delivered on the land. 

2. Covenants in Deeds 

The common law deed of release under seal and the modern quitclaim deed which is commonly used where the grantor does not wish to bind himself by any warranty, are mere conveyances; they contain no element of contract because they purport to convey merely whatever title or interest in the land the transferor may happen to have. But the commonly used warranty deed binds the transferor by contract to warrant the title; it is not merely a conveyance. 

Deeds formerly were usually under seal, and the promises contained in them were called covenants-that is, promises under seal. The usual warranty deed contains some or all of the following six covenants: (1) of seisin-that is, that the transferor has title; 162

(2) of right to convey-for example, that he is not merely a trustee of the land; (3) against encumbrances, such as mortgages, liens or easements; (4) of quiet enjoyment-that is, a promise that the transferee's possession will not be interfered with either by the transferor or anyone else; (5) for further assurance—that is, that the transferor will make any further conveyances necessary to make the transferee's title complete; (6) of warranty; that is, if the transferee suffers damage because of having an incomplete title the transferor will make good the damage.

Of these six covenants some are broken, if at all, as soon as made; others cannot be broken till later. Thus, the covenant for further assurances cannot be broken till it becomes necessary for the transferor to execute another deed of conveyance in order to make perfect the transferee's title. The covenant of quiet enjoyment and the covenant of warranty cannot be broken until the present or some future transferee is turned out of possession by one holding a better title to the land than the warrantor. Because these covenants cannot be broken immediately they are transferred or they "run" with the land into the hands of the transferee of the original covenantee.
