Miami Beach in three decades has been phenomenal, in large part if not entirely, due to its attractiveness to those who would escape business cares, the rigors of northern winters and the ravages of disease. Situated on a narrow peninsula between the Atlantic Ocean and an arm of it known as Biscayne Bay, blessed with a warm climate and fanned by the southeast trades, it has become known as one of the earth's principal vacation places. 

A preponderant number of those who live there for long or short periods have come to play. It is primarily a business or industrial center to which residence is incidental, but on the contrary it is principally populated by those who seek pleasure or health and business is secondary. 323

The general zoning plan so far as it relates to the immediate territory involved in this controversy provided for the construction of superior hotels and apartments along the Atlantic Ocean, unquestionably one of the main assets of the community, and for business houses along Lincoln Road which runs approximately at right angles with the beach. Where these two areas of considerable length, but of little width, unite, is situated the property of the plaintiff. The relief asked, and granted by the chancellor, was permission to erect business places in the region restricted to hotels and apartments at its juncture with the area where that use could be made of the property and it is insisted that the character of the property in question is precisely the same as that along the remainder of Lincoln Road from that place west. If we disregard the relative importance of the area along the beach and that along the business street, this is true. If one travels along 

Lincoln Road eastward and does not bear in mind the restricted area along the seashore, there appears no logical reason why the last two blocks next to the Atlantic Ocean should not be in the same category as those along the remainder of the path he has just trod. By the same token if one passes along Collins Avenue, a thoroughfare from north to south through the city, there seems no sensible ground for intersecting the territory with a street devoted to trade. If the character of the city as a resort and the necessity to its well-being, of its charm for the stranger are borne in mind; if the comparative importance of store and hotel are considered when the area allocated to the one strikes the district assigned to the other and a choice must be made, the former should give way. 

At the juncture of these streets property which may be devoted to business abruptly ends and that which may be used for hotels and apartments as suddenly begins but there must be a line of demarcation between them somewhere. *** The fact that his land is situated across the street from that on which commercial enterprises may be operated was not alone enough to support plaintiff's position that he should be given the same latitude in the use of his property. Were this the case it would be but a matter of time before alternations of the whole scheme by successively liberalizing the use of abutting property would result in disintegration and disappearance of the whole plan of zoning. 

It is fundamental that one may not be deprived of his property without due process of law, but it is also well established that he may be restricted in the use of it when that is necessary to the common good. So in this case we must weigh against the public weal, plaintiff's rights to enjoy unhampered property acquired since the enactment of the ordinance. Such restrictions must find their basis in the safety, health, morals or general welfare of the community. * ** 

In judging the merits of a controversy of this kind the facts peculiar to the particular case will govern and the court will not substitute its judgment for that of the legislative body of the city. In view of the nature of Miami 

Beach it is not important to consider here the indispensability of the restrictions to the health, the safety, the morals of the community but only their necessity to the general welfare. We do not comprehend how it can be successfully urged that the maintenance of safety, health or morals are involved but only whether in the circumstances of this particular case the restrictions are so unnecessary to the general welfare of the inhabitants that the curtailment of the rights of the plaintiff are unreasonable and arbitrary. 324

We will determine the reasonableness of the regulations as applied to the factual situation meanwhile keeping before us the accepted rules that the court will not substitute its judgment for that of the city council; that the ordinance is presumed valid, and that the legislative intent will be sustained if "fairly debatable."

In 3 McQuillin Municipal Corporations, Section 1048, appears a quotation from the Supreme Court of Wisconsin (State ex rel. Carter v. Harper, 182 

Wis. 148) recognizing the principle that government through "the exercise of its police power may impose restrictions upon the use of property in the interest of public health, morals, and safety." The court added that there was doubt about whether there could be regulation "in promotion of the public welfare, convenience, and general prosperity” but it seems that the Supreme Court of Florida has recognized "public welfare" as a purpose for which such restrictions could be imposed. In the Wisconsin case it is further pointed out that aesthetic considerations have also been recognized and we think what is said in the opinion is particularly relevant to the community of Miami Beach because of its general character which we have briefly described. It is difficult to see how the success of Miami Beach could continue if its aesthetic appeal were ignored because the beauty of the community is a distinct lure to the winter traveler.

In the Wisconsin case reference is made also to the many benefits which may spring from zoning such as the attraction to select citizenship, civic pride, the happiness and contentment of the citizens and the stabilization of the value of the property and general peace and good order. All of these elements are especially appropriate in connection with the City of Miami Beach.

It seems that if one observes the forest instead of the trees, the advantages of the zoning ordinance under attack are immediately apparent and that the enforcement of the ordinance encompassing the whole plan will redound to the benefit of all without oppression or injustice to plaintiff or any one in like position.

We do not find in the ordinance, meanwhile conscious of the presumption of the correct exercise of the discretion of the city council to deal with zoning within their jurisdiction, the earmarks of arbitrariness and unreasonableness which would justify judicial interference.

The limitation of the use by the plaintiff of his property seems a fair, just and reasonable contribution to the economic good, the prosperity, the welfare of the whole community and not so burdensome that it contravenes the organic inhibitions against deprivation without due process. Reversed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 450, par. 9. 325

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 19 INSURANCE 1. Making the Insurance Contract 

The purpose of insurance, the importance and extent of which has increased many times since 1900, is to distribute and equalize losses due to death, to fire, to accident, to storm, to floods or to any of the many other vicissitudes of life. The distribution is made, of course, through the payment of insurance premiums or assessments, among those who are subject to similar perils. 

The contract of insurance is usually unilateral, the insurer undertaking the risk in consideration of the payment, in advance, of the premium by the insured, but occasionally the risk is undertaken though the insured has merely promised to pay the premium. This is the only real promise made by the insured; the application for insurance or the insurance policy itself may contain stipulations which are in form promises by the insured, but the breech of such "promises" does not give rise to any cause of action against the insured but at most gives the insurer a defense to an action brought against it to collect the insurance. 

Since in making the insurance contract the insurer must rely largely, if not entirely, upon the statements made in the application for insurance, the applicant is required, in making such statements, to exercise a high degree of good faith; and since the insurer is the one who draws up the contract and the contract is usually long and detailed, and the insurer is in a position to protect itself by putting stipulations in the policy, a high degree of good faith is required of it so as to prevent it from taking advantage of the insured. 

Even though a contract for insurance may extend over many years it is not within the statute of frauds as to contracts not to be performed within a year, and unless some other statute forbids, the contract may be oral; but it is usually embodied in a formal written 327

instrument-the policy-which ordinarily becomes effective only upon delivery and upon the happening of any conditions precedent to its taking effect. Today most persons do not go to the trouble of reading insurance policies so it is held that the insured's failure to read a policy does not prevent the insured from having the policy corrected if, through mistake, it does not express the real contract. 

2. Insurable Interest-Life Insurance 

The purpose of insurance is the distribution and equalization of loss; hence a contract of insurance usually requires that the insured shall have an insurable interest in the subject matter of the insurance contract; if he has no such interest the contract is illegal and unenforcible as amounting merely to a wager or bet. 

One may insure his own life and he may name anyone as beneficiary even though the one named has no pecuniary interest in the continuance of the life insured, the danger that the beneficiary may murder the insured being regarded as too slight to render the policy invalid. Similarly he may take out insurance payable to himself or to his estate and assign it to one who has no insurable interest in the insured's life if the transaction is entered into in good faith. If he assigns it to a creditor to secure a debt the interest of the creditor is limited to the amount of his claim. 

A husband may insure the life of his wife because even though, by recent statutes, he may not have a legal right to her services, he usually has a reasonable expectation of receiving them. A wife may insure the life of her husband because he is under a legal obligation to support her. And the reasonable expectation of such support is probably enough where the marriage unexpectedly turns out to be invalid. Where there is a valid insurance policy between husband and wife a later divorce does not invalidate it. A woman may insure the life of her fiance.

A parent may insure the life of his minor child because of the legal right to the services of such child; and the minor child may insure the life of the parent because of the latter's obligation of support. Where the child is an adult there is an insurable interest between them only if there is a reasonable expectation of services or of support; and similar reasoning applies between brother and sister or any other close relationship. 

Where there is held to be an insurable interest the amount of the insurance is not limited to the value of that interest; it may be in any amount. Because although life insurance is founded upon the general principle of indemnity against loss, the contract itself is 328

not a contract of indemnity but to pay a fixed amount. 

A creditor may insure the life of his debtor provided that the amount of the policy is not so much greater than the amount of the debt as to show a want of good faith on the part of the creditor; and the fact that before the debtor's death the debt has been paid does not invalidate the policy. Similarly, a surety may insure the life of the principal debtor. And where there is a real pecuniary interest to be protected a partner may insure the life of his co-partner, a corporation the life of its president or director and an employer the life of his employee. It is the better view that apart from small policies taken out on the lives of young children the consent of the person whose life is insured is necessary to the validity of the contract. 

3. Same-Other Insurance 

Generally one has an insurable interest in property when the destruction of or damage to the property would cause him loss. This includes: (1) The holder of the legal title to property whether in fee or a life estate or an estate for years, or in reversion or remainder; it includes also the beneficiary of property held in trust; it includes also the trustee of property held in trust, it being his duty to insure for the benefit of the beneficiary and whatever he recovers in case of loss he will hold subject to the trust. (2) The holder of an equitable property right such as the right of a purchaser under a specifically enforcible contract to buy the property; the purchaser also has an insurable interest where the circumstances are such that the contract may be specifically enforced against the owner by him. Where property is pledged or mortgaged both pledgor and pledgee or mortgagor and mortgagee have insurable interests. In case of a mortgage on land the mortgage usually provides that the mortgagor shall keep the buildings on the land insured for the benefit of the mortagee "as his interest shall appear."

In such a case if there is a loss the mortgagee has a first claim against the proceeds up to the amount of the mortgage debt. (3) One who has a lien on property or who has merely possession of property has an insurable interest. 

On the other hand, a prospective heir, who has a mere expectancy, has no insurable interest in the property which he expects to inherit, nor has a creditor an insurable interest in property belonging to his debtor.

A floating policy on a fluctuating stock of goods is valid. And a building contractor may insure the building he is erecting though 329

the value of the unfinished building increases each day, because, by the law of contracts, the loss will fall upon him if the building is destroyed before it has been completed and accepted. 

4. Estoppel 

Suppose A has a life insurance policy in X Co. and he tells the agent of the company that he is about to enter military service and the agent assures him that the stipulation against his entering military service will not be enforced and relying upon this A enters such service and is killed, the X Co. is usually liable on the policy; as it is usually expressed, the X Co. is estopped from taking advantage of the stipulation because of the representation made to A by its agent, provided the agent was acting within the apparent scope of his authority in making the representation. 

Similar reasoning applies where B has a fire policy with the Y Co. and he notifies the agent of the Y Co. that he has been violating the stipulation against keeping gasoline on the premises and the agent then tells him that the policy is still valid and B in reliance upon the agent's statement does not take out another policy and there is a loss due to the gasoline.

If at the time that A's life policy was delivered to him he was already in military service and the agent of the X Co. knew it; or if when B's fire policy was delivered to him he was keeping gasoline on the premises and the agent of the Y Co. knew it, the weight of authority and the better view is that the insurers are estopped because of the representations implied from the failure of the agents to object. 

5. Waiver

In the case of an ordinary bilateral contract the operation of the doctrine of waiver is usually easy to understand. If there is an express condition in the contract which is exclusively for the benefit of one of the parties he may waive it-that is, throw it away-and he will ordinarily do this if it is to his advantage to do so. A contracted to buy X's crop of tobacco then growing in the field, at 10 cents a pound, but if as much as one-fourth of the crop should be frostbitten when harvested, A was entitled to cancel the contract. 

When X's crop was harvested more than one-fourth of it had been frostbitten so that A might have cancelled the contract but it was not to his advantage to do so because the price of tobacco had advanced to 15 cents a pound, so A waived the condition and insisted upon getting the tobacco and was held entitled to do so, the condition being for the sole benefit of A. 330

Where an insurance contract is bilateral, that is, where the insured merely promises to pay the premium, and there is a proviso that in case of default the company may cancel the policy, the company may waive its right to cancel and maintain an action for the premium because the stipulation was for its exclusive benefit. 

Where an action is brought against the insurance company to enforce the policy and the company sets up in defense the breach of a condition by the insured, if the plaintiff can show a promise by the company to waive the condition and that promise was based upon some consideration so as to make it a valid contract, such a contract is a good reply to the defense of breach of condition and the company will be held liable in the policy. 

Where there is no contract to waive the enforcement of a condition but there is an express assurance, either at the time of the delivery of the policy or later, that the company will not take advantage of the breach of a condition, and the insured acts upon such assurance the company is estopped by its assurance. Such cases of estoppel are frequently called waivers; and the cases are very few where a company has been held liable in spite of a breach of a condition unless there was either a contract to waive the breach and assurance that it would not be taken advantage of, followed by action in reliance thereon. 

6. Subrogation 

If A insures his building against fire with the X Company and the building is later burned through the negligent conduct of Y and the X Company pays the loss, it is subrogated to the right of A against Y because, as between one who is bound merely on a contract of indemnity and one who is liable because of his negligent conduct, the latter should, as a matter of fairness, bear the loss. If the X Company should recover more from Y than the amount it paid to A on the policy, it must account for that excess to A. (Subrogation means that the X Company is entitled to the assignment from A of A's rights against Y; that is, it is entitled to stand in A's position as it was the moment before the X Company paid A.)

If A takes out insurance on his life for $10,000 with the C Company and then is killed through the negligent conduct of Z, the C Company is not entitled to be subrogated to any claim against Z even though there is a statute providing that the claim for wrongfully killing A survives his death. And there is a similar holding with regard to insurance against accident causing personal injuries; but the Workmen's Compensation Acts usually proide for subro- 331

gation of the employer to whatever claims the employee may have against any third party who has wrongfully caused the injury. 

7. Suicide 

Where A takes out a policy on his own life and later kills himself while insane the insurance company is everywhere held liable unless there is an express stipulation in the policy contract. If he kills himself while sane and the policy was taken out with the intention of committing suicide the insurer is everywhere held not liable because of the fraud. If there was no such fraud the weight of authority is that the company is liable; and if there is a clause in the policy which exempts the company in case of suicide it is construed to include any cases of suicide while sane. As to what constitutes insanity in this connection the minority rule is that the insured is insane if unable to understand the physical consequences of his act; the majority rule is that he is insane if he does not understand the moral quality of his act. In order to escape the broader liability of the majority rule policies sometimes include a provision against suicide "sane or insane" and under this any intentional act of self-destruction excuses the insurer. 

8. Incontestable Clauses

As a means of reassuring the prospective policy holders that after his paying premiums for several years, the beneficiary-after loss of testimony of the insured and perhaps others—may have to contest in an expensive lawsuit the validity of the policy, there is a provision in many policies making the policy incontestable either from the date of issue or after a fixed period, usually one or two years from such date. The provision does not, of course, prevent the insurer from showing that the insured had no insurable interest or that the loss was due to a risk excepted in the policy, or that conditions to be performed after loss, such as giving notice of loss within a certain period, have not been complied with. Nor does it, where the provision is that the policy shall be incontestable from the date of issue, prevent the insurer from setting up that the policy was procured by fraud. The death of the insured during the prescribed period of one or two years does not prevent the incontestable clause from becoming operative at the end of the period. 

9. Construction of the Insurance Contract-Formalities 

The insurance contract was formerly drawn up by the insurer and usually printed in small type; hence the courts construed the contract strictly against the insurer, and this rule of construction has 332

been carried over to standard policies required by statute. For example, words of description of the property covered by a fire insurance policy are construed liberally in favor of the insured; and the phrase "unconditional and sole ownership" has been held to be satisfied though the insured's interest in the property was merely equitable and subject to encumbrances. 

Apart from statute an insurance contract need not be formal or even in writing; and it is the custom of insurance companies to issue temporary "binders" which give the insured protection while the insurer investigates the risk and decides whether to issue the policy. Such binders may be oral and they include all the obligations covered by the policies in customary use by the insurer. 

10. Extent of Insurer's Liability 

An insurer against fire is liable for damage caused directly by hostile fires; this includes damage caused by water used to extinguish the fire, the expense of removing insured goods to a place of safety and thefts committed during such removal. 

It is no defense that the fire was caused by the negligent conduct of a third party or even of the insured himself. 

If ignition takes place the insured may recover for damage due to charring or blistering.

The insurer is not liable for loss caused by friendly fires such as burn in a stove or furnace unless the fire escapes and becomes a hostile fire-for example if it escapes and sets fire to soot in a chimney and thereby ignites the building. 

In some states the standard fire policy provides that the insurer may cancel the policy five days after giving notice of cancellation. 

Fire policies frequently provide that satisfactory proofs of loss must be made within sixty days. In some states an inexcusable failure to comply voids the policy; in others it merely postpones the right of action against the insurer.

It is quite common to provide that action must be brought on a policy within one year after date of loss and this stipulation is usually held valid. 388

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 2) 

AMERICAN EMPLOYERS' LIABILITY INS. CO. v. BARR 

Circuit Court of Appeals of the United States, Eighth Circuit, 1895, 68 F. 873.

In Error to the Circuit Court of the United States for the District of Colorado.

This was an action by William P. Barr against the American Employers' 

Liability Insurance Company on a policy of insurance. The plaintiff recovered judgment in the circuit court. Defendant brings error. Affirmed. 

Before Cladwell, Sanborn, and Thayer, Circuit Judges.

THAYER, C. J. * * *The insured sustained certain injuries on May 20, 

1892, by falling from a platform in a building which was in process of construction in the city of Denver, and died four days thereafter, as it is claimed, from injuries resulting from such fall. A suit was brought on the policy by William P. Barr, the defendant in error, to whom, under the aforesaid provisions of the policy, the same was made payable in the event of the death of the assured, and a judgment was recovered against the defendant company for the sum of $5,626.58. ***

The remaining assignment, which we deem is necessary to notice, relates to a portion of the charge whereby the court instructed the jury, in substance, that the plaintiff, William P. Barr, had the right to recover on the policy without proof of an insurable interest in the life of his deceased uncle. It will be observed that the policy in suit was taken out by Cousley, that the premium was paid by him, and that the indemnity promised in case of injuries not resulting in death was made payable to Cousley; in other words, the contract was made by Cousley with the insurance company for his own benefit. Barr was named as the person to receive and receipt for the amount due on the policy only in the event that the assured sustained injuries which resulted in his death. Whether Barr was a creditor of his uncle, or whether the deceased intended that Barr should receive and retain the amount paid on the policy as a gratuity, or should collect it as trustee, for the benefit of the assured's wife and children, was not expressly stated in the policy, and was not proven on the trial. 

We are of the opinion, however, that it was not necessary to allege or prove either of these facts. The insurance was obtained by the deceased on his own life, obviously for his own benefit. He had the right to designate the person to whom the indemnity should be paid in case of an injury resulting in death, and having done so, and the company having agreed to pay the indemnity to the person thus designated, it cannot now insist that such person shall prove an insurable interest in the life of the deceased, as a condition precedent to a recovery. The policy sued upon is not a wager contract, but was valid when made, and is still valid, even if it be true that Barr is not a creditor of the deceased.

The record in the case discloses no error, and the judgment of the circuit court is therefore affirmed. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 375, par. 2. 334

(Par. 2) 

RUSE v. MUTUAL BENEFIT LIFE INS. CO. 

Court of Appeal of New York, 1861, 23 N. Y. 516. 

SELDEN, J. *** Our inquiry, therefore, is, whether at common law independent of any statute, it is essential to the validity of a policy, obtained by one person for his own benefit upon the life of another that the party obtaining the policy should have an interest in the life insured. 

A policy, obtained by a party who has no interest in the subject of insurance, is a mere wager policy. Wagers in general, that is, innocent wagers, are, at common law, valid; but wagers involving any immorality or crime, or in conflict with any principle of public policy, are void. To which of these classes, then, does a wagering policy of insurance belong?

Aside from authority, this question would seem to me of easy solution. 

Such policies, if valid, not only afford facilities for a demoralizing system of gaming, but furnish strong temptations to the party interested to bring about, if possible, the event insured against. In respect to insurances against fire, the obvious temptation presented by a wagering policy to the commission of the crime of arson has generally led the courts to hold such policies void, even at common law. It was so held in England, at an early day, and by Lord Hardwicke, in Saddlers' Company v. Badcock, 2 Atk. 557; and the courts in this country have generally acquiesced in and approved of the doctrine. In this state such policies would fall under the condemnation of our statute avoiding all wagers and gambling contracts of every sort; but they would, no doubt also be held void, independently of that statute, at common law. * * *

In regard, however, to marine insurances, a different rule seems to have prevailed in England; and the cases of the Clendinning v. Church, 3 Caines, 141, Juhel v. Church, 2 Johns. Cas. 333, and Buchanan v. Ocean Insurance

Company, 6 Cow. 318, are supposed to have established the same rule in this state. No reason, that I am aware of, has ever been given for this difference between fire and marine policies. The latter, when of a wagering character, are vicious and evil in their tendencies as well as the former, and have been generally considered as noxious and dangerous, whenever the question has arisen. They should, therefore, as it would seem, for the reason applied to policies against fire, have been held void, as contrary to public policy.

The distinction between these two classes of policies, is, in my view, a mere matter of accident, and grew out of the peculiar manner in which the question was presented in respect to marine policies. The case of Epaba v. Ludlow, Comyn. 361, shows how the doctrine, that wagering policies upon ships are valid, originated. The defendant there had insured the plaintiff, "interest or no interest." On the trial it was objected that the plaintiff could not recover, unless he had a property in the ship; but the court said that the insurance was good and that the plaintiff had no occasion to prove his interest. Had the question been directly presented in this case, whether a mere wagering policy was valid, the decision would, I think, have been different. 

The case itself shows the court to have supposed that the plaintiff actually had an interest; and it is apparent, from the authorities, that it had always been previously held, in suit upon policies not containing the words "interest *335

or no interest," or other equivalent words, that the plaintiff must aver and prove that he had an interest. This is distinctly asserted by Lord Hardwicke, in the case of Sadlers' Company v. Badcock, supra; and in the case of Crauford v. Hunter, 8 Term, 14, the counsel, on looking into the precedents at the request of the court, found that it had been the uniform practice, in suits upon marine policies, to insert an averment of interest. To me, therefore, it seems clear, that the decision in Epaba v. Ludlow was made because the court failed to distinguish between a waiver of proof at the trial, which the defendant was, of course, at liberty to make, and a waiver in the policy itself, by which it was converted into a mere wager. 

In consequence of this case, and others which followed it, Parliament was forced to interfere, as it did, by the act of 19 George II (chapter 37), reciting the mischiefs which had arisen from the making of marine insurances, "interest or no interest," and prohibiting them thereafter; and when the question subsequently arose in Crauford v. Hunter, supra, as to the validity at common law of a mere wagering policy upon a ship, it was held to be valid, solely upon the authority of the recitals in this act. It was in this indirect way that the doctrine in question, as to marine policies, first crept into the law. It was important to show this, because the effect of what I consider as the inadvertence of the Court in Epaba v. Ludlow was not confined to policies upon ships. It must have been, I think in consequence of the doctrine initiated by that case, that it came to be understood in England that, in insurances upon lives, it was not necessary at common law that the party to be benefitted by the policy should have any interest in the life insured. There may not have been any direct decision to that effect; yet, that such was the prevalent impression, is to be inferred from the enactment of the statute of 14 George III (chapter 48), prohibiting insurances upon lives where the person insuring had no interest in the life. Angell, in speaking of this statute says: "At common law it seemed to have been thought unnecessary that, at the time of effecting the policy, the assured should have had any interest which might be prejudiced by the happening of the event insured against." In New Jersey they have no such statute; and the question now to be decided, therefore is, whether the impression which seems to have prevailed in England prior to the statute of 14 George III was well founded.

That impression does not appear to be supported by any adjudged case. 

Life insurance seems not to have been practised to a great extent in England until a comparatively modern date, and the probability is, that as soon as such insurance became frequent, the evils of gambling in them was so apparent that Parliament interposed, upon the assumption that the same rule would be applied to them as to insurances upon ships. I cannot regard that act as affording any very strong evidence, that, at common law, wagering policies upon lives were valid. It seems to me, that, were the naked question presented, whether such a policy comes within the admitted exception to the validity of wagers in general, that is, whether it is repugnant to a sound public policy, no court, not hampered by some unfortunate or mistaken precedent, would hesitate for a moment in holding the affirmative. In 

Massachusetts, in Vermont, in Pennsylvania, and I believe other states, it has been held so in regard to wager policies in general. But policies without interest, upon lives, are more pernicious and dangerous than any other class of wager policies; because temptations to tamper with life are more mis- 336

chievous than incitements to mere pecuniary frauds. 

Chancellor Kent was evidently embarrassed by the position of this question in England. He commences his remarks on the subject by saying that "the party insuring must have an interest in the life insured," and then immediately refers to the English statute, of 14 George III, chapter 48, but says not a word upon the question whether at common law an interest was necessary. He however, concludes by saying that "the necessity of an interest in the life insured, in order to support the policy, prevails generally in this country, because wager contracts are almost universally held to be unlawful, either in consequence of some statute provision, or upon principles of the common law." 3 Kent, Com. 368.

This obscure manner of treating the subject is plainly to be attributed to the reluctance of the learned author to admit (notwithstanding the impression that appears to have obtained in England) that gambling in life insurance could be tolerated at common law. That impression has been here traced, as I think, with justice to marine policies. It has never, that I am aware of, been recognized and adopted by any American court, and is so obviously repugnant to the plainest principles of public policy, that it is somewhat surprising that it should ever have existed. My conclusion, therefore, is, that the statute of 14 George III, avoiding wager policies upon lives was simply declaratory of the common law, and that all such policies would have been void, independently of that act. 

It is said that the defendants, by issuing the policy upon the representation of the plaintiff that he had an interest, have admitted his interest, and that the production of the policy is at least prima facie evidence of such interest. 

This position cannot be sustained. All the older authorities show, that even in actions upon marine policies, not containing the clause "interest or no interest," it was necessary to aver, and of course to prove, the interest of the plaintiff. It is an indispensable part of the plaintiff's case, to be made out affirmatively at the trial. Upon this ground, therefore, as well as that before considered, the judgment of the Supreme Court must be reversed; and there must be a new trial, with costs to abide the event. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 375-6, par. 2. 

(Par. 3) RIGGS v. COMMERCIAL MUT. INS. CO. 

Court of Appeals of New York, 1890, 125 N. Y. 7.

Appeal from superior court of New York city, general term. 

Action by John S. Riggs against the Commercial Mutual Insurance Com pany. Defendant issued to Joseph L. Tobias a policy of insurance upon the steamer Falcon for $1,000, loss payable to Andrew Simonds. Tobias was, at the time of effecting this insurance, a stockholder in the Merchants' Steam- 

Ship Company, which then owned the steamers Sea Gull and Falcon. Simonds, by an indorsement on the policy, directed the insurance company to "pay to John S. Riggs." Plaintiff recovered judgment at special term, which was reversed at general term, and a new trial ordered. *** 

ANDREWS, J. *** The question whether a stockholder in a corporation, as such, has an insurable interest in the corporate property, which he may protect by an insurance of specific, tangible property of the corporation, is 337

the question now presented. The policy does not disclose the nature of the interest of Tobias in the vessel insured; but this was not necessary, unless required by some condition of the policy. The policy, if otherwise valid, attached to whatever insurable interest he had, whether as owner or otherwise. What constitutes an insurable interest has been the subject of much discussion in the cases, and is often a question of great difficulty. It is quite apparent that the tendency of decisions in recent times is in the direction of a more liberal doctrine upon this subject than formerly prevailed. Contracts of insurance where the insured had no interest were permitted at common law, but the manifest evils attending such contracts, and the temptation which they afforded for fraud and crime, led to the enactment in England of the statute 19 Geo. II c. 37. prohibiting wager policies, and this was followed by the enactment in this state of a similar statute prohibiting wagers. 

But to prevent the application of the statute to cases of insurance by way of security and indemnity it was provided that it should "not be extended so as to prohibit or in any way affect any insurances made in good faith for the security or indemnity of the party insured, and which are not otherwise prohibited by law." It would seem, therefore that whenever there is a real interest to protect, and a person is so situated with respect to the subject of insurance that its destruction would or might reasonably be expected to impair the value of the interest, an insurance on such interest would not be a wager within the statute, whether the interest was an ownership in or a right to the possession of the property, or simply an advantage of a pecuniary character, having a legal basis, but dependent upon the continued existence of the subject. It is well settled that a mere hope or expectation which may be frustrated by the happening of some event, is not an insurable interest.

The stockholder in a corporation has no legal title to the corporate assets or property, nor any equitable title which he can convert into a legal title. 

The corporation itself is the legal owner, and can deal with corporate property as owner, subject only to the restrictions of the charter. But stockholders in a corporation have equitable rights of a pecuniary nature, growing out of their situation as stockholders, which may be prejudiced by the destruction of the corporate property. The object of business corporations is to make profits through the exercise of the corporate franchises, and gains so made are distributable among the stockholders according to their respective interests, although the time of the division is ordinarily in the discretion of the managing body. It is this right to share in the profis which constitutes the inducement to become stockholders. So also, on the winding up of the corporation, the assets, after payment of debts, are divisible among the stockholders. It is very plain that both these rights of stockholdersviz., the right to dividends and the right to share in the final distribution of the corporate property-may be prejudiced by its destruction. In this case the ships were the means by which profits were to be earned and their loss would naturally, in the ordinary course of things, diminish the capacity of the corporation to pay dividends, and consequently impair the value of the stock. The same would be true in other cases which might be mentioned; as, for example, where buildings producing rent, owned by a corporation, should be burned. It is not necessary to constitute an insurable interest, that the interest is such that the event insured against would necessarily subject the 338

insured to loss. It is sufficient that it might do so, and that pecuniary injury would be the natural consequence.

The question now before us was considered by the supreme court of Iowa in the case of Warren v. Insurance Co., 31 Iowa, 464, 7 Am. Rep. 160. The court, in a careful opinion, reached the conclusion that a stockholder in a corporation had an insurable interest in the corporation's property. In 

Philips v. Insurance Co., 20 Ohio, 174, there is an adverse dictum, but the decision went on another ground. In Wilson v. Jones, L.R. 2 Exch. 139, the action was upon a policy in favor of the plaintiff, a shareholder in the Atlantic Telegraph Company, a company organized to lay the Atlantic cable. The court construed the contract as an insurance of the plaintiff in respect to the adventure undertaken by the company to lay the cable, and it was held that his interest as shareholder was an insurable interest, and likened it to an insurance on profits. It is difficult to perceive any good reason why, if a stockholder could be insured on his shares in a corporation against a loss happening in the prosecution of a corporate enterprise, he could not insure specifically the corporate property itself embraced in the adventure, and prove his interest by showing that he was a shareholder. 

The question here is, did the plaintiff have an insurable interest covered by the policy? The amount of damages is not in question. Except that the parties have taken that question out of controversy, the extent of the loss would be a question of fact to be ascertained by proof, and the recovery up to the amount insured would be measured by the actual loss. We are of opinion that the view that a stockholder in a corporation may insure specific corporate property by reason of his situation as stockholder, stands upon the better reason, and also that it is in consonance with the current of authority defining insurable interest in our courts. The cases of Herkimer v. Rice, 27 N. Y. 163, Rohrback v. Insurance Co., 62 N. Y. 47, 20 Am. Rep. 451, and National Filtering Oil Co. v. Citizens' Ins. Co., 106 N. Y. 535, sustained policies upon interests quite as remote as the interest now in question. It would be useless reiteration to restate the particular facts and grounds of the decisions in these cases. 

It is sufficient to refer to them, and to say in conclusion that it seems to us, both upon authority and reason, that the insurance now in question is not a wager policy, but is a fair and reasonable contract of indemnity, founded upon a real interest, though not amounting to an estate, legal or equitable, in the property insured.

The judgment should therefore be affirmed. All concur. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 376, par. 3. 

(Par. 4) LUMBER UNDERWRITERS OF NEW YORK v. RIFE Supreme Court of the U. S., 1915, 237 U. S. 605. 

MR. JUSTICE HOLMES delivered the opinion of the court: 

This is a suit upon a policy insuring lumber for one year from May 22, 1909. The policy contained a warranty by the assured that a continuous clear space of 100 feet should be maintained between the lumber and the mill of the assured, and also a provision requiring any waivers to be written upon or attached to the instrument. The lumber was burned during the year, but it appeared by the undisputed evidence that the warranty had been broken, and the judge directed a verdict for the defendants. It appeared, 339

however, that the policy was indorsed, "No. 27,868, renewing No. 27,566," and the plaintiffs offered to prove that pending the earlier policy the defendants had the report of an inspection that informed them of the actual conditions, showing permanent structures between where some of the lumber was piled and the mill, that made the clear space in this direction less than 100 feet, and that with that knowledge they issued the present policy and accepted the premium. This evidence was excluded, subject to exception. But it was held by the circuit court of appeal that the jury should have been allowed to find whether the defendants had knowledge of the conditions and reasonable expectation that they would continue, and so had waived the warranty. For this reason the judgment was reversed. 

When a policy of insurance is issued, the import of the transaction, as everyone understands, is that the document embodies the contract. It is the dominant, as it purports to be the only and entire, expression of the parties' intent. In the present case this fact was put in words by the provisio for the indorsement of any change of terms. Therefore when, by its written stipulation, the document gave notice that a certain term was insisted upon, it would be contrary to the fundamental theory of the legal relations established to allow parol proof that at the very moment when the policy was delivered that term was waived. It is the established doctrine of this court that such proof cannot be received. * **There is no hardship in this rule. No rational theory of contract can be made that does not hold the assured to know the contents of the instrument to which he seeks to hold the other party. The assured also knows better than the insurers the condition of his premises, even if the insurers have been notified of the facts. If he brings to the making of his contract the modest intelligence of the prudent man, he will perceive the incompatibility between the requirements of 100 feet clear space and the possibilities of his yard, in a case like this, and will make a different contract, either by striking out the clause or shortening the distance or otherwise, as may be agreed. The distance of 100 feet that was written into this policy was not a fixed, conventional formula that there would be trouble in changing, if the insured would pay what more, if anything, it might cost. Of course, if the insured can prove that he made a different contract from that expressed in the writing, he may have it reformed in equity. 

What he cannot do is to take a policy without reading it, and then, when he comes to sue at law upon the instrument, ask to have it enforced otherwise than according to its terms. The court is not at liberty to introduce a short cut to reformation by letting the jury strike out a clause. 

The plaintiffs try to meet these recognized rules by the suggestion that after a contract is made, a breach of conditions may be waived, "void" only meaning voidable at the option of the insurers. ***; that this policy was a renewal of a former one, and that the case stands as if, after the breach of warranty had been brought to the notice of the insurers, a premium had been paid and accepted without a new instrument. But what would be the law in the case supposed we need not consider, as in our opinion it is not the one before us. The policy in suit is a document complete in itself. The indorsement that we have quoted is probably only for history and convenient reference. We see no ground for attributing to it any effect upon the. contract made. The fact that the policy has a provision for renewal has no bearing, and we do not perceive how it would matter if the previous one had the same. No use was made of the clause. Therefore, in our opinion 340

the principles that we have laid down apply to the present case, and the action of the District Court was right in not allowing recovery on the policy. 

Judgment reversed. 

The Chief Justice, Mr. Justice McKenna, and Mr. Justice Day are of opinion that the Circuit Court of Appeals properly disposed of the case and dissent. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 379, par. 7. (Par. 6) 

SUTTLES v. RAILWAY MAIL ASS'N Supreme Court of New York, Appellate Division, Fourth 

Department, 1913 156 App. Div. 435, 141 N. Y. 1024

ROBSON, J. Defendant has been cast in damages for the breach of an agreement with plaintiff which provided, for a good consideration therein expressed, that, if plaintiff should receive bodily injuries during the continuance of the certificate, issued by it to him, through external violent and accidental means, which should wholly and continuously disable him from following the occupation of a railway postal clerk, he should be entitled to receive from defendant's benefit fund a sum not exceeding $15 per week "for loss of time resulting from bodily injuries effected through the means aforesaid." The single point made by appellant is that, although the defendant may have been proved to be otherwise liable, yet as it was not on the trial permitted to assert and prove as a defense to the action the fact that plaintiff had already received in full settlement of an action which he had theretofore brought against the railroad company, which he alleged negligently caused the injury, a sum considerably in excess of the amount of his claim against this defendant, and had thereupon given the railroad company a general release of his claim, reversible error was committed, and a new trial must be had. 

Defendant claims that this accident insurance contract is, like contracts for fire or marine insurance, one of indemnity only, and that, if liability to pay thereon arose by reason of injury occasioned by the negligent act of a third person, it would, on payment of the amount required by its contract, be entitled to be subrogated to the extent of such payment to any right of action for the loss or injury which plaintiff had originally against the wrongdoer who caused it. It is true that this stipulated sum which plaintiff seeks to recover in this action is later in the insurance certificate referred to as a weekly indemnity. But this use of the term "indemnity" as the correspondent or equivalent of a payment under the contract can mean no more than that such payment is to be regarded as compensation for the damage to plaintiff's insurable interest, which the defendant by its contract agrees to pay at the rate and to the amount stated in the certificate. Calling the payments an indemnity is a mere matter of nomenclature, and does not affect the substance of the contract, nor change its legal effect. Appellant's counsel does not question this, for he says: "Although the policy here refers to the sum payable as weekly indemnity, what the parties choose to call it is not determinative of the nature of the contract."

The question is, then: Is this contract for accident insurance one for indemnity only? The theory and basis of the right of the insurer to subrogation is that the insured has a claim against a third person, which he could enforce for the loss insured against. But, although the assured may be 341

able to recoup his loss, partially or in full, by enforcing some contract, or other right, he may have, which is not dependent upon the direct responsibility of such person for the loss insured against, that fact does not clothe the insurer with the right of subrogation thereto, at least in the absence of an agreement in the policy to that effect. Even in fire insurance contracts, recognized, as they are, to be indemnity contracts, it does not follow in all cases where there has been a loss that the insurer is entitled to be subrogated to every means available to the insured for recouping his loss. Examples of such cases are Foley v. Manufacturers Ins. Co., 152 N. Y. 131, 46 N.E. 318, 43 L.R.A. 664; Michael v. Prussian Nat. Ins. Co., 171 N.Y. 25. These cases illustrate and apply the principle, as to the right of subrogation, which I have sought to state above It follows that, treating defendant's contract liability as that of an indemnitor, if the loss insured against is not the same loss for which plaintiff had a right of action against the wrongdoer, defendant had no right of subrogation to such claim. Referring again to the certificate of insurance issued to plaintiff, it will be seen that defendant in terms insured him for total disability due to bodily injuries caused by external violent and accidental means by reason of which he might be prevented from performing his duties as mail clerk. Under the express terms of his contract plaintiff was entitled to recover, at the rate fixed, for the disability resulting in loss of time, regardless of the pecuniary loss he might sustain. It was not the loss of earning capacity, loss of wages, nor the pain or personal disfigurement resulting from the injury, nor necessary expenses for medical or hospital attendance, that were to be covered by the insurance. Though his wages might continue, as they did in this case, to be paid by his employer during the period of his disability, though the pain or personal disfigurement might have been much or little, though the bills for hospital and medical service might have been large, or, on the other hand, merely nominal, yet, if such disability resulted in continuous loss of time, the contract required payment of the stipulated weekly sum. But it is these very matters, that are not within the contract of insurance, which are the basis of an award of damages against the negligent author of the injury. For that reason it would seem that defendant had no right of subrogation to plaintiff's claim against the wrongdoer.

But we are further of the opinion that an accident insurance contract is not an indemnity contract. As was written in Gatzweiler v. Milwaukee 

Electric Ry. & L. Co., 136 Wis. 34, in which case the court was concerned in determining the quality of a similar contract for accident insurance: "A policy of casualty insurance, ordinarily, has much the same features as one of life insurance, though, it is true, it more nearly than one of life insurance has the indemnity feature. The amount stipulated to be paid is a fixed sum as to each particular injury specified, or is computable without any such definite data, as in the case of the loss of property." 

"The opinion concludes: 

"That such a policy is an investment contract, giving to the owner or beneficiary an absolute right, independent of the right against any third party responsible for the injury covered by the policy; that if such a company desires protection against loss caused by the wrongs of a third person, who would ordinarily be liable, they must do so by the contracts they make; that, in the absence of a feature expressly making the policy of in- 342

surance an indemnity contract, it should not be regarded as such, but held to be an investment contract, in which the only parties concerned are the insurer and the assured or the beneficiary." * * *

Our attention has not been called to any case deciding that accident insurance is an indemnity contract. Some general statements to that effect have been made by text-writers, but they do not seem to be supported by authority. Some expressions found in the opinion in Travelers' Ins. Co. v. Great 

Lakes Engineering Works Co., 184 Fed. 426, are pointed to by appellant's counsel as indicating that accident insurance is a contract of that kind. But the court in that case was considering an employer's liability policy, and these expressions were obiter.

The judgment and order should be affirmed, with costs. All concur. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 380, par. 9 (Par. 7)

NORTHWESTERN MUT. LIFE INS. CO. v. JOHNSON NATIONAL LIFE INS. CO. OF MONTPELIER, VT., v. MILLER Supreme Court of the United States, 1920, 254 U. S. 96 MR. JUSTICE HOLMES delivered the opinion of the Court. 

These are suits upon policies issued to George P. Johnson upon his life, payable in the first case to his wife, in the second to his executors or administrators. The wife and the administrator respectively recovered in the District 

Court and the cases having gone to the Circuit Court of Appeals the latter has certified certain questions to this court. The policy payable to the wife contained a provision that "if within two years from the date hereof, the said insured shall * * * die in consequence of a duel, or shall, while sane or insane, die by his own hand, then, and in every such case, this policy shall be void."

Johnson, the insured, died by his own hand more than two years after the date of the policy. The first question put in the wife's suit is whether the above provision, there being no other in the policy as to suicide, makes the insurance company liable in the event that happened. The second is in substance whether the contract if construed to make the company liable is against public policy and void.

The policy payable to the administrator had no provision as to suicide but did agree that "this contract shall be incontestable after one year from date of its issue, provided the required premiums are duly paid." 

Johnson's suicide was more than a year after the date of this policy. The first question propounded is whether the above provision prevents the insurer from denying liability in this case, it not appearing that Johnson was insane when he killed himself. The second is whether such a contract which makes no exception for death resulting from suicide is against public policy, and therefore void. There is a third as to a possible distinction between insurance payable to the wife and that payable to the estate of the insured which will not need to be discussed.

The public policy with regard to such contracts is a matter for the states to decide. This case qualified the statement in Ritter v. Mutual Life Ins. Co., 169 U.S. 139, to the effect that insurance on a man's own life payable to his estate and expressly covering suicide committed by him when sane would be against public policy. The point decided was only that when the contract 343

was silent there was an implied exception of such a death. There was evidence that the insurance was taken out with intent to commit suicide, and it plainly appeared that the act was done by the hand of the insured for the purpose of enabling his estate to pay his debts. The application, although excluded below, warranted against suicide within two years, within which time the death took place. So that all the circumstances gave moral support to the construction of the policy adopted by the court in accordance with the view that has prevailed in some jurisdictions as to the general rule. In Burt v. Central Life Ins. Co., 187 U.S. 362, it was held that there was a similar tacit exclusion from the risk assumed of the death of the insured by execution for murder, and the same decision was reached in Northwestern Mutual Life 

Ins. Co. v. McCue, 223 U.S. 234, * * * But the question here does not concern implied exceptions, it concerns the effect of express undertakings which as we have said depends upon the policy of the state. 

The certificates do not disclose in what states these contracts were made but it is not necessary to postpone our answer on that account. It appears from 

Whitfield v. Aetna Life Insurance Co., supra, that some legislatures have thought it best to insist that life insurance should cover suicide unless taken out in contemplation of the deed. But the case is much stronger when a considerable time is to elapse before the fact that the death was by the insured's hand ceases to be a defense. The danger is less sinister and probably a good deal smaller than the danger of murder when the insurance is held by a third person having no interest in the continuance of the life insured, yet insurance on the life of a third person does not become void by assignment to one who has no interest in the life. When a clause makes a policy indisputable after one or two years, the mere evocation of a possible motive for self-slaughter is at least not more objectionable than the creation of a possible motive for murder. The object of the clause is plain and laudable-to create an absolute assurance of the benefit, as free as may be from any dispute of fact except the fact of death, and as soon as it reasonably can be done. It is said that the insurance companies now generally issue policies with such a clause. The state decisions, so far as we know, have upheld it. Unless it appears that the state concerned adopts a different attitude we should uphold it here. * * 

We are of opinion that the provision in the first mentioned document avoiding the policy if the insured should die by his own hand within two years from the date is an inverted expression of the same general intent as that of the clause in the second making the policy incontestable after one year, and that both equally mean that suicide of the insured, insane or sane, after the specified time shall not be a defense. It seems to us that would be the natural interpretation of the words by the people to whom they are addressed, and that the language of each policy makes the company issuing it liable in the event that happened. ***

Mr. Justice Day took no part in the decision of these cases. 

See Clark, Summary of American Law, p. 381, par. 10. 344

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 19 Questions 1. What is the purpose of insurance? 

2. In making the contract what is required of the insured? Of the insurance company? 

3. May an insurance contract be oral? 

4. May one insure his own life and assign the policy to his creditors? May the creditor insure the life of his debtor? 

5. May one insure the life of one's husband? Of one's wife? Of one's child? 

6. May the holder of a life estate in property insure it? A reversionary interest? May a mortgagee? A trustee? The beneficiary of a trust? A prospective heir? 

7. When may an insurance company be estopped from contending that it is not liable? 

8. What is meant by the right of the insurer to subrogation? 

9. If the insured kills himself must the insurer pay? 

10. What is the effect of an incontestible clause in a policy? 345

REAL ESTATE LAW LESSON 20 LANDLORD AND TENANT-ADVERSE POSSESSION- FIXTURES MISCELLANEOUS 1. Landlord and Tenant-Duties of Landlord 

At what time does the relation of landlord and tenant arise? 

Usually upon the giving and acceptance of a valid lease; it is not necessary that the lessee take possession under it. 

Though there is no express covenant in the lease that the tenant is to have quiet enjoyment of the premise and that the landlord has the right to execute the lease, the landlord is liable therefor, upon implied covenants. But there is no implied covenant that the premises are in a tenantable condition; if the tenant wishes to be protected on that point, he should insist upon an express covenant. 

Must the landlord keep the premises in repair? No, unless he makes an express covenant to repair or there is a statute-as there is in some states-imposing that obligation. Apart from an express covenant by the lessee, the landlord must pay taxes on the leased property. 

2. A. Duties of Tenant 

Unless there is an express covenant by the landlord, or a statute shifting the duty to repair to the landlord, the tenant must keep the premises in repair. Suppose that A leases to B at $100 a month rent; then X notifies B that he, not A, is the owner of the leased premises and that the rent must be paid to him; what is B to do about it? He must still pay to A, unless and until he has been ousted by X, or, upon threatened ouster by X, he has in good faith recognized X's title and agreed to pay rent to him; the technical term is "attorned."

Suppose A, after leasing the land to B, wrongfully puts B out of possession of all the land leased; in such case, B's liability 346

to pay rent comes to an end. If A wrongfully ousts B from only a part of the premises, B need pay no rent at all until such ouster comes to an end. If B is ousted from a part of the premises by X, B is excused only proportionally from paying rent. Suppose the building on land leased by A to B is destroyed, does this excuse B from paying rent? No, unless there is an express provision to that effect in the lease, because, for the period of the lease, he is regarded as owner of the land. But suppose that A has leased to B only an apartment in the building and the building burns. Then B is excused because, in such a case, he does not become owner of the land. 

B. Breach of Duty by Either Party; Remedy of Each 

Suppose that the premises become uninhabitable, either because of A's maintaining a nuisance on adjoining property, or because of his breach of some other duty to the tenant, what is B's remedy? 

He may either move out and quit paying rent, or he may continue in possession and get a judgment against A for damages. 

Suppose that B fails to pay rent, what remedies does A have? 

He has a choice of three: (1) he may get a judgment for the amount of rent due; (2) he may distrain, that is, seize any chattels on the premises and sell them and apply the proceeds toward the payment of his claim; (3) he may have B ousted by a sheriff or constable under the forcible entry and detainer statutes. If he himself ousts B, he becomes liable to be prosecuted for a crime and in some states to be sued in tort for the damage done to B. The remedy of distraint has in some states been abolished; in others, it has been modified. 

3. Assignments and Subleases 

Suppose A leases a piece of land to B for four years; a year later B transfers his interest under the lease to X; may A collect rent from X? Yes, until X in turn assigns his interest under the lease to Y. And A may during that time hold Y liable for the breach of any other covenant in the lease that runs with the land; (see ante, lesson XVIII). And this is true even though X does not take possession of the land or agree with B to perform the covenants. 

But, of course, if he does undertake with B to perform the covenants he remains liable throughout the rest of the period of the lease, no matter how many times the leasehold interest may be assigned. 

Similarly B, the original lessee and covenantor, remains liable throughout the whole period.

Suppose that, instead of B assigning his interest in the lease to X, he merely sublets to him for two years, thereby reserving the 347

last two of the four years for himself. In such a case X, being a mere sublessee does not become liable to A; his sole liability is to 

B on the sublease. 

Suppose that in the lease from A to B there is a covenant by B that he will not assign his interest under the lease; does this prevent his subletting to X? No. And, conversely, if he covenants not to sublet it does not prevent his assigning. Suppose that A leases to B for three years at $1200 a year rental; at the end of a year A assigns his interest in the land to C; does this give C the right to collect the rent from B? Yes; but suppose A assigns his interest in the land to C, reserving the right to collect the rent, and then assigns the latter right to D; may that be validly done? Yes. 

4. Surrender-Eviction by Summary Proceedings 

If A is willing to accept, B may put an end to his rights and obligations under the lease by surrendering the premises to A; but the statute of frauds requires that an express surrender shall be in writing unless the lease is for a brief period. But there may be implied surrenders which are, of course, not affected by the statute. For example, if A gives and B accepts a new lease which is inconsistent with the old one; for example, at a higher rent and for a longer period, or if A should accept some third person as lessee in place of B, or if B should abandon the premises and A should enter and occupy the premises for himself. But A may avoid the legal effects of a surrender by giving notice to B that he is entering and leasing to a new tenant for the rest of the term of the lease in order to reduce the liability of B to himself on the lease. Where at the end of the term B refuses to give up possession, A may by summary proceedings under the forcible entry and detainer statute get possession of the premises promptly-usually in five days. 

5. Adverse Possession-Constructive Possession 

Suppose A enters upon B's land and occupies it openly and continuously for the statutory period-usually either twenty or fifteen years-all the time claiming that it is his own; at the end of the statutory period, title to the land passes from B to A. B has no right to complain; he should have contested the matter before the end of the period, by bringing an action of ejectment to get possession. Does it make any difference whether or not A knew the land did not belong to him? By the better view, no; but in some states he does not get title if he intended to occupy only 348

what he thought was his own. When such adverse possession is taken, it is usually under color of title; that is, under a document purporting to be a deed or will conveying the land to A; but this is not necessary. But if he does take possession under color of title, say to 160 acres, claiming all of it, he will get title to all of it at the end of the statutory period, even if he occupies and cultivates only a part, say 20 acres of it. This doctrine of constructive adverse possession would probably not apply if A had occupied only two acres for the statutory period; the disproportion would be too great. 6. Tacking Disabilities Suppose A enters upon B's land and occupies for six years and then transfers the possession to X, who occupies for fourteen more, does X get title at the end of his fourteen years? Yes. He is allowed to tack A's six years possession to his own fourteen years. 

The same rule applies, whether B was A's heir or devisee or transferee by deed or by a mere oral transfer of possession. Suppose that when A started to occupy B's land, B was a minor or insane; in such a case, if the statutory period has fully run and B is still a minor or insane, B is entitled, by express stipulation in the various statutes, to a further period, usually two years after the disability of minority or insanity is removed, in which to bring his action. But this rule applies only to a disability existing at the time his possession began; if at that time B was a sane adult and later died leaving as heir C, a minor or an insane person, such minority or insanity will have no effect upon the running of the statutory period. 

7. Fixtures-(A) Annexation 

If A brings onto his land lumber, plaster and other building materials, and builds an ordinary residence thereon, the materials thereby cease to be chattels and become a part of the land; that is, they become fixtures. But suppose he brings onto the land a movable garage or a movable residence, does that become also a part of the land? Not ordinarily, unless he decides to and does affix it to the land in much the same way as an ordinary garage or residence is annexed to the land. The question is, usually, what was the intent of A? Not his secret intent, but the intent as would be understood by a reasonable man acquainted with what A did. The question as to whether or not a chattel has become a fixture may come up in many different kinds of cases. It may come up between a vendor of land and the purchaser; if it is a fixture the purchaser is entitled to 349

it and the vendor may not remove it. The question may come up, in case of A's death, between A's heir and A's executor. If it is a fixture, A's heir is entitled to keep it on the land; if not, A's executor is entitled. The question may arise between a tenant and his landlord.

There are two main factors involved in answering the question. 

One is the character of the chattel and the other is the character of the annexation. Suppose the chattel has been so attached to the building that its removal would cause serious damage to the building; this is strong but not necessarily conclusive evidence that the chattel was meant to be a fixture. Thus, cold storage rooms put in by a tenant are likely to be held to be fixtures because of the difficulty of removal and the damage that would be caused by such removal. Suppose there are removable parts of a chattel which has been permanently affixed. In such a case, since the main part is affixed, the removable parts which are incidental thereto also become fixtures; for example, the removable rolls of an iron rolling mill.

Physical attachment to the land is not always necessary; for example, fences, whether of wood or stone, or statues which are held in place merely by gravity, are fixtures if so intended by the person who placed them on the land. 

8. Character of the Chattel

The character of the chattel is frequently of importance in determining whether or not it has become a fixture; for example, if it is so well adapted to the use which is being made of the land that it is reasonably necessary for the full enjoyment of the land, this tends strongly to show that it was meant to be a fixture. 

Common illustrations of this principle are: farm buildings erected for use on a farm but not securely attached to the soil; doors, sash and trim which have been made to order for a house but not yet attached thereto; a portable grist mill, put into a building for the use of grinding grain for use on the farm. 

What as to gas and electric light fixtures? Are they technically "fixtures?" Logically they should be, but they are not usually regarded as fixtures because they took the place of the old stoves and lamps which were clearly not fixtures. Suppose the tenant wishes to bring on the land something which would ordinarily be regarded as a fixture; may he bargain with the landlord for the privilege of removing it? Yes. 350

9. Removal 

If A is the owner in fee of land and is in possession, he may remove any fixture just as he may remove the soil or minerals beneath the surface. But if A is not the owner in fee in possession, he must bargain with such owner if he wishes to remove a fixture. 

Suppose that a chattel is brought on by a tenant for life, and it remains a chattel, and the tenant for life dies; how soon must the chattel be removed by his executor? Within a reasonable time after the tenant's death. But if the tenancy is for years instead of for life, then the tenant must remove it before his lease expires, unless the landlord expressly consents to his leaving it there. But if he should hold over without removing it, this does not mean that the landlord is entitled to the chattel but he may merely remove the chattel to a safe place off the premises or put it in a safe place for storage at the tenant's expense. 

10. Trees-Perennial and Annual Crops-Manure 

If a tree is growing on the line between A and B, the parties are tenants in common of the tree, and if either A or B cuts the tree the other may hold him liable for damages. (As to a tree whose trunk is entirely on A's land with branches overhanging B's land, see ante, Lesson 7).

Perennial crops, such as timothy or alfalfa or clover, are a part of the land and upon the death of the owner they do not go to the executor but to the heir or devisee. On the other hand, annual crops such as are produced each year by the occupant of the landfor example, wheat, rye, corn, apples, nursery trees and hopsare regarded as chattels and pass to the executor upon the owner's death. But where the owner sells the land, such crops pass to the purchaser unless the seller specifically reserves them. 

Whether manure is or is not to be regarded as a part of the land depends upon the circumstances of its production. If it is produced on the farm in the usual course it is regarded as a part of the land; the tenant may not remove it at the expiration of his lease; and if the owner of the land dies, the heir, and not the executor, is entitled. But if the manure is the result of feed brought onto the land it is regarded as a chattel going to the executor of the owner and is removable by a tenant. 

11. Emblements, Avulsions and Accretions

If A, occupying land as a tenant for life, plants a crop and dies before it is harvested, his executor is entitled to harvest 351

the crop. This right to emblements is given by the law, in order to encourage tenants whose estates are of uncertain duration to use the land beneficially; the right is, therefore, given to a tenant at will, and to a tenant from year to year, where the estate is terminated by the landlord.

A tenant for a fixed term is not entitled to emblements; but in some localities there is a custom, that where a tenant's term ends on March 1st he may come back and harvest a crop of winter wheat which he sowed the preceding fall. 

If the waters of the sea or of a lake or of a navigable river gradually recede, the owner of the adjoining land is entitled to the new land uncovered. Where soil is washed down gradually from 

A's land by a river, and the soil is slowly deposited on B's land below, it becomes part of B's land; but if the river takes a large part of A's land at one time and deposits it down stream, such land still belongs to A. 352

LESSON CASES 

(Par. 1) 

ALTZ v. LEIBERSON 

Court of Appeals of New York, 1922, 233 N.Y. 16

CARDOZO, J. The plaintiff in November, 1917, was a tenant in the defendant's apartment house in the city of New York. She was injured while in her room by a ceiling, which the defendant, after timely notice of the danger, had omitted to repair. So, at least, the jury found, and the unanimous affirmance at the Appellate Division carries with it the presumption that there is evidence to sustain the verdict. The question to be determined is whether the omission was a breach of duty. 

At common law there was no duty resting on the landlord of an apartment house to repair the rooms demised. * * * His duty of repair was limited to those parts of the building which the occupants enjoyed in common. The tenement House Law, has changed the measure of his burden. “A tenement house' is any house or building, or portion thereof, which is either rented, leased, let or hired out, to be occupied, or is occupied, in whole or in part, as the home or residence of three families or more living independently of each other, and doing their cooking upon the premises, and includes apartment houses, flat houses and all other houses so occupied." Tenement House 

Law, para. 2, subd. 1. “Every tenement house and all the parts thereof shall be kept in good repair."

The comprehensive sweep of this enactment admits of no exception. We are not at liberty to confine it to those parts of the building not included within the premises demised. The legislature has said the duty shall extend, not only to some parts, but to all. Apter words could hardly have been chosen wherewith to exclude division of responsibility between one part and another. 

The command of the statute, directed, as it plainly is, against the owner has thus changed the ancient rule. Whether "owner" may mean at times a lessee of the whole building is a question not before us. No doubt, before a right of action will accrue in favor of the tenant, there must be notice, actual or constructive, of the defect to be repaired.
